Positivity of generalized cluster scattering diagrams
Amanda Burcroff, Kyungyong Lee, Lang Mou

Abstract
. We introduce a new class of combinatorial objects, named tight gradings , which are certain nonnegative integer-valued functions on maximal Dyck paths. Using tight gradings, we derive a manifestly positive formula for any wall-function in a rank-2 generalized cluster scattering diagram. We further prove that any consistent rank-2 scattering diagram is positive with respect to the coefficients of initial wall-functions. Moreover, our formula yields explicit expressions for relative Gromov–Witten invariants on weighted projective planes and the Euler characteristics of moduli spaces of framed stable representations on complete bipartite quivers. Finally, by leveraging the rank-2 positivity, we show that any higher-rank generalized cluster scattering diagram has positive wall-functions, which leads to a proof of the positivity of the Laurent phenomenon and the strong positivity of Chekhov–Shapiro’s generalized cluster algebras.



1.
Introduction

In Fomin–Zelevinsky’s cluster algebras
[
18
]
, cluster variables are iteratively generated rational functions in
n
algebraically independent variables that are proven to remain Laurent polynomials, a property known as the
Laurent phenomenon

[
19
]
. It was conjectured originally in
[
18
]
that the non-trivial coefficients in these Laurent polynomials are positive integers. The positivity of the Laurent phenomenon turned out to be a deep property that has driven plentiful research employing wide range of techniques across combinatorics, representation theory and algebraic geometry including
[
42
,
45
,
41
,
12
]
. In this paper, we obtain new positivity results for the generalized cluster algebras of Chekhov and Shapiro
[
9
]
where polynomial type exchange relations (as opposed to binomials) are used to generate cluster variables. Our proofs are built upon a novel counting formula for wall-functions to rank-2 generalized cluster scattering diagrams and the subsequent positivity of wall-functions in higher ranks.

Lee and Schiffler
[
38
]
first proved the positivity for all skew-symmetric cluster algebras. Their proof is of an algebraic combinatorial nature and uses an explicit rank-2 formula of cluster variables in terms of combinatorics on Dyck paths
[
37
]
. This breakthrough led to Lee–Li–Zelevinsky’s construction of
greedy bases
of all rank-2 cluster algebras
[
36
]
and the subsequent generalization by Rupel
[
51
]
. Cluster monomials, which are monomials of cluster variables in one cluster, form only a proper subset of the greedy basis except for finite-type cases. So it is a significant feature of greedy bases that they give explicit combinatorial information even outside the discrete cluster dynamics.

In their seminal work
[
27
]
, Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Konstevich solved a number of conjectures fundamental to the structures of cluster algebras by bringing in novel techniques from log Calabi–Yau mirror symmetry
[
26
]
. Scattering diagrams
[
31
,
30
]
, also known as wall-crossing structures, play a crucial role in this study. In particular, the authors constructed
theta functions
through cluster scattering diagrams and showed that the theta functions include all cluster monomials. The positivity of the Laurent phenomenon follows from the positivity of wall-functions in cluster scattering diagrams.

A main theme of
[
27
]
is to address Fock–Goncharov’s cluster duality
[
16
,
17
]
, in which it was conjectured that the algebra of functions on a cluster variety
V
has a canonical basis containing global monomials parametrized by the tropical points
V^{\vee}(\mathbb{Z}^{T})
of the mirror cluster variety
V^{\vee}
. Although this conjecture is false in general
[
25
]
, theta functions do serve as a topological basis that exists in a certain formal limit. For
V
an
\mathcal{A}
-type cluster variety, global monomials are precisely cluster monomials and they are parametrized by the tropical points inside the cluster complex
\Delta^{+}
, which can be realized as a simplicial cone complex in the ambient real vector space of a cluster scattering diagram. The cluster complex
\Delta^{+}
is usually infinite but not complete. As noted in
[
27
]
, it is precisely the rich structure outside
\Delta^{+}
that scattering diagram technique brings into the the cluster algebra picture.

Our discoveries stem from an effort to bridge the explicit combinatorial terms developed in
[
37
,
38
,
36
,
51
]
and the more abstract constructions of
[
27
]
through scattering diagrams. Such a precedent is that greedy bases and theta bases are actually the same in rank 2
[
10
]
. However, the explicit scattering diagram structure outside
\Delta^{+}
is still elusive, even in rank 2. While one expects a non-trivial wall in every rational direction to appear in this region (
Appendix

B
), the wall-function in general is notoriously hard to compute. Our formula presented in
Theorem

1.2
computes any such wall-function in terms of combinatorics on Dyck paths. In fact, the formula itself is far more general than the ordinary cluster situation. As explained in
Section

1.1
, the formula essentially computes the commutator of two arbitrary generators of the tropical vertex group
[
31
,
29
]
. As rank-2 scattering diagrams are already linked with other interesting objects
[
29
,
49
]
, our formula invites immediate applications to Gromov–Witten invariants and quiver representations; see
Section

6
.

An immediate consequence of
Theorem

1.2
is that any wall-function has nonnegative integer coefficients. We extend this positivity to wall-functions in any rank-2 consistent scattering diagram (
Theorem

1.4
) and in any higher-rank generalized cluster scattering diagram (
Theorem

1.5
). The latter scattering diagrams encode structures of the associated generalized cluster algebras
[
40
,
11
]
. The positivity of wall-functions allows us to prove the positivity of the Laurent phenomenon for any generalized cluster algebra, previously conjectured by Chekhov and Shapiro
[
9
, Section 5]
. The
strong positivity
, which says that the structure constants in the multiplication of theta functions are nonnegative, also follows.

We now give more detailed description of the main results in
Section

1.1
through
1.3
.

1.1.
Formula for wall-functions

Let
\Bbbk
be a field of characteristic zero. Following
[
29
]
, we consider the automorphism group

A\coloneqq\operatorname{Aut}_{\Bbbk\llbracket t\rrbracket}(\Bbbk[x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1}]\llbracket t\rrbracket)

of the
1
-parameter formal family of the two-dimensional torus. For a non-zero vector
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and a function
f\in\Bbbk[x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1}]\llbracket t\rrbracket
of the form

f=1+tx^{a}y^{b}\cdot g(x^{a}y^{b},t),\quad g(z,t)\in\Bbbk[z]\llbracket t\rrbracket,

we define
T_{(a,b),f}\in A
by

T_{(a,b),f}(x)=f^{-b}\cdot x\quad\text{and}\quad T_{(a,b),f}(y)=f^{a}\cdot y.

Clearly
T_{(a,b),f}^{-1}=T_{(a,b),f^{-1}}\in A
.

The
tropical vertex group
is the completion with respect to the maximal ideal
(t)\subseteq\Bbbk\llbracket t\rrbracket
of the subgroup of
A
generated by all elements of the form
T_{(a,b),f}
. This group and its close variants arose in mirror symmetry
[
31
,
30
]
, and subsequently also play important roles in wall-crossing identities of invariants in triangulated categories
[
32
,
33
]
.

One initial task to understand the tropical vertex group is to compute the ordered factorization of the commutator of two generators. This turns out to be a rather hard question, but investigations into it have led to links to interesting objects including relative Gromov–Witten invariants
[
29
,
28
]
and moduli spaces of stable quiver representations
[
47
,
48
,
49
]
. Our approach to this problem is combinatorial and is largely motivated by Fomin–Zelevinsky’s cluster structure.

Let
P_{1}=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}p_{1,k}x^{k}
and
P_{2}=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}p_{2,k}y^{k}
be elements in
\Bbbk[x^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1}]\llbracket t\rrbracket
. Here
p_{i,k}\in\Bbbk\llbracket t\rrbracket
,
i=1,2
are regarded as coefficients. As observed by Kontsevich and Soibelman
[
31
]
, there exists a unique ordered factorization of the commutator

(1.1)

T_{(0,1),P_{2}}^{-1}\cdot T_{(1,0),P_{1}}\cdot T_{(0,1),P_{2}}\cdot T_{(1,0),P_{1}}^{-1}=\prod_{b/a\ \text{decreasing}}^{\rightarrow}T_{(a,b),f_{(a,b)}}.

The right-hand side of (
1.1
) is an infinite product ranging over all positive coprime pairs
(a,b)
. It is understood as the limit of finite products modulo each
(t^{n})
for
n\geq 1
.

Theorem 1.2

(Tight grading formula)
.

Fix coprime positive integers
(a,b)
. For each
k\geq 1
, choose integers
(d_{1,k},d_{2,k})
such that
ad_{2,k}-bd_{1,k}=\pm 1
,
ka\leq d_{1,k}
, and
kb\leq d_{2,k}
. Then

(1.3)

f_{(a,b)}=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{\omega}\mathrm{wt}(\omega)x^{ka}y^{kb},

where the second sum is over all tight gradings
\omega
on the Dyck path
\mathcal{P}(d_{1,k},d_{2,k})
of total horizontal weight
kb
and total vertical weight
ka
, and
\mathrm{wt}(\omega)\in\Bbbk\llbracket t\rrbracket
is the weight monomial in
\{p_{i,k}\mid i=1,2,k\geq 1\}
associated to
\omega
.

Tight gradings, the main combinatorial ingredients in
Theorem

1.2
, have simple descriptions in terms of combinatorics on the Dyck path
\mathcal{P}(d_{1,k},d_{2,k})
given in
Section

3.2
and
Section

5.1
. They form a subset of Rupel’s compatible gradings
[
51
]
, whose definition was inspired by the compatible pairs defined by Lee–Li–Zelevinsky in
[
36
]
. The formula (
1.3
) was announced in
[
7
, Theorem 3.5]
with a proof sketch. We give two different proofs in
Section

5.4
and
Section

5.5
.

Theorem

1.2
is a special case of
Theorem

5.17
, where we give a combinatorial formula of the
m
-th power of
f_{(a,b)}
for any
m\geq 1
using more general
shadowed gradings
. The more general formula will be used to compute Euler characteristics of quiver moduli spaces; see
Corollary

6.3
. It also yields explicit expressions of relative Gromov–Witten invariants on weighted projective planes; see
Theorem

6.8
.

We give in
Theorem

9.5
a formula for the commutator of two arbitrary generators
T_{(a_{1},b_{1}),f_{1}}
and
T_{(a_{2},b_{2}),f_{2}}
in the tropical vertex group. The difference with
Theorem

1.2
is that the vectors
(a_{1},b_{1})
and
(a_{2},b_{2})
may only generate a proper rank-2 sub-lattice of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. One may compare this formula with Gross–Pandharipande–Siebert’s
full commutator formula

[
29
, Section 5]
, which involves relative Gromov–Witten invariants defined through orbifold blowups of toric surfaces and a process factorizing
f_{1},f_{2}
into products of binomials. Our formula is direct in terms of the coefficients of
f_{1},f_{2}
and by counting combinatorial objects. It is thus much simpler from the perspective of computation and can be efficiently implemented through computer programs.

1.2.
Positivity of scattering diagrams

In the main body of the paper, the factorization (
1.1
) is packed in a structure called a rank-2
consistent scattering diagram
determined by two initial
lines
. More generally such a structure
\mathfrak{D}=\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})
can start with a countable collection
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
of lines in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and result in adding a collection
\mathfrak{D}\cut\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
of
rays
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to achieve consistency. Lines and rays are uniformly called
walls
and are attached with
wall-functions
. Each
f_{(a,b)}
in (
1.1
) is the wall-function associated to the ray
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)
while
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are associated to the coordinate axes. We will soon see this description in
Section

2.2
.

The starting point of the remaining discussion is the positivity demonstrated by the tight grading formula (
1.3
). The combined coefficient for each term
x^{ka}y^{kb}
is a polynomial with nonnegative integer coefficients, that is, an element in

\mathbb{N}[p_{i,k}\mid i=1,2,k\geq 1],

where
\mathbb{N}
denotes
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
throughout. It turns out that this positivity is shared by any consistent scattering diagram in rank 2.

Theorem 1.4

(
Theorem

9.9
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
be an initial scattering diagram consisting of lines. Any coefficient of any wall-function in
\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})
can be expressed as a polynomial in the coefficients of wall-functions in
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
with nonnegative integer coefficients.

We next utilize
Theorem

1.4
to show the positivity of wall-functions of certain scattering diagrams living in higher dimensions. There will be immediate implications on several positivity phenomena of generalized cluster algebras.

Briefly, a scattering diagram in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
is a (countable) collection of codimension one rational polyhedral cones, each with an attached power series, referred to as
walls
and their
wall-functions
. The (ordinary) cluster scattering diagram of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich
[
27
]
is determined by a finite collection of initial walls through a higher dimensional consistency condition that generalizes the equation (
1.1
). These scattering diagrams encode the information of Fomin–Zelevinsky’s cluster algebras.

In more concrete terms, a
generalized cluster scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
is the unique consistent completion of an initial collection of walls

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s},\mathrm{in}}=\{(H_{i},f_{i})\mid i=1,\dots,n\}

such that
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}\cut\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s},\mathrm{in}}
consists only of non-incoming walls, a notion in higher dimensions generalizing rays in rank 2. Each
H_{i}
is the coordinate hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
(for
n\leq N
) with vanishing
i
-th coordinate and

f_{i}=1+p_{i,1}x^{w_{i}}+\dots+p_{i,\ell_{i}}x^{\ell_{i}w_{i}},

where each
w_{i}
is a (column) vector in
\mathbb{Z}^{N}
,
\ell_{i}
is a positive integer, and
p_{i,k}
are formal variables. The matrix
(\ell_{1}w_{1},\dots,\ell_{n}w_{n})
is assumed to have full rank. Its top
n\times n
submatrix needs to be skew-symmetrizable, as required for exchange matrices that govern mutations in cluster algebras. These scattering diagrams, constructed and studied in
[
40
,
11
]
, generalize the cluster scattering diagrams of
[
27
]
, where each
P_{i}
is a binomial
1+x^{\ell_{i}w_{i}}
. They encode the information of Chekhov–Shapiro’s generalized cluster algebras
[
9
]
.

A significant property of ordinary cluster scattering diagrams is the positivity of wall-functions
[
27
, Theorem 1.24]
, which says that any wall-function is a (possibly infinite) product of binomials of the form
(1+x^{w})
. Thus, as a power series, every wall-function has nonnegative integer coefficients. The proof in
loc. cit
heavily relies on the assumption that the initial wall-functions are binomials. Our next main result shows the positivity of wall-functions in the polynomial case.

Theorem 1.5

(
Theorem

8.11
)
.

Any wall-function in a generalized cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
has coefficients in
\mathbb{N}[p_{i,s}\mid 1\leq i\leq n,1\leq s\leq\ell_{i}]
.

The proof of
Theorem

1.5
relies on
Theorem

1.4
, the rank-2 positivity in full generality. In
Section

9.3
, we give an order-by-order construction of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
analogous to the algorithm in
[
27
, Appendix C]
, where local scatterings are essentially governed by rank-2 behaviors. Then the proof undergoes a careful induction that ensures positivity at each order.

Remark 1.6
.

It would be interesting to explore quiver representation-theoretic interpretations of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
beyond the rank-2 case (
Section

6.1
). Following Bridgeland
[
4
]
, stability scattering diagrams equivalent to a particular class of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
are constructed in
[
35
]
through representations of Jacobian algebras whose quivers have loops.
Theorem

1.5
then implies that the Euler characteristic type wall-function coefficients in
[
35
]
are all nonnegative integers.

1.3.
Positivity of generalized cluster algebras

The remaining structural properties regarding the positivity of generalized cluster algebras follow from
Theorem

1.5
, summarized below. This is a series of results extended from the ordinary case
[
27
]
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
(resp.
\mathcal{U}
) denote the generalized cluster algebra (resp. upper cluster algebra) associated to a generalized cluster structure (with coefficients and frozen directions) of Chekhov–Shapiro as defined in
Section

7
.

(1)

Every cluster monomial in
\mathcal{A}
is a Laurent polynomial in the cluster variables of any cluster with coefficients in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}]
where
\mathbb{P}
is the coefficient group.
(
Theorem

7.9
)

(2)

When the cluster structure is generated by a seed data
\mathbf{s}

(
Section

8.1
)
that determines
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
, we have the so-called theta functions
\{\vartheta_{m}\mid m\in\mathbb{Z}^{N}\}
constructed from
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
in certain formal completion
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}
(dependent on
\mathbf{s}
) of
\mathcal{U}
. Each
\vartheta_{m}
is a formal Laurent series with coefficients in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}]
. The set of theta functions contain all cluster monomials.
(
Theorem

8.26
)

(3)

Theta functions form a topological basis of
\,\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}
and satisfy the formal multiplicative rule

\vartheta_{p_{1}}\cdot\vartheta_{p_{2}}=\sum_{q\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}}\alpha(p_{1},p_{2};q)\vartheta_{q}\in\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}},

where each structure constant
\alpha(p_{1},p_{2};q)
is defined through broken lines and is in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}]
.
(
Theorem

8.30
)

(4)

The
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
-module

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]}\{\vartheta_{m}\mid m\in\mathbb{Z}^{N},\vartheta_{m}\in\mathcal{U}\}

is closed under multiplication, hence a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
-algebra. There are natural inclusions
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.
(
Theorem

8.34
)

(5)

Theta functions are characterized as the atomic elements in
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}
with respect to the scattering atlas.
(
Theorem

8.37
)

Theorem

1.7
(1) confirms the
positivity of the Laurent phenomenon
for generalized cluster algebras, previously conjectured by Chekhov and Shapiro
[
9
]
. It is proven in
Section

8.3
through
Theorem

1.7
(2). We refer to
Theorem

1.7
(3) as the
strong positivity
. There is an extensive discussion in
[
27
]
on when any of the inclusions
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
in the ordinary case becomes equality, which we expect to naturally carry over to the generalized case but do not discuss in this paper.
Theorem

1.7
(5) is suggested to the authors by Ben Davison. The proof follows Mandel’s
[
39
]
in the ordinary case.

1.4.
Structure of the paper

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
, we review rank-2 scattering diagrams and important constructions such as broken lines and theta functions. Generalized cluster scattering diagrams in rank 2 are studied in detail in
Section

2.2
, whose cluster complex structure leads to the definition of rank-2 generalized cluster algebras in
Section

2.3
.

In
Section

3.1
, we define greedy elements for rank-2 generalized cluster algebras through a positive recurrence of coefficients. Compatible gradings are defined in
Section

3.2
and are used to express greedy elements in
Section

3.3
.

We establish the equality between greedy elements and theta functions in
Section

4
, generalizing
[
10
]
. This serves as a main ingredient in our first proof of
Theorem

5.17
given in
Section

5.4
.

In
Section

5
, we introduce the main combinatorial objects
shadowed gradings
and
tight gradings
and prove
Theorem

5.17
, which is a more general formula covering
Theorem

1.2
. Definitions and examples of shadowed and tight gradings are given in
Section

5.1
. Another important ingredient towards
Theorem

5.17
is the
one-bending property
regarding combinatorics of broken lines, introduced in
Section

5.2
. Two different proofs for
Theorem

5.17
are given respectively in
Sections

5.4
and
5.5
.

In
Section

6
, we apply
Theorem

5.17
to obtain combinatorial formulas for Euler characteristics of quiver moduli spaces and relative Gromov–Witten invariants.

Sections

7
and
8
deal with positivity properties of generalized cluster algebras. We review Chekhov–Shapiro’s generalized cluster structure in
Section

7
and state the positivity of Laurent phenomenon in
Theorem

7.9
, whose proof is given in
Section

8.3
. Generalized cluster scattering diagrams are defined in
Section

8.1
. The cluster complex structure is described in
Section

8.2
. Other structural results regarding generalized cluster algebras such as the strong positivity and atomicity of theta functions are discussed in
Sections

8.4
and
8.5
.

In
Section

9
, we fill in rather technical results on positivity of scattering diagrams. We establish the full positivity in rank 2 in
Sections

9.1
and
9.2
. The proof of
Theorem

8.11
, the positivity of higher-rank generalized cluster scattering diagrams, is completed in
Section

9.3
.

Appendix

A
contains the proof of the compatible grading formula for greedy elements in generalized cluster algebras. In
Appendix

B
, we give a combinatorial proof that the Badlands wall-function coefficients are non-vanishing in rank-
2
generalized cluster scattering diagrams whose initial wall-functions have positive coefficients.

2.
Rank 2 scattering diagrams and theta functions

In this section, we review rank-2 scattering diagrams, broken lines and theta functions. Generalized cluster scattering diagrams in rank 2 are studied in detail in
Section

2.2
, whose cluster complex structure leads to the definition of rank-2 generalized cluster algebras in
Section

2.3
.

2.1.
Scattering diagrams in rank 2

We introduce basic notions of rank-2 scattering diagrams following
[
24
, Section 6.3]
. Fix a rank-
2
lattice
M\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and let
N=\operatorname{Hom}(M,\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\mathscr{P}
be a finitely generated monoid equipped with a map

r\colon\mathscr{P}\rightarrow M.

Denote by
\mathscr{P}^{\times}
the group of units in
\mathscr{P}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}
denote both the monoid ideal
\mathscr{P}\cut\mathscr{P}^{\times}
in
\mathscr{P}
and the monomial ideal
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}\cut\mathscr{P}^{\times}]
in the monoid algebra
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]
over a ground field
\Bbbk
of characteristic zero. Let

\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}\coloneqq\lim_{\longleftarrow}\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k}

be the
\mathfrak{m}
-adic completion of
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]
.

We say a monomial ideal
I
in
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]
is
\mathfrak{m}
-primary
if its radical
\sqrt{I}
is
\mathfrak{m}
.

Definition 2.1
.

Fix
r\colon\mathscr{P}\rightarrow M
and let
I
be an
\mathfrak{m}
-primary ideal.
A
ray
or
line
, uniformly referred to as a
wall
, over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
or
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
is the data
\mathfrak{d}
of

•

the
support

S_{\mathfrak{d}}\subseteq M_{\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq M\otimes\mathbb{R}
given by

S_{\mathfrak{d}}=b-\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}\quad\text{or}\quad S_{\mathfrak{d}}=b-\mathbb{R}m_{\mathfrak{d}}

for some
b\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
and the
direction

-m_{\mathfrak{d}}\in M\cut\{0\}
;

•

the
wall-function

f_{\mathfrak{d}}
is an element in
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
or in
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
such that

f_{\mathfrak{d}}=1+\sum_{m\in\mathscr{P}({m_{\mathfrak{d}}})}c_{m}x^{m}

where
\mathscr{P}({m_{\mathfrak{d}}})\coloneqq\{m\in\mathscr{P}\mid r(m)\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}\}
and
f_{\mathfrak{d}}\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}
.

Remark 2.2
.

A ray does not start at the origin when
b\neq 0
and a line does not cross the origin when
b\notin\mathbb{R}m_{\mathfrak{d}}
. A wall
\mathfrak{d}
is sometimes represented by the pair
(S_{\mathfrak{d}},f_{\mathfrak{d}})
, but two walls can have the same pair of support and wall-function.

Definition 2.3
.

A
scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
is a finite set of walls over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
. A
scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
is a countable set of walls over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
such that for each
k\geq 0
, the subset

\mathfrak{D}_{k}\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}\mid f_{\mathfrak{d}}\not\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\}

is a scattering diagram over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
.

Definition 2.4
.

The
support
of
\mathfrak{D}
is defined as

\operatorname{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})\coloneqq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}}S_{\mathfrak{d}}\subseteq M_{\mathbb{R}}.

The
singular locus

\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
of
\mathfrak{D}
is the union of the boundary point of every ray and the zero-dimensional intersection locus of any pair of walls.

Associated to a wall
\mathfrak{d}
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
or
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
and a vector
v\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
transversal to
S_{\mathfrak{d}}
is the
wall-crossing automorphism

\mathfrak{p}_{v,\mathfrak{d}}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I)
or
\operatorname{Aut}(\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]})
. It is an algebra automorphism defined by

(2.5)

\mathfrak{p}_{v,\mathfrak{d}}(x^{m})\coloneqq x^{m}f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{n\cdot r(m)}\quad\text{for}\quad m\in\mathscr{P},

where
n\in\operatorname{Hom}(M,\mathbb{Z})
is primitive and orthogonal to
S_{\mathfrak{d}}
such that
n\cdot v<0
, where
\cdot
denotes the natural pairing between
\operatorname{Hom}(M,\mathbb{Z})
and
M
. Clearly
\mathfrak{p}_{v,\mathfrak{d}}^{-1}=\mathfrak{p}_{-v,\mathfrak{d}}
.

A path (a smooth immersion)
\gamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
is called
regular
with respect to a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
if it is a smooth immersion such that

•

\gamma([0,1])\cap\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})=\varnothing
and
\{\gamma(0),\gamma(1)\}\cap\operatorname{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})=\varnothing
;

•

it crosses walls only transversally.

For a regular path
\gamma
, let

0<t_{1}\leq\cdots\leq t_{s}<1

be a sequence of maximal length such that
\gamma(t_{i})\in S_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}
for some wall
\mathfrak{d}_{i}\in\mathfrak{D}
and that if
t_{i}=t_{j}
, then
\mathfrak{d}_{i}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{j}
are two distinguished elements in
\mathfrak{D}
.

The
path-ordered product
of
\gamma
is defined to be

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}\coloneqq\mathfrak{p}_{\dot{\gamma}(t_{s}),\mathfrak{d}_{s}}\circ\cdots\circ\mathfrak{p}_{\dot{\gamma}(t_{1}),\mathfrak{d}_{1}}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I)\,,

where
\dot{\gamma}(t)
denotes the tangent vector
\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}
. The order of
\mathfrak{p}_{\dot{\gamma}(t_{i}),\mathfrak{d}_{i}}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{\dot{\gamma}(t_{j}),\mathfrak{d}_{j}}
in the product when
t_{i}=t_{j}
is irrelevant because they commute in
\operatorname{Aut}(\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I)
.

A path
\gamma
is
regular
with respect to a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
if it is regular with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
for each
k\geq 0
. The
path-ordered product
of
\gamma
is defined to be

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}\coloneqq\lim_{\longleftarrow}\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{k}}\in\operatorname{Aut}(\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]})\,,

where each
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}_{k}}
is considered to be in
\operatorname{Aut}(\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1})
.

Definition 2.6
.

Two scattering diagrams
\mathfrak{D}
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
(both over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
or
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
) are said to be
equivalent
and denoted as
\mathfrak{D}\equiv\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
if

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}}

for any path
\gamma
that is regular to both.

In view of the above equivalence, it is convenient to consider a “wall” with support
S_{\mathfrak{d}}=b+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}}
and wall-function
f_{\mathfrak{d}}
. It does not qualify either as a line or a ray in
Definition

2.1
, but is equivalent to the union of a line and a ray

(2.7)

\{(b+\mathbb{R}m_{\mathfrak{d}},f_{\mathfrak{d}})\}\cup\{(b+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m_{\mathfrak{d}},f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{-1}).

Definition 2.8
.

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is called
consistent
if the path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
is the identity for any regular path
\gamma
that is a closed loop, or, equivalently,
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
only depends on the endpoints
\gamma(0)
and
\gamma(1)
.

Remark 2.9
.

It is straightforward to see that
\mathfrak{D}
is consistent if and only if each
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
is consistent. By definition,
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
is empty. If
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
only consists of lines, then it is consistent because wall-crossing automorphisms in
\operatorname{Aut}(\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{2})
commute.

The following theorem is due to Kontsevich and Soibelman
[
31
]
. The version adopted below can be found in
[
24
, Theorem 6.38]
where an order-by-order algorithmic construction is given; see also
[
29
, Theorem 1.4]
.

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram (over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
or
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
). There always exists a consistent scattering diagram
\Scat(\mathfrak{D})
unique up to equivalence such that
\Scat(\mathfrak{D})\cut\mathfrak{D}
consists only of rays.

The above theorem implies that
\Scat(\mathfrak{D})
only depends on the lines in
\mathfrak{D}
because for any ray in
\mathfrak{D}
one can always add another ray with the exact same support but with the inverse wall-function to cancel out their contribution to any path-ordered product.

Theorem

2.10
is functorial in the following sense.

In the complete case, suppose that we have
\varphi\colon\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{1}]}\rightarrow\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{2}]}
such that
\varphi(\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{1}(m)]})\subseteq\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{2}(m)]}
for any
m\in M\cut\{0\}
.
For
\mathfrak{D}
a scattering diagram over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{1}]}
, we have

(2.11)

\Scat(\varphi(\mathfrak{D}))\equiv\varphi(\Scat(\mathfrak{D})),

as scattering diagrams over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{2}]}
by
Theorem

2.10
, where the operation
\varphi
keeps the support of a wall and applies the map
\varphi
to the attached wall-function.

2.2.
Generalized cluster scattering diagrams

In this subsection, we study a particular class of scattering diagrams that are relevant to generalized cluster algebras in rank 2. They are fundamental to our study of positivity in more general cases.

Let
M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with the standard unit vectors
e_{1}=(1,0)
and
e_{2}=(0,1)
. Let
\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
be positive integers. Choose the monoid

\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}=M\oplus\mathbb{M}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}

with
r\colon\mathscr{P}\rightarrow M
the projection to
M
, where
\mathbb{M}_{i}
is the (multiplicative) monoid of monomials in variables
\{p_{i,k}\mid 0\leq k\leq\ell_{i}\}
. We set conventionally
p_{i,0}=1
, thus
\mathbb{M}_{i}\cong\mathbb{N}^{\ell_{i}}
. The monoid
\mathbb{M}_{i}
embeds in
\mathbb{P}_{i}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\ell_{i}}
the group of Laurent monomials. Denote

\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{P}_{2}.

To express monomials in
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]
, let
x
and
y
denote respectively the monomials associated to
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
in
M
.

Definition 2.12
.

The
generalized cluster scattering diagram
associated to the pair
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is the consistent scattering diagram
\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})
over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
(up to equivalence) where

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}\coloneqq\{(\mathbb{R}e_{1},P_{1}),(\mathbb{R}e_{2},P_{2})\},

(2.13)

P_{1}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell_{1}}p_{1,k}x^{k}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{2}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell_{2}}p_{2,k}y^{k}.

This scattering diagram (and its close variants) has been considered many times in different contexts, for example in
[
29
,
28
,
49
,
40
,
11
]
. Since it is determined by the initial wall-functions
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, we denote it by
\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
. This notation will be further extended to
\Scat(f_{i}\mid i\in I)
for a collection of wall-functions
\{f_{i}\mid i\in I\}
, which represent the lines (through the origin) they determine. This specializes to the
ordinary cluster scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})}
(cf.
[
27
]
) when
P_{1}=1+x^{\ell_{1}}
and
P_{2}=1+y^{\ell_{2}}
.

Remark 2.14
.

Note that we are using the
d
-vector version of the scattering diagram rather than the
g
-vector version, see
[
10
]
for a comparison.

Following the Kontsevich–Soibelman algorithm explained in the proof of
[
24
, Theorem 6.38]
, one can choose
\mathfrak{D}=\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
(in its equivalent class) so that any added ray in
\mathfrak{D}\cut\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
has support in the strict third quadrant and is pointed at the origin, i.e., of the form
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)
for
(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
. Further, rays with identical support can be combined into one ray by multiplying their wall-functions together. Therefore, we can assume that
\mathfrak{D}
has distinct rays with non-trivial wall-functions
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}
. It is convenient to assign degrees
\deg p_{1,k}=\deg p_{2,k}=k
, and thus a multi-degree in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
to any (Laurent) monomials in
\mathbb{P}
so that for a monomial term
px^{ka}y^{kb}
in
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}
, the multi-degree of
p
is
(ka,kb)
by homogeneity constraints.

A first step towards understanding the structure of
\mathfrak{D}
is the following
mutation
operation. Take a representative of
\mathfrak{D}
as

\{(\mathbb{R}e_{1},P_{1})\}\cup\{(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e_{2},P_{2})\}\cup\{(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b),f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)})\mid a\geq 0,b>0\},

where
(a,b)
is over all primitive vectors such that
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}
is non-trivial. Namely we break the wall
(\mathbb{R}e_{2},P_{2})
into two halves. The positive half is a wall in the sense of (
2.7
) and the negative half is included in the third subset of the above union.

Define the piecewise linear transformation
T\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
by for
(a_{1},a_{2})\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
,

(2.15)

T(m)\coloneqq\begin{cases}(a_{1},a_{2})\quad&a_{2}\geq 0,\\
(a_{1}-\ell_{1}a_{2},a_{2})\quad&a_{2}\leq 0.\end{cases}

Define the monoid
\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\coloneqq M\oplus{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}
with the projection
r^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{P}^{\prime}\rightarrow M
, where
{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}_{1}
is the monoid of monomials in
\{p_{1,k}/p_{1,\ell_{1}}\mid 0\leq k\leq\ell_{1}\}
. Let
P^{\prime}_{1}=P_{1}/(p_{1,\ell_{1}}x^{\ell_{1}})\in\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}^{\prime}]}
.

Define the collection

T(\mathfrak{D})\coloneqq\{(\mathbb{R}(-e_{1}),P^{\prime}_{1})\}\cup\{(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e_{2},P_{2})\}\cup\{(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}T((a,b)),T(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}))\mid a\geq 0,b>0\},

where
T(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)})(x,y)=f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}\left(x,y/(p_{1,\ell_{1}}x^{\ell_{1}})\right)
.

Theorem 2.16
.

The set
T(\mathfrak{D})
is a scattering diagram over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}^{\prime}]}
. Furthermore it is equivalent to
\Scat(P^{\prime}_{1},P_{2})
as scattering diagrams over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}^{\prime}]}
.

Proof.

In the context of cluster scattering diagrams of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich, this result is well-known as the
mutation invariance

[
27
, Theorem 1.24]
. In principle one obtains a similar proof which involves generalizing the very hard Theorem 1.28 in
loc. cit
. Here we are in rank 2 so a simpler argument is available although more complex initial wall-functions are allowed.

It suffices to show that (1)
T(\mathfrak{D})
is indeed a scattering diagram over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}^{\prime}]}
and then (2) it is consistent and contains the same collection of lines as
\Scat(P^{\prime}_{1},P_{2})
. To show (1), we check that each pair

\left(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}T((a,b)),T(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)})\right)

qualifies as a wall over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}^{\prime}]}
. Let
px^{ka}y^{kb}
be a monomial in a non-trivial
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}
. It gets transformed by
T
into

pp_{1,\ell_{1}}^{-kb}x^{ka-\ell_{1}kb}y^{kb},

which can be induced by the map

(2.17)

T\colon M\oplus\mathbb{P}\rightarrow M\oplus\mathbb{P},\quad(m,p_{1},p_{2})\mapsto(T(m),p_{1}p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{-\deg p_{2}},p_{2}).

It is enough to show that the first entry of the multi-degree of
pp_{1,\ell_{1}}^{-kb}
is always non-positive. This is equivalent to

ka-\ell_{1}kb\leq 0\iff a/b\leq\ell_{1},

which follows directly from
[
28
, Corollary 6]
. This also implies that every ray of
T(\mathfrak{D})
lies in the fourth quadrant. Notice that as in (
2.7
) the wall
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}e_{2},P_{2})
in
T(\mathfrak{D})
is equivalent to

\{(\mathbb{R}e_{2},P_{2})\}\cup\{(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}e_{2},P_{2}^{-1})\}.

Hence
T(\mathfrak{D})
and
\Scat(P^{\prime}_{1},P_{2})
can be regarded to have the same set of lines.

It only remains to show the consistency, that is,
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,T(\mathfrak{D})}=\operatorname{id}
for a simple loop
\gamma
around the origin. This can be checked analogously as in
Step II
of the proof of
[
27
, Theorem 1.24]
, which essentially follows from the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}
.
∎

One immediate consequence of
Theorem

2.16
is

f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(\ell_{1},1)}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell_{2}}p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{k}p_{2,k}x^{\ell_{1}k}y^{k},

which follows from
T(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(\ell_{1},1)})=P_{2}
.

We also know from the proof of
Theorem

2.16
that
\mathfrak{D}
has no ray in the interior of

\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\{(-1,0),(-\ell_{1},-1)\}.

Such a region will be called a “chamber” and many more can be obtained by applying iterative mutations of scattering diagrams as follows.

Let
{\mathbb{M}}^{\prime}_{2}
be the monoid of monomials in
\{p_{2,k}/p_{2,\ell_{2}}\mid 0\leq k\leq\ell_{2}\}
. Let
P^{\prime}_{2}\coloneqq P_{2}/(p_{2,\ell_{2}}y^{\ell_{2}})
. Denote for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\mathfrak{D}^{(i)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})\\
\Scat(P^{\prime}_{1},P_{2})\\
\Scat(P^{\prime}_{1},P^{\prime}_{2})\\
\Scat(P_{1},P^{\prime}_{2})\end{cases}\ \text{over}\quad\mathscr{P}^{(i)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}M\oplus\mathbb{M}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}\\
M\oplus\mathbb{M}_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}\\
M\oplus\mathbb{M}_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\prime}\\
M\oplus\mathbb{M}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}^{\prime}\end{cases}\ \text{for}\quad i=\begin{cases}0\mod 4\\
1\mod 4\\
2\mod 4\\
3\mod 4.\end{cases}

This periodic sequence of scattering diagrams can be seen as obtained by iteratively applying mutations in
Theorem

2.16
. We denote by
T_{i}
the mutation operation turning
\mathfrak{D}^{(i)}
into
\mathfrak{D}^{(i+1)}
analogous to the mutation
T
defined earlier from
\mathfrak{D}^{(0)}
to
\mathfrak{D}^{(1)}
. It involves a piecewise linear map
T_{i}\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
as in (
2.15
) and a group homomorphism
T_{i}\colon M\oplus\mathbb{P}\rightarrow M\oplus\mathbb{P}
as in (
2.17
). The composition
T_{i+3}T_{i+2}T_{i+1}T_{i}
does not act on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
by the identity map. Hence it sends a wall
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}^{(i)}
to another one with different support, giving non-trivial implication on the structure of
\mathfrak{D}^{(i)}
.

We use the above idea to generate a
\mathbb{Z}
-sequence of “chambers” in
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}^{(0)}
. A connected component in the complement of the closure of a scattering diagram’s support is called a
chamber
. Each
\mathfrak{D}^{(i)}
has a
positive chamber

\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{C}^{(i)})
, the interior of the cone
\mathcal{C}^{(i)}
non-negatively spanned by the
incoming
halves of the two initial walls, that is, the
(i+1)
-th (modulo
4
) quadrant in
M_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Define piecewise linear maps on
M_{\mathbb{R}}
for
i\geq 1

T^{(i)}\coloneqq T_{i-1}\cdots T_{1}T_{0},\quad T^{(-i)}=T_{-i}^{-1}\cdots T_{-1}^{-1},\quad\text{and}\quad T^{(0)}=\mathrm{id}.

Define for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
cones

\mathcal{C}_{i}\coloneqq\left(T^{(i)}\right)^{-1}\left(\mathcal{C}^{(i)}\right)\subseteq M_{\mathbb{R}}.

Namely, we pull back
\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{C}^{(i)})
via the piecewise linear transform
T^{(i)}
to obtain a chamber
\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
in
\mathfrak{D}
. Every
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\mathcal{C}_{i+1}
intersect at their common boundary, which is either the support of a ray or a half line in
\mathfrak{D}
. The associated wall-function can also be obtained by pulling back from the initial wall-functions in
\mathfrak{D}^{(i)}
. The cones
\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
form a discrete part of
\mathfrak{D}
, which is referred to as the
cluster complex
of
\mathfrak{D}
where
\mathcal{C}_{i}
is called a
cluster cone
while its interior is called a
cluster chamber
. The map
T^{(4)}\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
acts on the cluster complex such that
T^{(4)}(\mathcal{C}_{i+4})=\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

When
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}<4
, there are only finitely many
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and their union is
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus there are finitely many rays in
\mathfrak{D}\cut\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
. We depict in
Figure

1
the case
\ell_{1}=3
and
\ell_{2}=1
. All the remaining cases can be obtained by specializing certain
p_{i,j}
to zero (by the functoriality) and possibly switching
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
. In
Figure

1
, the added rays are

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(3,1),\ 1+p_{1,3}p_{2,1}x^{3}y\right),

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(2,1),\ 1+p_{1,2}p_{2,1}x^{2}y+p_{1,1}p_{1,3}p_{2,1}^{2}x^{4}y^{2}+p_{1,3}^{2}p_{2,1}^{3}x^{6}y^{3}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(3,2),\ 1+p_{1,3}p_{2,1}^{2}x^{3}y^{2}\right),

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(1,1),\ 1+p_{1,1}p_{2,1}xy+p_{1,2}p_{2,1}^{2}x^{2}y^{2}+p_{1,3}p_{2,1}^{3}x^{3}y^{3}\right).

Figure 1.
\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
, where
P_{1}=1+p_{1,1}x+p_{1,2}x^{2}+p_{1,3}x^{3}
and
P_{2}=1+p_{2,1}y
.

When
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\geq 4
, the structure of the cluster complex is summarized in
Theorem

2.21
, for which we need some preparation. Define vectors
{\mathbf{d}}_{n}=(d_{1;n},d_{2;n})\in M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by setting initially

{\mathbf{d}}_{0}=e_{2},\quad{\mathbf{d}}_{1}=-e_{1},\quad{\mathbf{d}}_{2}=-e_{2},\quad{\mathbf{d}}_{3}=e_{1},

and for
n\neq 1,2

(2.18)

{\mathbf{d}}_{n+1}+{\mathbf{d}}_{n-1}=\begin{cases}\ell_{1}{\mathbf{d}}_{n}\quad&\text{when $n$ is odd},\\
\ell_{2}{\mathbf{d}}_{n}\quad&\text{when $n$ is even}.\end{cases}

The following properties of
d_{n}
are easily obtained by induction.

Lemma 2.19
.

We have
(1)

{\mathbf{d}}_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
for
n\neq 0,1,2,3
;
(2)

\{{\mathbf{d}}_{n},{\mathbf{d}}_{n+1}\}
is a basis of
M
;
(3)
for
n\neq 0,1,2,3
,

\begin{cases}d_{1;n}/\ell_{1},\ (d_{2;n}-1)/\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N},\quad&n\equiv 0\mod 4,\\
(d_{1;n}+1)/\ell_{1},\ d_{2;n}/\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N},\quad&n\equiv 1\mod 4,\\
d_{1;n}/\ell_{1},\ (d_{2;n}+1)/\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N},\quad&n\equiv 2\mod 4,\\
(d_{1;n}-1)/\ell_{1},\ d_{2;n}/\ell_{2}\in\mathbb{N},\quad&n\equiv 3\mod 4.\end{cases}

We define

(2.20)

\overline{P}_{1}(z):=\frac{1}{p_{1,\ell_{1}}}z^{\ell_{1}}P_{1}(z^{-1})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{P}_{2}(z):=\frac{1}{p_{2,\ell_{2}}}z^{\ell_{2}}P_{2}(z^{-1}).

Notice that they both have constant term
1
. For
(u,v)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, let
W((u,v))=p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{u/\ell_{1}}p_{2,\ell_{2}}^{v/\ell_{2}}
.

Theorem 2.21
.

Outside the closed cone “Badlands”

C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\coloneqq\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}}\left\{\left(2\ell_{1},\ell_{1}\ell_{2}+\sqrt{\ell_{1}^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-4\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}\right),\left(\ell_{1}\ell_{2}+\sqrt{\ell_{1}^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-4\ell_{1}\ell_{2}},2\ell_{2}\right)\right\},

the rays in
\mathfrak{D}\cut\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
are precisely

\left\{\left(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}d_{n},\zeta_{n}(x^{d_{1;n}}y^{d_{2;n}})\right)\,\middle|\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\cut\{0,1,2,3\}\right\}

where for
n\neq 1,2
,

\zeta_{n}(z)\coloneqq\begin{cases}P_{2}(W({\mathbf{d}}_{n}-e_{2})z)\quad&n\equiv 0\mod 4,\\
\overline{P}_{1}(W({\mathbf{d}}_{n}+e_{1})z)\quad&n\equiv 1\mod 4,\\
\overline{P}_{2}(W({\mathbf{d}}_{n}+e_{2})z)\quad&n\equiv 2\mod 4,\\
P_{1}(W({\mathbf{d}}_{n}-e_{1})z)\quad&n\equiv 3\mod 4.\end{cases}

Proof.

When
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are binomials, this structure is well-known from
[
27
, Example 1.15]
; see also
[
10
, Section 4]
. When
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are monic polynomials, this result can already be extracted from
[
28
, Section 5]
. In the current setting, the stated description of rays is obtained by working out the cones
\mathcal{C}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and the wall-functions on their boundary rays. Inductively we have

\mathcal{C}_{n}=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}}\{{\mathbf{d}}_{n+1},{\mathbf{d}}_{n+2}\}.

The attached wall-functions on
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}{\mathbf{d}}_{n}
are also obtained inductively from iterative mutations in
Theorem

2.16
. Notice that there is no ray in the interior of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
. The limits of
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}{\mathbf{d}}_{n}
when
n\rightarrow+\infty
and
n\rightarrow-\infty
are respectively the two boundaries of
C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
(which degenerates to a single ray when
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}=4
). Therefore we have exhausted all added rays outside
C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
.
∎

The next two examples are when
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}=4
. In these cases, the cluster complex is infinite but the cone
C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
is one-dimensional.

Example 2.22
.

Let
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=2
. We take the initial wall-functions

P_{1}=(1+s_{1}x)(1+s_{2}x)\quad\text{and}\quad P_{2}=(1+t_{1}y)(1+t_{2}y),

which are related to the polynomials in (
2.13
) by the substitution

p_{1,1}=s_{1}+s_{2},\quad p_{2,1}=t_{1}+t_{2},\quad p_{1,2}=s_{1}s_{2},\quad p_{2,2}=t_{1}t_{2}.

The closed cone
C_{2,2}
is a single ray
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(1,1)
. The associated wall-function is computed by Reineke and Weist
[
49
]
to be

\displaystyle f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(1,1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+s_{1}t_{1}xy)(1+s_{1}t_{2}xy)(1+s_{2}t_{1}xy)(1+s_{2}t_{2}xy)}{(1-s_{1}s_{2}t_{1}t_{2}x^{2}y^{2})^{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(p_{1,1}p_{2,2}xy^{2}+p_{2,1}y)(p_{1,2}p_{2,1}x^{2}y+p_{1,1}x)}{(1-p_{1,2}p_{2,2}x^{2}y^{2})^{4}}+\frac{1}{(1-p_{1,2}p_{2,2}x^{2}y^{2})^{2}}.

We see that in the expansion (where
x
and
y
are omitted), every coefficient is a polynomial of
p_{1,1},p_{1,2},p_{2,1},p_{2,2}
with nonnegative integer coefficients:

(2.23)

\sum_{k\geq 0}(k+1)p_{1,2}^{k}p_{2,2}^{k}+\left[\binom{k+3}{3}+\binom{k+2}{3}\right]p_{1,1}p_{2,1}p_{1,2}^{k}p_{2,2}^{k}\\
+\binom{k+3}{3}p_{2,1}^{2}p_{1,2}^{k+1}p_{2,2}^{k}+\binom{k+3}{3}p_{1,1}^{2}p_{1,2}^{k}p_{2,2}^{k+1}.

A combinatorial interpretation of each term is given in
Theorem

5.17
. The objects corresponding to
(k+1)p_{1,2}^{k}p_{2,2}^{k}
are classified in
Example

5.7
. These are precisely the terms that appear in the ordinary cluster case.

Example 2.24
.

Let
\ell_{2}=4
and
\ell_{1}=1
. We take the initial wall-functions

P_{1}=\prod_{i=1}^{4}(1+s_{i}x)=1+p_{1,1}x+p_{1,2}x^{2}+p_{1,3}x^{3}+p_{1,4}x^{4}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{2}=1+p_{2,1}y.

The closed cone
C_{4,1}
is a single ray
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(2,1)
. The wall-function is again computed in
[
49
]
to be

f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(2,1)}=\dfrac{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}(1+s_{i}s_{j}p_{2,1}x^{2}y)}{(1-p_{1,4}p_{2,1}^{2}x^{4}y^{2})^{4}}.

To simplify notations we let
x=y=p_{2,1}=1
as they can be recovered from homogeneity constraints. We can rewrite

\frac{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}(1+s_{i}s_{j})}{(1-p_{1,4})^{4}}=\frac{1+p_{1,2}+p_{1,4}}{(1-p_{1,4})^{2}}+\frac{(p_{1,1}p_{1,4}+p_{1,3})(p_{1,1}+p_{1,3})}{(1-p_{1,4})^{4}}.

The power series expansion of
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(2,1)}
then has nonnegative integer coefficients. Explicitly the expansion (with
x
and
y
omitted) is

(2.25)

\sum_{k\geq 0}(2k+1)p_{1,4}^{k}p_{2,1}^{2k}+(k+1)p_{1,2}p_{1,4}^{k}p_{2,1}^{2k+1}+\binom{k+3}{3}p_{1,1}^{2}p_{1,4}^{k+1}p_{2,1}^{2k+3}\\
+\left[\binom{k+3}{3}+\binom{k+2}{3}\right]p_{1,1}p_{1,3}p_{1,4}^{k}p_{2,1}^{2k+2}+\binom{k+3}{3}p_{1,3}^{2}p_{1,4}^{k}p_{2,1}^{2k+3}.

A combinatorial interpretation of each term is given in
Theorem

5.17
. The objects corresponding to
(2k+1)p_{1,4}^{k}p_{2,1}^{2k}
are classified in

Example

5.8
, which are precisely the terms that appear in the ordinary cluster case.

When
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}>4
, the structure of
\mathfrak{D}
within
C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
is rather elusive. One aim of this paper is to understand the rays and their wall-functions within
C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
.
Theorem

5.17
in particular provides a combinatorial formula for the wall-function of every such ray. We further discuss in
Appendix

B
that every ray within
C_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
is non-trivial and give a lower bound for wall-function coefficients.

2.3.
Broken lines and theta functions

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
as in
Section

2.1
.

Definition 2.26
.

Let
m_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
and
Q\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\cut\Supp(\mathfrak{D})
. A
broken line

\beta
for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
is a piecewise linear continuous map

\beta\colon(-\infty,0]\to M_{\mathbb{R}}\cut\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})

with values
-\infty=\tau_{0}<\tau_{1}<\cdots<\tau_{\ell+1}=0
and an associated monomial
c_{i}x^{m_{i}}
(with
c_{i}\in\Bbbk
and
m_{i}\in\mathscr{P}
) for each domain of linearity
(\tau_{i},\tau_{i+1})
,
i=0,\dots,\ell
such that

(1)

\beta(0)=Q
and
c_{0}=1
;

(2)

\dot{\beta}(\tau)=-r(m_{i})
for any
\tau\in(\tau_{i},\tau_{i+1})
for each
i=0,\dots,\ell
;

(3)

\beta
transversally crosses (the support of) some wall at
\tau_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,\ell
, that is, neither
-r(m_{i-1})
nor
-r(m_{i})
is tangent to the wall.

(4)

for
i=0,\dots,\ell-1
,
c_{i+1}x^{m_{i+1}}
is a monomial term (other than
c_{i}x^{m_{i}}
) of the power series

c_{i}x^{m_{i}}\prod_{\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}\mid\beta(\tau_{i})\in S_{\mathfrak{d}}\}}f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{n_{i}\cdot r(m_{i})},

where
n_{i}
is primitive in
N
and orthogonal to any
\mathfrak{d}
with
\beta(\tau_{i})\in S_{\mathfrak{d}}
such that
n_{i}\cdot r(m_{i})>0
.

When
r(m_{0})=0
, there is only the “static” broken line at
Q
for
m_{0}
and any endpoint
Q
.

Definition 2.27
.

The
theta function
associated to
m_{0}
and
Q
is

\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}\coloneqq\sum_{\beta}\operatorname{Mono}(\beta)

where the (formal) sum is over all broken lines for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
and
\operatorname{Mono}(\beta)=c_{\ell}x^{m_{\ell}}
is the last monomial of
\beta
.

Remark 2.28
.

The theta function
\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}
can be viewed as an element in
x^{m_{0}}\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
. In fact, broken lines and theta functions can be defined for
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
. Then
\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}
is the limit of theta functions in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
where each one is necessarily a finite sum in
x^{m_{0}}\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
. More detail is given in the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 3.4]
.

Theorem 2.29

(
[
8
]
,
[
27
, Theorem 3.5]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a consistent scattering diagram, and let
m_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
and
Q,Q^{\prime}\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\cut\operatorname{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
be two points. Suppose further that the coordinates of
Q
are linearly independent over
\mathbb{Q}
, and the same is true for
Q^{\prime}
. Then for any regular path
\gamma
with endpoints
\gamma(0)=Q
and
\gamma(1)=Q^{\prime}
, we have

\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},m_{0}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}).

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be
\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
as in
Section

2.2
. Define the
weight
of a broken line
\beta
to be

(2.30)

c(\beta)\coloneqq c_{\ell}x^{m_{\ell}}/x^{r(m_{\ell})},

a monomial in
\Bbbk[\mathbb{M}]
. Let
m(\beta)\coloneqq r(m_{\ell})
. Thus
c(\beta)x^{m(\beta)}=\operatorname{Mono}(\beta)
.

Corollary 2.31
.

Let
m_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
. For any
m_{\mathrm{fin}}\in M
, the sum

\sum_{m(\beta)=m_{\mathrm{fin}}}c(\beta)x^{m(\beta)}

over all broken lines
\beta
for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
such that
m(\beta)=m_{\mathrm{fin}}
is independent of
Q
chosen generally in the same
\mathcal{C}_{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. If
m_{\mathrm{fin}}
is tangent to the ray
\mathcal{C}_{n}\cap\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
, then the sum is independent of
Q
chosen generally in
\mathcal{C}_{n}\cup\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
.

Proof.

The first half is a direct corollary of
Theorem

2.29
. To see the second half, we choose general
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
,
Q^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
, and a regular path
\gamma
with
\gamma(0)=Q
and
\gamma(1)=Q^{\prime}
. The path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
is a single wall-crossing automorphism associated to the wall
\mathfrak{d}
whose support is
\mathcal{C}_{n}\cap\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
. Then any monomial
x^{m^{\prime}}
in the expansion of
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x^{m})
must have that
r(m^{\prime})=r(m)+km_{\mathfrak{d}}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In particular,
r(m^{\prime})
is tangent to
\mathcal{C}_{n}\cap\mathcal{C}_{n+1}
if and only if
r(m)
is. Then the result follows by comparing the monomials containing
x^{m}
with
r(m)=m_{\mathrm{fin}}
on the two sides of
\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},m_{0}}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}})
.
∎

Proposition 2.32

(cf.
[
27
, Proposition 3.6]
)
.

The mutation
T
in
Theorem

2.16
defines a one-to-one correspondence
\beta\mapsto T(\beta)
between broken lines for
m_{0}\in\mathscr{P}
with endpoint
Q=(q_{1},q_{2})\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
for
\mathfrak{D}=\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
and broken lines for
T(m_{0})
with endpoint
T(Q)
for
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}=\Scat(P_{1}^{\prime},P_{2})
such that

\operatorname{Mono}(T(\beta))=\begin{cases}T(\operatorname{Mono}(\beta))\quad&q_{2}\leq 0,\\
\operatorname{Mono}(\beta)\quad&q_{2}\geq 0.\end{cases}

Proof.

One constructs the bijection in the same way as in the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 3.6]
.
∎

Proposition 2.33
.

Let
m_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{n}\cap M
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
Q
be in the interior of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
. Then

\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}=x^{m_{0}}.

Proof.

There is always the straight broken line without bending which contributes to
x^{m_{0}}
in
\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}
. We need to show that this is the only broken line.

When
n=0
,
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is the first quadrant. For a broken line
\beta
, if there is
t\in(-\infty,0)
such that
\dot{\beta}(t)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{2}
and
\beta(t)\notin\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, then
\beta
can never end at
Q
in the interior of
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. This is because
\beta
can further bend only at the negative
x
-axis,
y
-axis, or the rays in the third quadrant. So
\dot{\beta}(t)
(whenever defined) always stays in
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{2}
and thus
\beta
can never reach the first quadrant. Therefore we have proven the case
n=0
.

For any other
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, by applying
Proposition

2.32
to a sequence of mutations we have a bijection between the broken lines for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
for
\mathfrak{D}
and the broken lines for
T^{(n)}(m_{0})
with endpoint
T^{(n)}(Q)
for
\mathfrak{D}^{(n)}
. Both
T^{(n)}(Q)
and
T^{(n)}(m_{0})
are in the positive chamber of
\mathfrak{D}^{(n)}
. There is thus only one straight broken line by the case
n=0
. It is pulled back to be the straight broken line
\beta
in
\mathfrak{D}
with
\operatorname{Mono}(\beta)=x^{m_{0}}
.
∎

Let
Q_{+}
denote a general point in the first quadrant
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{2}
. It follows directly from
Proposition

2.33
and
Theorem

2.29
that for any
m
in
\mathcal{C}_{n}\cap M
,

(2.34)

\vartheta_{Q_{+},m}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x^{m}),

where
\gamma
is a regular path from
Q\in\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
to
Q_{+}
.

We now consider a sequence of theta functions known as
cluster variables
. This is an unorthodox way to introduce cluster variables, but it will soon be clear that they satisfy (generalized) cluster exchange relations
[
18
,
9
]
. Denote
\vartheta_{m}=\vartheta_{Q_{+},m}
.

Definition 2.35
.

The
cluster variables

\{x_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
are defined to be

x_{n}\coloneqq\vartheta_{-{\mathbf{d}}_{n}}.

Lemma 2.36
.

The cluster variables satisfy the following cluster exchange relations

(1)

x_{0}x_{2}=P_{1}(x_{1})
,

(2)

x_{1}x_{3}=P_{2}(x_{2})
,

(3)

x_{n-1}x_{n+1}=\bar{\zeta}_{n}(x_{n})
for
n\neq 1,2
,

where
\bar{\zeta}_{n}(z)\coloneqq z^{\deg(\zeta_{n})}\zeta_{n}(1/z)
.

Proof.

We first check (1) and (2). By
Proposition

2.33
, we have
x_{1}=x
and
x_{2}=y
. Notice
x_{0}=\vartheta_{(0,-1)}
and
x_{3}=\vartheta_{(-1,0)}
. By (
2.34
), we have
x_{0}=x_{2}^{-1}P_{1}(x_{1})
and
x_{3}=x_{1}^{-1}P_{2}(x_{2})
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\gamma_{n}
be a regular path going from the interior of
\mathcal{C}_{n}
to the first quadrant
\mathcal{C}_{0}
. For some
n\neq 1,2
, let
\gamma
be a regular path going from
\mathcal{C}_{n-1}
to
\mathcal{C}_{n-2}
by crossing the wall
(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}d_{n},\zeta_{n})
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(x^{-d_{1;n+1}}y^{-d_{2;n+1}})

\displaystyle=x^{-d_{1;n+1}}y^{-d_{2;n+1}}\zeta_{n}(x^{d_{1;n}}y^{d_{2;n}})

\displaystyle=x^{\deg(\zeta_{n})d_{1;n}-d_{1;n+1}}y^{\deg(\zeta_{n})d_{2;n}-d_{2;n+1}}\bar{\zeta}_{n}(x^{-d_{1;n}}y^{-d_{2;n}})

\displaystyle=x^{d_{1;n-1}}y^{d_{2;n-1}}\bar{\zeta}_{n}(x^{-d_{1;n}}y^{-d_{2;n}}).

Applying
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{n-2},\mathfrak{D}}
to both sides of the above equation, we obtain

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{n-1},\mathfrak{D}}\left(x^{-d_{1;n+1}}y^{-d_{2;n+1}}\right)=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{n-2},\mathfrak{D}}\left(x^{d_{1;n-1}}y^{d_{2;n-1}}\right)\bar{\zeta}_{n}\left(\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma_{n-2},\mathfrak{D}}\left(x^{-d_{1;n}}y^{-d_{2;n}}\right)\right).

Now by (
2.34
), we have for
n\geq 3
,

x_{n+1}=x_{n-1}^{-1}\bar{\zeta}_{n}(x_{n}).\qed

2.4.
Theta functions are universal Laurent

Recall the notations in
Section

2.2
. In that situation the monoid
\mathscr{P}
decomposes as
M\oplus\mathbb{M}
, where
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}
embeds in
\mathbb{P}
the group of Laurent monomials in
\{p_{i,j}\mid i=1,2,1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}\}
. Elements in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
are regarded as coefficients.

Let
\mathcal{F}
denote the field of rational functions
\operatorname{Frac}(\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}])
. The generalized
cluster algebra

\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
is defined to be the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
-subalgebra in
\mathcal{F}
generated by all cluster variables
\{x_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. The generalized
upper cluster algebra
is defined to be

(2.37)

\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}(P_{1},P_{2})\coloneqq\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{n}^{\pm 1},x_{n+1}^{\pm 1}]\subseteq\mathcal{F}.

An element in
\mathcal{U}
is called
universal Laurent
as it belongs to every
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{n}^{\pm 1},x_{n+1}^{\pm 1}]
.

Proposition 2.38
.

For any
m\in M
, the theta function
\vartheta_{m}
belongs to
{\mathcal{U}}
. Hence
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

This is a slight generalization from
[
10
, Theorem 3.9(b)(c)]
to our setting. For each
\mathcal{C}_{n}
, choose a general point
Q_{n}
in its interior. If
m\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have
\vartheta_{m}=\vartheta_{Q_{0},m}=x^{m}
by
Proposition

2.33
. If
m\notin\mathbb{N}^{2}
, for any broken line
\beta
for
m
with endpoint
Q_{0}
, the vector
m_{\beta}\in M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
has at least one negative coordinate. Then there are finitely many walls where any such
\beta
can bend. Moreover only finitely many terms on these wall-functions are relevant. Then
\vartheta_{m}
can be equivalently computed in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
for a large
k
. Therefore,
\vartheta_{m}
is a Laurent monomials. This shows
\vartheta_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1}]
for any
m\in M
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we first show that
\vartheta_{Q_{n},m}
is in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1}]
. By
Proposition

2.32
, there is a piecewise linear transformation
T^{(n)}\colon M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow M_{\mathbb{R}}
that takes
\mathcal{C}_{n}
to the positive chamber of
\mathfrak{D}^{(n)}
, which induces a one-to-one correspondence between broken lines in
\mathfrak{D}
and those in
\mathfrak{D}^{(n)}
. Since
T^{(n)}(Q_{n})
lies in the positive chamber of
\mathfrak{D}^{(n)}
, there are finitely many broken lines by the first paragraph. Thus there are too only finitely many broken lines for
\vartheta_{Q_{n},m}
, which means
\vartheta_{Q_{n},m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1}]
.

Each cluster variable
x_{n}
corresponds to the ray
\mathcal{C}_{n-2}\cap\mathcal{C}_{n-1}
in the cluster complex. The primitive integral generator of this ray is
-{\mathbf{d}}_{n}
(see
Definition

2.35
). Any pair
\{{\mathbf{d}}_{n},{\mathbf{d}}_{n+1}\}
is a basis of
M
(
Lemma

2.19
). Then for any
m\in M
we have

\vartheta_{Q_{n},m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x^{\pm{\mathbf{d}}_{n+1}},x^{\pm{\mathbf{d}}_{n+2}}].

Therefore for a regular path
\gamma
from
Q_{n}
to
Q_{0}
, we have by
Theorem

2.29
that

\vartheta_{m}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}(\vartheta_{Q_{n},m})\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{n+1}^{\pm 1},x_{n+2}^{\pm 1}].\qed

Definition 2.39
.

The set of theta functions
\{\vartheta_{m}\mid m\in M\}
is called the
generalized theta basis
of
\mathcal{U}
.

Remark 2.40
.

One can prove that theta functions
\{\vartheta_{m}\mid m\in M\}
form a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
-basis of
\mathcal{U}
analogously as in
[
27
]
. This also follows from the fact that theta functions equal so-called
greedy elements
, proven in
Section

4
, and that the greedy elements form a basis of
\mathcal{U}
.

3.
Greedy bases for rank-2 generalized cluster algebras

Following Lee–Li–Zelevinsky
[
36
]
in the cluster algebra setting and Rupel
[
51
]
in the palindromic generalized cluster algebra setting, we define the generalized greedy basis for rank-
2
generalized cluster algebras. We will then, following these previous works, show that the coefficients can be realized as counts of combinatorial objects called
compatible gradings
.

3.1.
Generalized greedy elements

Let
\mathcal{T}_{k}=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k}^{\pm 1},x_{k+1}^{\pm 1}]
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Therefore
\mathcal{U}=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{T}_{k}
from (
2.37
).

Definition 3.1
.

An element
x\in\mathcal{T}_{k}
is
pointed
at
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
in the
k^{\text{th}}
cluster if it can be expressed in the form

x=x_{k}^{-d_{1}}x_{k+1}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{p,q\geq 0}c(p,q)x_{k}^{p}x_{k+1}^{q}

for some
c(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
and
c(0,0)=1
.

An element pointed in the initial cluster
\mathcal{T}_{1}
is simply called
pointed
.

Definition 3.2
.

For a sequence of variables
p_{\bullet}=(p_{k})_{k=1}^{\ell}
where
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
with
\deg p_{k}=k
, we write for any
m\geq 0

\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 1}p_{k}x^{k}\right)^{m}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\pi_{m,n}(p_{\bullet})x^{n},

defining
\pi_{m,n}(p_{\bullet})
as a homogeneous polynomial in
(p_{k})_{k}
of degree
n
. We write for
m\geq 0

\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 1}p_{k}x^{k}\right)^{-m}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\varsigma_{m,n}(p_{\bullet})x^{n},

defining
\varsigma_{m,n}(p_{\bullet})
as a homogeneous polynomial in
(p_{k})_{k}
of degree
n
.

We take the conventions that
\pi_{m,0}(p_{\bullet})=\varsigma_{m,0}(p_{\bullet})=1
for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and that
\pi_{m,n}(p_{\bullet})=\varsigma_{m,n}(p_{\bullet})=0
when
m<0
and
n\neq 0
.

Lemma 3.3
.

For any
s\geq 1

\sum_{k=0}^{s}\pi_{m,s-k}(p_{\bullet})\varsigma_{m,k}(p_{\bullet})=0.

Proof.

By
Definition

3.2
, we have
\left(\sum_{n\geq 0}\pi_{m,n}(p_{\bullet})x^{n}\right)\left(\sum_{n\geq 0}\varsigma_{m,n}(p_{\bullet})x^{n}\right)=1
. The statement then follows from isolating the
s^{\text{th}}
coefficient of this product.
∎

The above lemma implies

(3.4)

\pi_{m,s}(p_{\bullet})=\sum_{k=1}^{s}-\pi_{m,s-k}(p_{\bullet})\varsigma_{m,k}(p_{\bullet}).

This can be seen as a recursive definition of
\pi_{m,s}(p_{\bullet})
for
m,s\geq 1
. The fact that any
\pi_{m,s}(p_{\bullet})
has nonnegative integer coefficients is less obvious from this definition. Next we consider a two-dimensional recursion generalizing (
3.4
).

For an element
f
in any polynomial ring
Y=\mathbb{Z}[y_{1},...,y_{n}]
where
y_{1},...,y_{n}
are algebraically independent, and for
e=(e_{1},...,e_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
, let
[y^{e}]f=[y_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots y_{n}^{e_{n}}]f
be the coefficient of
y_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots y_{n}^{e_{n}}
in
f
. For a pair of elements
f
and
g
in
Y
, we define

\operatorname{T-max}(f,g)\coloneqq\sum_{e\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\max([y^{e}]f,[y^{e}]g)y^{e}.

Consider two sequences of variables
p_{i,\bullet}=(p_{i,k})_{k=1}^{\ell_{i}}
for
i=1,2
and
\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\cup\{\infty\}
.

Definition 3.5
.

Fix
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Define
c(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}[p_{1,\bullet}\cup p_{2,\bullet}]
for any
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
by the base case
c(0,0)=1
and the recursion

c(p,q)\coloneqq\operatorname{T-max}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{p}-c(p-k,q)\varsigma_{d_{2}-q,k}(p_{1,\bullet}),\ \sum_{k=1}^{q}-c(p,q-k)\varsigma_{d_{1}-p,k}(p_{2,\bullet})\right).

Remark 3.6
.

It is not hard to determine all
c(p,q)
unless both
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are positive. In fact, we have by (
3.4
) that

(1)

when
d_{1}\leq 0,d_{2}\leq 0
, every
c(p,q)=0
except
c(0,0)=1
;

(2)

when
d_{1}\leq 0,d_{2}\geq 0
, every
c(p,q)=0
except
c(p,0)=\pi_{d_{2},p}(p_{1,\bullet})
;

(3)

when
d_{1}\geq 0,d_{2}\leq 0
, every
c(p,q)=0
except
c(0,q)=\pi_{d_{1},q}(p_{2,\bullet})
.

Definition 3.7
.

Define
d_{+}(0,0)=d_{-}(0,0)\coloneqq c(0,0)=1
, and for
(p,q)\neq(0,0)
,

\displaystyle d_{+}(p,q)\coloneqq c(p,q)-\sum_{k=1}^{p}-c(p-k,q)\varsigma_{d_{2}-q,k}(p_{1,\bullet}),

\displaystyle d_{-}(p,q)\coloneqq c(p,q)-\sum_{k=1}^{q}-c(p,q-k)\varsigma_{d_{1}-p,k}(p_{2,\bullet}).

By definition, both
d_{\pm}(p,q)
are polynomials with nonnegative integer coefficients.

Lemma 3.8
.

We have

\displaystyle c(p,q)

\displaystyle=d_{+}(p,q)+\sum_{s=1}^{p}\pi_{d_{2}-q,s}(p_{1,\bullet})d_{+}(p-s,q)

\displaystyle=d_{-}(p,q)+\sum_{s=1}^{q}\pi_{d_{1}-p,s}(p_{2,\bullet})d_{-}(p,q-s).

Proof.

We prove the first equality by induction on
p
. By the definition of
d_{+}(p,q)
, it is equivalent to show

(3.9)

\sum_{k=1}^{p}-c(p-k,q)\varsigma_{d_{2}-q,k}(p_{1,\bullet})=\sum_{s=1}^{p}\pi_{d_{2}-q,s}(p_{1,\bullet})d_{+}(p-s,q).

We only need to deal with the case when
d_{2}-q\geq 1
. Assume that the first equality is true for
c(0,q),\dots,c(p-1,q)
. Then we can write the left-hand side of (
3.9
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{p}-\varsigma_{d_{2}-q,k}(p_{1,\bullet})\sum_{t=0}^{p-k}\pi_{d_{2}-q,t}(p_{1,\bullet})d_{+}(p-k-t,q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{p}d_{+}(p-s,q)\sum_{k=1}^{s}-\pi_{d_{2}-q,s-k}(p_{1,\bullet})\varsigma_{d_{2}-q,k}(p_{1,\bullet}).

Applying (
3.4
), the above equals the right-hand side of (
3.9
).

The second equality of the statement can be proven similarly by induction on
q
.
∎

A consequence of
Lemma

3.8
is that each
c(p,q)
has nonnegative integer coefficients.

Definition 3.10
.

For
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we define the
greedy element

x[d_{1},d_{2}]\coloneqq x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}c(p,q)x_{1}^{p}x_{2}^{q}\in x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\mathbb{N}[p_{1,\bullet}\cup p_{2,\bullet}]\llbracket x_{1},x_{2}\rrbracket.

According to
Remark

3.6
, the greedy elements are clear in the following cases

(3.11)

x[d_{1},d_{2}]=\begin{cases}x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\quad&d_{1}\leq 0,d_{2}\leq 0,\\
x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 1}p_{1,k}x_{1}^{k}\right)^{d_{1}}\quad&d_{1}\geq 0,d_{2}\leq 0,\\
x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 1}p_{2,k}x_{2}^{k}\right)^{d_{2}}\quad&d_{1}\leq 0,d_{2}\geq 0.\end{cases}

When
p_{1,\bullet}
and
p_{2,\bullet}
are both finite sequences, in the cases of (
3.11
),
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
is a finite sum, thus a pointed element in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
(
Definition

3.1
). When
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are positive,
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
is also a finite sum. This fact is non-trivial from the definition and is a consequence of the combinatorial formula of
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
given in
Theorem

3.18
, where each
c(p,q)
is interpreted as the weighted count of combinatorial objects called
compatible gradings
.

3.2.
Compatible gradings

Definition 3.12
.

A
North-East (NE) path

\mathcal{P}
is a lattice path in
(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R})\cup(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z})\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
starting at
(0,0)
and ending at
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, proceeding by only unit north and east steps. We also view
\mathcal{P}
as the set of its unit north and east steps, where we refer to each step as an
edge
.

•

Given a set
C
of edges in
\mathcal{P}
, we denote the set of horizontal edges (east steps) by
C_{\mathbf{E}}
and the set of vertical edges (north steps) by
C_{\mathbf{N}}
. We let
|C|
denote the number of edges in
C
.

•

For edges
e
and
f
in
\mathcal{P}
, let
\overrightarrow{ef}
denote the subpath proceeding east from
e
to
f
(including both
e
and
f
), continuing cyclically around
\mathcal{P}
if
e
is to the north or east of
f
.

Note that if
e=f
, then
\overrightarrow{ef}=\{e\}
. If
e
is the edge adjacent to
f
and to the northeast of
f
, then
\overrightarrow{ef}=\mathcal{P}
.

Consider the rectangle
R(d_{1},d_{2})
with vertices
(0,0)
,
(0,d_{2})
,
(d_{1},0)
, and
(d_{1},d_{2})
with a main diagonal from
(0,0)
to
(d_{1},d_{2})
. A
Dyck path
is an NE path from
(0,0)
to
(d_{1},d_{2})
that never passes strictly above the main diagonal of
R(d_{1},d_{2})
. The Dyck paths from
(0,0)
to
(d_{1},d_{2})
form a partially ordered set by comparing the heights at all vertices. The
maximal Dyck path

\mathcal{P}(d_{1},d_{2})
is the maximal element under this partial order. When
d_{1}
and
d_{2}
are relatively prime, the maximal Dyck path
\mathcal{P}(d_{1},d_{2})
corresponds to the lower Christoffel word of slope
d_{2}/d_{1}
. We label the horizontal edges from left to right by
u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{d_{1}}
and the vertical edges from bottom to top by
v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{d_{2}}
.

Example 3.13
.

In
Figure

2
, the maximal Dyck path
\mathcal{P}(6,4)
is shown in the top left and
\mathcal{P}(7,4)
is shown in the top right.

Motivated by Lee–Schiffler
[
37
]
, Lee, Li, and Zelevinsky
[
36
]
introduced combinatorial objects called
compatible pairs
to construct the
greedy basis
for rank-
2
cluster algebras, consisting of indecomposable positive elements including the cluster monomials. Rupel
[
51
,
52
]
extended this construction to the setting of
generalized
rank-
2
cluster algebras by defining
compatible gradings
.

A
grading

\omega:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{N}
assigns a nonnegative integer to every edge of the NE path
\mathcal{P}
. For any grading
\omega
and for any set of edges
C\subseteq\mathcal{P}
, let
\displaystyle\omega(C)\coloneqq\sum_{e\in C}\omega(e)
.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be an NE path. A grading
\omega:\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is called
compatible
if for every pair of
u\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}}
and
v\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}}
with
\omega(u)\omega(v)>0
, there exists an edge
e
along the subpath
\overrightarrow{uv}
so that at least one of the following holds:

(3.15)

\displaystyle e\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}}\setminus\{v\}\quad\text{and}\quad|\overrightarrow{ue}_{\mathbf{N}}|=\omega\left(\overrightarrow{ue}_{\mathbf{E}}\right);

\displaystyle e\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}}\setminus\{u\}\quad\text{and}\quad|\overrightarrow{ev}_{\mathbf{E}}|=\omega\left(\overrightarrow{ev}_{\mathbf{N}}\right).

Example 3.16
.

For each
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, let
\omega_{i}:\mathcal{P}(i+5,4)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be the grading given by
\omega_{i}(u_{1})=\omega_{i}(u_{2})=2
,
\omega_{i}(v_{3})=\omega_{i}(v_{4})=3
, and
\omega_{i}(e)=0
for every edge
e
in
\mathcal{P}(i+5,4)\setminus\{u_{1},u_{2},v_{3},v_{4}\}
(see
Figure

2
for
i=1,2
). Then
\omega_{1}
is not compatible, but
\omega_{2}
and
\omega_{3}
are compatible. The main difference between
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
is that the edge
e=u_{2}
in
\mathcal{P}(7,4)
satisfies the second condition in (
3.15
) for
u=u_{1}
and
v=v_{4}
, as both sides of the equation equal
6
.

3.3.
Generalized greedy element formula

We now give a combinatorial interpretation of the coefficients of generalized greedy elements using compatible gradings, as presented in
Theorem

3.18
. This generalizes the work of Rupel
[
50
, Theorem 2.15]
to the non-palindromic setting, which will be needed in our discussion on the positivity of higher-rank scattering diagrams in
Section

9
. The proof of
Theorem

3.18
, which is inspired by Rupel’s work, will be given in
Appendix

A
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
denote the multiplicative monoid of monomials in
p_{1,\bullet}\cup p_{2,\bullet}
.

Definition 3.17
.

The
weight
of a grading
\omega:\mathcal{P}(d_{1},d_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
is the monomial

\text{wt}(\omega)\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{d_{1}}p_{2,\omega(u_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{d_{2}}p_{1,\omega(v_{j})}\in\mathbb{M}\,.

Fix
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Let
\CG(d_{1},d_{2},p,q)=\CG(p,q)
denote the set of all compatible gradings
\omega
on
\mathcal{P}([d_{1}]_{+},[d_{2}]_{+})
such that
\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})=q
and
\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})=p
. Define the weighted sum

|\CG(p,q)|\coloneqq\sum_{\omega\in\CG(p,q)}\text{wt}(\omega)\in\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{M}]\,.

The coefficients
c(p,q)
of a generalized greedy element are then given as weighted sums of compatible gradings on a fixed Dyck path with a fixed total vertical and horizontal grading.

Theorem 3.18
.

For any
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the greedy element
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
can be computed as

(3.19)

x[d_{1},d_{2}]=x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{\omega}\wt(\omega)x_{1}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}\,,

where the sum is over all compatible gradings
\omega
on
\mathcal{P}([d_{1}]_{+},[d_{2}]_{+})
. In other words, for any
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have

c(p,q)=|\CG(p,q)|\,.

When
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}<\infty
, we set in
Definition

3.17
that
p_{1,k}=0
for any
k>\ell_{1}
and
p_{2,k}=0
for any
k>\ell_{2}
. Then in this case, it follows from (
3.19
) that
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
is always a finite sum as there are only finitely many gradings with non-zero weights.

We will show in
Corollary

A.10
that
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
is not only a pointed element in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
, but also belongs to any other
\mathcal{T}_{k}
(hence in
\mathcal{U}
which actually equals
\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
). Moreover, the set of all greedy elements

\{x[d_{1},d_{2}]\mid(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}

is in fact a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
-basis of
\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
. This statement generalizes
[
36
, Theorem 1.7(c)]
and can be proven analogously as in
[
36
, Section 6]
. This basis will be referred to as the
generalized greedy basis
.

4.
The generalized greedy basis equals the generalized theta basis

It was shown by Cheung, Gross, Muller, Musiker, Rupel, Stella, and Williams that, in the setting of cluster algebras, the greedy basis equals the theta basis
[
10
]
. Their proof is algebraic, using a characterization of greedy elements by their support and properties of rank-
2
scattering diagrams. We extend their results to the generalized setting.

Theorem 4.1
.

The generalized greedy basis equals the generalized theta basis. More precisely,
\vartheta_{-(d_{1},d_{2})}=x[d_{1},d_{2}]
for any
(d_{1},d_{2})\in M
.

Before presenting the proof, we establish a couple of lemmas. First, we give a support condition that can be used to identify generalized greedy elements, following
[
10
, Scholium 2.6]
. A crucial part of this argument is the compatible grading formula for coefficients of generalized greedy elements, which is developed in the previous section (see
Theorem

3.18
).

Definition 4.2
.

Let
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
be a region in
M_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
defined as follows:

(1)

If
d_{1},d_{2}\leq 0
, then
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}=\{(-d_{1},-d_{2})\}
,

(2)

If
d_{1}\leq 0<d_{2}
, then
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}=\{(m_{1},-d_{2}):-d_{1}\leq m_{1}\leq-d_{1}+\ell_{1}d_{2}\}
,

(3)

If
d_{2}\leq 0<d_{1}
, then
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}=\{(-d_{1},m_{2}):-d_{2}\leq m_{2}\leq-d_{2}+\ell_{2}d_{1}\}
,

(4)

Otherwise, consider the vertices
O=(0,0)
,
A=(-d_{1}+\ell_{1}d_{2},-d_{2})
,
B=(-d_{1},-d_{2})
, and
C=(-d_{1},-d_{2}+\ell_{2}d_{1})
. Then
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
is the interior of the polygon
OABC
along with the closed line segments
AB
and
BC
(see
[
10
, Figure 1]
).

Given a Laurent polynomial
f=\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}c_{m}x^{m}
in
\Bbbk[\mathbb{Z}^{\ell_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\ell_{2}}][x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1}]
, the support of
f
is the set
\{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:c_{m}\neq 0\}
. Rupel showed that the region
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
contains the support of the generalized greedy element
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
when
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
satisfy certain restrictions. In fact, this result also holds with these restrictions removed.

Proposition 4.3

(
[
51
, Proposition 4.22]
)
.

The support of the generalized greedy element
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
is contained in
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
.

Proof.

Rupel showed that this holds when
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are palindromic polynomials by considering the generalized greedy coefficients as counts of compatible gradings (
[
51
, Theorem 2.15]
). In the previous section, we extend this interpretation to the non-palindromic setting (see
Theorem

3.18
). Rupel’s argument then also holds without requiring
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
to be palindromic.
∎

We then obtain the following characterization of generalized greedy elements.

Lemma 4.4

(
[
10
, Scholium 2.6]
)
.

If
z\in\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
is any element containing the monomial
x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}
with coefficient
1
and whose support is contained in
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
, then
z=x[d_{1},d_{2}]
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the proof of
[
10
, Theorem 2.5]
and the proof of
[
10
, Scholium 2.6]
, both of which carry through in the generalized cluster algebra setting.
∎

Thus, it is enough to show that the support of each generalized theta basis element is contained in
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
for a corresponding choice of
d_{1},d_{2}
. This is shown in the classical setting in
[
10
, Section 5]
, and their methods work essentially verbatim in the generalized case. Their results do not rely on the structure of the scattering diagram, other than that all walls are along the coordinate axes or in the third quadrant, which holds for the generalized cluster scattering diagrams. This culminates in bounds on the final exponent of the broken lines contributing to the generalized theta basis, yielding the desired bounds on the support of the theta basis elements.

Definition 4.5
.

Given a broken line
\beta
, fix a point
q=(q_{1},q_{2})\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
on the linear portion
\beta|_{(\tau_{i-1},\tau_{i})}
. The
angular momentum of
\beta
(at
q
)
is given by the dot product
(q_{2},-q_{1})\cdot r(m_{i})
.

The angular momentum of a broken line does not depend on the choice of
q
, as shown in
[
10
, Lemma 5.3]
. Broken lines of positive (resp. negative) angular momentum proceed counter-clockwise (resp. clockwise) around the origin. The behavior of broken lines terminating in the first quadrant can be characterized based on the sign of their angular momentum
[
10
, Lemma 5.4]
. If
\beta
is a broken line of positive (resp. negative) angular momentum, then the slopes of the linear domains of
\beta
decrease (resp. increase) at each bend, except possibly at the boundary of the first quadrant.

Fix a general point
Q_{+}
in the first quadrant of
M_{\mathbb{R}}=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and denote
\vartheta_{Q_{+},m}=\vartheta_{m}
for
m\in M
.

Lemma 4.6

(
[
10
, Lemma 5.5]
)
.

Let
\gamma
be a broken line in
\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
which begins in the third quadrant, with endpoint
Q_{+}
in the first quadrant. Denote the initial exponent by
(-d_{1},-d_{2})
and the final exponent by
m(\gamma)=(m_{1},m_{2})
.

(1)

If
\gamma
has positive angular momentum, then
-d_{2}\leq m_{2}<0
and

-d_{1}\leq m_{1}\leq\left(\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}-\ell_{1}\right)m_{2}\,,

where the upper bound is achieved only when
(m_{1},m_{2})=(-d_{1}+\ell_{1}d_{2},-d_{2})
.

(2)

If
\gamma
has negative angular momentum, then
-d_{1}\leq m_{1}<0
and

-d_{2}\leq m_{2}\leq\left(\frac{d_{2}}{d_{1}}-\ell_{2}\right)m_{1}\,,
