where the upper bound is achieved only when
(m_{1},m_{2})=(-d_{1},-d_{2}+\ell_{2}d_{1})
.

We now have all the tools necessary to prove
Theorem

4.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.1
.

We proceed by showing that the theta function
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

4.4
, following the approach of
[
10
, Proof of Theorem 5.1]
. First, note that a broken line with the same initial and terminal momentum in
\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
must not bend, as any bending would increase one of the entries of the exponent. There is a unique broken line terminating at
Q_{+}
that does not bend and has terminal momentum
(-d_{1},-d_{2})
, which contributes the term
x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}
to
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
. Hence
x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}
appears with coefficient
1
in
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
. For the support condition, we split into four cases.

(1)

If
d_{1},d_{2}\leq 0
, then by
Proposition

2.33
we have
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}=x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}
, so the support is simply
\{(-d_{1},-d_{2})\}=R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
.

(2)

If
d_{1}\leq 0<d_{2}
, then a broken line with initial momentum
(-d_{1},-d_{2})
terminating at
Q_{+}
can bend only at the
x
-axis. This follows because any other bending would need to occur to the left of
Q_{+}
, and such a broken line can never move in the positive
x
direction to return to
Q_{+}
. Thus, the terminal momentums of broken lines that contribute to
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
are precisely
(-d_{1},-d_{2})+i(\ell_{1},0)
for any integer
i\in\{0,1,\dots,\ell_{1}\}
.

(3)

If
d_{2}\leq 0<d_{1}
, then an analogous argument to that in Case (2) applies.

(4)

If
d_{1},d_{2}>0
, then we can apply
Lemma

4.6
to conclude that the final exponent of any broken line
\gamma
contributing to
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
must have final exponent contained in
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
.

Therefore, the terminal momentum of any broken line contributing to
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
, and hence the support of
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
, is contained in
R^{\circ}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
. Applying
Lemma

4.4
, we can conclude that
\vartheta_{(-d_{1},-d_{2})}=x[d_{1},d_{2}]
.
∎

5.
Shadowed gradings and tight gradings

We define new combinatorial objects called
tight gradings
that give a combinatorial interpretation of the wall-function coefficients in rank-
2
generalized cluster scattering diagrams. By considering a larger class of gradings called
shadowed gradings
, we also give a combinatorial interpretation of the coefficients of powers of the wall-functions. The latter is useful for computing Euler characteristics of quiver moduli (see
Section

6.1
) and relative Gromov–Witten invariant in toric surfaces (see
Section

6.2
).

In this section, we begin by defining shadowed gradings and tight gradings. We then describe certain conditions under which we can isolate a unique broken line that corresponds to a set of shadowed gradings. Utilizing these conditions, we relate the combinatorial approach of Lee–Li–Zelevinsky and the scattering diagram approach of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich
[
36
,
27
]
. This yields a directly computable, manifestly positive, and elementary but highly nontrivial formula describing rank-2 consistent scattering diagrams.

5.1.
Shadowed gradings and tight gradings

We now introduce shadowed gradings and tight gradings, which are subsets of the compatible gradings defined by Rupel
[
51
]
and inspired by the compatible pairs of Lee–Li–Zelevinsky
[
36
]
(see
Section

3.2
). We first require the notion of the “shadow” of a set of edges in an NE path.

Definition 5.1
.

Fix an NE path
\mathcal{P}
and a grading
\omega\colon\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
.
For each edge
e
in
\mathcal{P}
, we define its
shadow
, denoted by
\sh(e)
or
\sh(e;\omega)
, as follows.

•

If
e
is horizontal, then its shadow is
(\overrightarrow{ev})_{\mathbf{N}}
, where
v\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}}
is chosen such that
|(\overrightarrow{ev})_{\mathbf{N}}|=\omega((\overrightarrow{ev})_{\mathbf{E}})
and
\overrightarrow{ev}
has minimal length under this condition. If no such
v
exists, let
\sh(e)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}}
.

•

If
e
is vertical, then its shadow is
(\overrightarrow{ue})_{\mathbf{E}}
, where
u\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}}
is chosen such that
|(\overrightarrow{ue})_{\mathbf{E}}|=\omega((\overrightarrow{ue})_{\mathbf{N}})
and
\overrightarrow{ue}
has minimal length under this condition. If no such
u
exists, let
\sh(e)=\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}}
.

For
S\subset\mathcal{P}
, let the
shadow
of
S
be
\sh(S)=\bigcup_{e\in S}\sh(e)
.

Note that if
\omega(e)=0
, then
\sh(e)=\emptyset
.

Example 5.2
.

Consider
\omega_{2}
as in
Example

3.16
. Then
\sh(u_{2})=\{v_{1},v_{2}\}
and
\sh(u_{1})=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{4}\}=\sh(\{u_{1},u_{2}\})
. We also have
\sh(v_{3})=\{u_{4},u_{5},u_{6}\}
and
\sh(v_{4})=\{u_{2},u_{3},\dots,u_{7}\}=\sh(\{v_{3},v_{4}\})
.

We then define a subset of compatible gradings where the vertical edges with nonzero grading value are contained in the shadow of the horizontal edges, or vice versa.

Definition 5.3
.

A
shadowed grading
on
\mathcal{P}
is a compatible grading satisfying either

S_{\mathbf{E}}\subseteq\sh(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})\quad\text{or}\quad S_{\mathbf{N}}\subseteq\sh(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})\,,

where
S
is the set of edges
e
in
\mathcal{P}
with
\omega(e)>0
.

The notion of shadowed gradings generalizes the notion of tight gradings, as first defined in
[
7
]
and partially motivated by
[
6
]
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
\omega
be a grading on
\mathcal{P}
with
p=\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})
and
q=\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})
.
The grading
\omega
is a
tight grading
if
\omega
is a shadowed grading satisfying
p\leq d_{1}
,
q\leq d_{2}
, and

pd_{2}-qd_{1}=\pm\gcd(p,q)\,.

We now explain how to represent shadowed gradings as rectangular tilings. This is important for the illustration of shadowed and tight gradings. First, rotate (i.e. cyclically shift) the maximal Dyck path so that the shadow of each horizontal (resp. vertical) edge does not extend beyond the left (resp. top) boundary of the rotated path. Such a rotation always exists provided
\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})\leq d_{1}
and
\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})\leq d_{2}
, which always holds for tight gradings. Then draw blue rectangles above each horizontal edge
e
with total height equal to
|\sh(e)|
, partitioned into the vertical grading values contributing to the shadow. Similarly, we draw red rectangles to the left of each edge in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}}
. The grading is compatible only if the resulting rectangles are non-overlapping. The condition on the shadows can be easily read off by seeing if every blue rectangle has a red rectangle above, or if every red rectangle has a blue rectangle to the left.

2
2
3
3
2
2
3
3

Figure 2.
In the top images, we depict gradings
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
on the Dyck paths
\mathcal{P}(6,4)
and
\mathcal{P}(7,4)
from
Example

3.16
, where edges with no weight shown are assigned weight
0
. Below, we depict the corresponding rectangular tilings. The grading
\omega_{1}
has overlapping rectangles and hence is not compatible, while the grading
\omega_{2}
is.

Remark 5.5
.

The word “tight” is coined by the tight space between blue and red rectangles.

Example 5.6
.

(1) The grading
\omega_{2}
as in
Example

3.16
(and displayed on the right of
Figure

2
) is shadowed. However, it is not tight because
(d_{1},d_{2})=(7,4)
does not satisfy
pd_{2}-qd_{1}=\pm\gcd(p,q)
for
(p,q)=(6,4)
.

(2) Let
(p,q)=(2,1)
and
(d_{1},d_{2})=(3,1)
. Let
\omega\colon\mathcal{P}(3,1)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be a grading such that
\omega(u_{1})=1
,
\omega(u_{2})=\omega(u_{3})=0
, and
\omega(v_{1})=2
. Then
\omega
is tight as displayed on the left of
Figure

3
.

(3) Let
(p,q)=(4,2)
and
(d_{1},d_{2})=(5,2)
. Let
\omega\colon\mathcal{P}(5,2)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be a grading such that
\omega(u_{1})=\omega(u_{2})=\omega(v_{1})=1
,
\omega(v_{2})=3
, and
\omega(u_{3})=\omega(u_{4})=\omega(u_{5})=0
. Then
\omega
is tight as displayed in the middle of
Figure

3
.

(4) Let
(p,q)=(6,3)
and
(d_{1},d_{2})=(7,3)
. Let
\omega\colon\mathcal{P}(7,3)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
be a grading such that
\omega(v_{2})=\omega(v_{3})=3
,
\omega(u_{1})=\omega(u_{2})=\omega(u_{3})=1
, and
\omega(v_{1})=\omega(u_{4})=\omega(u_{5})=\omega(u_{6})=\omega(u_{7})=0
. Then
\omega
is tight as displayed on the right of
Figure

3
.

(5) Let
(p,q)=(12,8)
and
(d_{1},d_{2})=(14,9)
. There are a total of 14 tight gradings
\omega
such that
\omega(h)=2
for exactly four horizontal edges
h
,
\omega(v)=3
for exactly four vertical edges
v
, and
\omega(e)=0
for all other edges on
\mathcal{P}(14,9)
.

Figure 3.
The three tight gradings in
Example

5.6
(2)(3)(4).

We now discuss two families of tight gradings with relevance to the rank-2 cluster scattering diagrams of affine type, i.e.,
\Scat(1+x^{\ell_{1}},1+y^{\ell_{2}})
where
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}=4
. This connection is made explicit in
Example

5.21
and
Example

5.22
.

Example 5.7
.

Let
P_{1}(x)=1+p_{1,2}x^{2}
and
P_{2}(y)=1+p_{2,2}y^{2}
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the path
\mathcal{P}(2k,2k+1)
. There are
k+1
tight gradings of weight
p_{1,2}^{k}p_{2,2}^{k}
, that is, tight gradings
\omega
where
\omega(h)=2
for exactly
k
horizontal edges
h
,
\omega(v)=2
for exactly
k
vertical edges
v
, and
\omega(e)=0
for all other edges
e
.

It is straightforward to describe these gradings explicitly as follows. Fix
i\in\{0,1,\dots,k\}
. Let
S_{\mathbf{E}}=\{u_{1+i},u_{2+i},\dots,u_{k+i}\}
and let
S_{\mathbf{N}}=\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{i}\}\cup\{v_{k+2+i},v_{k+3+i},\dots,v_{2k+1}\}
, where the sets in the union can be empty if
i=0
or
i=k
. Then the grading

\omega(e)=\begin{cases}2&\text{ if }e\in S_{\mathbf{E}}\cup S_{\mathbf{N}}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

is one of the desired tight gradings, and varying the choice of
i
yields all such tight gradings.

Figure 4.
The four tight gradings in the setting of
Example

5.7
when
k=3
.

Example 5.8
.

Let
P_{1}(x)=1+p_{1,4}x^{4}
and
P_{2}(y)=1+p_{2,1}y
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the path
\mathcal{P}(4k+1,2k)
. There are
2k+1
tight gradings of weight
p_{1,4}^{k}p_{2,1}^{2k}
. These are the tight gradings
\omega
such that
\omega(h)=1
for exactly
2k
horizontal edges
h
,
\omega(v)=4
for exactly
k
vertical edges
v
, and
\omega(e)=0
for all other edges
e
.

We can describe these gradings explicitly as follows. Fix
i\in\{0,1,\dots,k-1\}
and
s\in\{0,1\}
, with the restriction that if
i=k
then
s=0
. Let
S_{\mathbf{N}}=\{v_{k+1-i},v_{k+2-i},\dots,v_{2k-i}\}
and let
S_{\mathbf{E}}=\{u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{4k-2i-1}\}\cup\{u_{4k-2i+s}\}\cup\{u_{4k-2i+2},u_{4k-2i+3},\dots,u_{4k+1}\}
, where the sets in the union can be empty if
i=0
. Then the grading

\omega(e)=\begin{cases}2&\text{ if }e\in S_{\mathbf{E}}\cup S_{\mathbf{N}}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

is one of the desired tight gradings, and varying the choice of
i
yields all but one of the
2k+1
such tight gradings. The last tight grading can be obtained by considering
S_{\mathbf{E}}=\{u_{2k+2},u_{2k+3},\dots,u_{4k+1}\}
and
S_{\mathbf{N}}=\{v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\}
, with the grading
\omega
constructed as above.

Figure 5.
The five tight gradings in the setting of
Example

5.8
when
k=2
.

5.2.
One-bending property

The
bend-type
of a broken line is the tuple
(r(m_{0}),\dots,r(m_{\ell}))\in M^{\ell+1}
. In a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
, for a fixed endpoint
Q
, every broken line
\beta
with a given bend-type has identical image in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. This can be seen by working backwards from
Q
to reconstruct the broken line.

Consider a generalized cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}=\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and coprime
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that

d_{1}b-d_{2}a\neq 0.

Let
\beta
be a (possible) broken line in
\mathfrak{D}
such that it has initial exponent
(-d_{1},-d_{2})
, one unique bend at the wall
(\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b),f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)})
, and final exponent
(ka-d_{1},kb-d_{2})
for
k\geq 1
. We say that the pair
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfies the
one-bending property
(with respect to
\mathfrak{D}
) if any broken line that

(5.10)

has initial exponent
(-d_{1},-d_{2})
and final exponent
(-d_{1}+ka,-d_{2}+kb)
, and

(5.11)

does not bend at positive coordinate axes

has the same bend-type as
\beta
.

Remark 5.12
.

Let
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy the one-bending property. Depending on the choice of terminal point
Q\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
, there is either exactly one broken line or no broken lines satisfying (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) and terminating at
Q
. In particular, there will be one such broken line if

Q\in\begin{cases}\left\{(q_{1},q_{2})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}:q_{2}<\min\left(\frac{kb-d_{2}}{ka-d_{1}}q_{1},\frac{d_{2}}{d_{1}}q_{1}\right)\right\}&\text{ if }d_{1}b-d_{2}a>0,\\[10.0pt]
\left\{(q_{1},q_{2})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}:q_{2}>\max\left(\frac{kb-d_{2}}{ka-d_{1}}q_{1},\frac{d_{2}}{d_{1}}q_{1}\right)\right\}&\text{ if }d_{1}b-d_{2}a<0,\end{cases}

and no such broken lines otherwise.

Lemma 5.13
.

If
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy

\frac{kab+1}{kb^{2}}\geq\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}>\frac{a}{b}\quad\text{or}\quad\frac{ka^{2}}{kab+1}\leq\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}<\frac{a}{b},

then they satisfy the one-bending property.

Proof.

We prove for the first case and the second case is similar. It suffices to show that there is no broken line satisfying (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) that bends at least twice. Suppose that there is one such
\beta
. Notice that
\beta
can only bend at walls
strictly below
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(d_{1},d_{2})
, that is, walls
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
such that

\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}>\frac{a^{\prime}}{b^{\prime}}.

Denote the amount of bending cumulated between
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(d_{1},d_{2})
and
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)
(inclusive) by
(a_{1},b_{1})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Denote the amount of bending cumulated between
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)
(exclusive) and
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(0,1)
(inclusive) by
(a_{2},b_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Thus
(a_{1},b_{1})+(a_{2},b_{2})=(ka,kb)
. Now we have

\frac{kab+1}{kb^{2}}\geq\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}>\frac{a_{1}}{b_{1}}>\frac{a}{b}>\frac{a_{2}}{b_{2}}.

We note that since
\beta
bends at least twice, the last three inequalities are all strict.
We derive from
\dfrac{kab+1}{kb^{2}}>\dfrac{a_{1}}{b_{1}}>\dfrac{a}{b}
and
b_{1}\leq kb

that

ab_{1}+\frac{b_{1}}{kb}>a_{1}b>ab_{1}\implies ab_{1}+1>a_{1}b>ab_{1}.

There is no integer solution for
(a_{1},b_{1})
and hence such a broken line
\beta
does not exist.
∎

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
be coprime and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

If
(d_{1},d_{2})=(kab+1,kb^{2})
or
(ka^{2},kab+1)
, then
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy the one-bending property.

(2)

If
d_{1}\geq mka
when
d_{1}b-d_{2}a<0
or
d_{2}\geq mkb
when
d_{1}b-d_{2}a>0
where
m=|d_{1}b-d_{2}a|
, then
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy the one-bending property.

(3)

For any
m>0
, there exist infinitely many
(d_{1},d_{2})
such that
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy the one-bending property and that
m=|d_{1}b-d_{2}a|
.

Proof.

Statement (1) is a direct corollary of
Lemma

5.13
. We prove (2) when
m=d_{1}b-d_{2}a
, and the other case is similar. Again by
Lemma

5.13
, it suffices to check

\frac{kab+1}{kb^{2}}=\frac{a}{b}+\frac{1}{kb^{2}}\geq\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}\iff\frac{1}{kb^{2}}\geq\frac{m}{d_{2}b}\iff d_{2}\geq mkb.

For (3), choose some
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
so that
m=|d_{1}b-d_{2}a|
. By (2), for sufficiently large
N
,
(d_{1}+Na,d_{2}+Nb)
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy the one-bending property.
∎

Consider the ordinary cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})}=\Scat(1+x^{\ell_{1}},1+y^{\ell_{2}})
. More one-bending pairs can be found and will be useful in later applications. Notice that in this case, the total amount of bending of a broken line can only be of the form
(k\ell_{1}\bar{a},k\ell_{2}\bar{b})
,
where
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
(\bar{a},\bar{b})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
is coprime.

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
(\bar{a},\bar{b})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
be coprime and
k\geq 1
.

(1)

If
\bar{b}\leq\ell_{1}
, then the vectors
(d_{1},d_{2})=(k\ell_{1}\bar{a}+1,k\ell_{2}\bar{b})
and
(k\ell_{1}\bar{a},k\ell_{2}\bar{b})
satisfy the one-bending property with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})}
.

(2)

If
\bar{a}\leq\ell_{2}
, then the vectors
(d_{1},d_{2})=(k\ell_{1}\bar{a},k\ell_{2}\bar{b}+1)
and
(k\ell_{1}\bar{a},k\ell_{2}\bar{b})
satisfy the one-bending property with respect to
\mathfrak{D}_{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})}
.

Proof.

We prove (1) and the other case is similar. As in the proof of
Lemma

5.13
, it suffices to show that there is no integer solution
a_{1}\leq k\bar{a}
,
b_{1}\leq k\bar{b}
for the inequality

\displaystyle\frac{k\ell_{1}\bar{a}+1}{k\ell_{2}\bar{b}}>\frac{\ell_{1}a_{1}}{\ell_{2}b_{1}}>\frac{\ell_{1}\bar{a}}{\ell_{2}\bar{b}}\quad\iff\quad b_{1}\bar{a}+\frac{b_{1}}{k\ell_{1}}>a_{1}\bar{b}>b_{1}\bar{a}.

Since
b_{1}\leq k\bar{b}\leq k\ell_{1}
, we have that
a_{1}\bar{b}
belongs to an open interval without any integer in it.
∎

The above lemma applies in particular to the
central ray

\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(\ell_{1}\bar{a},\ell_{2}\bar{b})
with
(\bar{a},\bar{b})=(1,1)
.

5.3.
Formula for wall-functions

Fix two positive integers
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
. Consider the generalized cluster scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}=\Scat\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell_{1}}p_{1,k}x^{k},1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell_{2}}p_{2,k}y^{k}\right).

Let
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
be coprime. Let
m
be a positive integer. Then we can express the
m
-th power of the wall-function
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}
by

(5.16)

\left(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}\right)^{m}=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}\lambda^{(m)}(ka,kb)x^{ka}y^{kb},

where each coefficient
\lambda^{(m)}(a,b)
is a polynomial in
\{p_{i,j}\mid i=1,2,1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}\}
. When
m=1
, we simply denote
\lambda(ka,kb)=\lambda^{(1)}(ka,kb)
.

Theorem 5.17

(Shadowed grading formula)
.

(5.18)

\left(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}\right)^{m}=1+\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{\omega}\mathrm{wt}(\omega)x^{ka}y^{kb},

where the second sum is over all shadowed gradings on the Dyck path
\mathcal{P}(d_{1,k},d_{2,k})
of total horizontal weight
kb
and total vertical weight
ka
for any
(d_{1,k},d_{2,k})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that

•

d_{1,k}\geq ka
and
d_{2,k}\geq kb
,

•

(d_{1,k},d_{2,k})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy the one-bending property,

•

m=|ad_{2,k}-bd_{1,k}|
.

For any tuple
\mathbf{Q}=(q_{j})_{j=1}^{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, define the weighted sum
w(\mathbf{Q})\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}jq_{j}
. For tuples
\mathbf{Q}_{i}=(q_{i,j})_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell_{i}}
,
i=1,2
, denote
p_{i}^{\mathbf{Q}_{i}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}p_{i,j}^{q_{i,j}}
. Then the expression (
5.16
) can be refined as

(5.19)

\left(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}\right)^{m}=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka\\
w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb\end{subarray}}\lambda^{(m)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb}.

It follows from
Theorem

5.17
that for each
(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})
such that
w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka,w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb
,

\lambda^{(m)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})=\left|\left\{\omega\,\middle|\,\text{$\omega$ is shadowed},\ \mathrm{wt}(\omega)=p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}\right\}\right|,

that is, the number of shadowed gradings on
\mathcal{P}(d_{1,k},d_{2,k})
whose weight polynomial is
p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
. The fact that this number computes
\lambda_{a,b}^{(m)}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})
implies it does not depend on the choice of
(d_{1,k},d_{2,k})
. This turns out to be a rather delicate property of shadowed gradings from the view of combinatorics.

Remark 5.20
.

When
m=1
, any
d_{1,k}\geq ka
,
d_{2,k}\geq kb
such that
|ad_{2,k}-bd_{2,k}|=1
satisfy the one-bending property by
Corollary

5.14
(2). In this case, shadowed gradings are precisely tight gradings and the formula (
5.18
) was announced in
[
7
, Theorem 3.5]
.

We can use the formula (
5.18
) to directly calculate the wall-function of the single wall comprising the Badlands in an affine cluster scattering diagram.

Example 5.21
.

Consider the cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{(2,2)}
. It follows from
Theorem

5.17
and
Example

5.7
that

f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(1,1)}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(k+1)x^{2k}y^{2k}\,.

This was known to experts
[
32
]
and was shown using quiver representations by Reineke
[
47
]
.

Example 5.22
.

Consider the cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{(4,1)}
. It follows from
Theorem

5.17
and
Example

5.8
that

f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(2,1)}=\sum_{k\geq 0}(2k+1)x^{4k}y^{2k}\,.

This has been previously calculated by Reading
[
46
]
using limits of
F
-polynomial expressions.

5.4.
Proof of
Theorem

5.17

We now prove the shadowed grading formula for the coefficients of powers of wall-functions, as described in
Section

5.3
. This proof relies on the fact that the generalized greedy and generalized theta bases coincide, a result established in
Section

4
. We use the one-bending property from
Section

5.2
to refine the equality of these bases, allowing us to find the shadowed gradings corresponding to a particularly nice broken line. This broken line is chosen to have weight equal to a chosen coefficient of the power of a wall-function, yielding the desired formula in terms of shadowed gradings.

For the entirety of this subsection, fix
a
,
b
,
k
, and
m
as in
Section

5.3
, and set
d_{1}\coloneqq d_{1,k}
and
d_{2}\coloneqq d_{2,k}
.

Recall that
\CG(p,q)
is the set of compatible gradings
\omega
on
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}([d_{1}]_{+},[d_{2}]_{+})
with
p=\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})
and
q=\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})
. We let
\CG_{+}^{t}(p,q)\subseteq\CG(p,q)
consist of the compatible gradings with
\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}}\cut\sh(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}}))=t
. Similarly, we let
\CG_{-}^{t}(p,q)\subseteq\CG(p,q)
consist of the gradings with
\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}}\cut\sh(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}}))=t
. Let

\CG^{t}(p,q)=\begin{cases}\CG_{+}^{t}(p,q)&\text{ if $bd_{1}\geq ad_{2}$},\\
\CG_{-}^{t}(p,q)&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

and let
|\CG^{t}(p,q)|=\sum_{\omega\in\CG^{t}(ka,kb)}\text{wt}(\omega)\in\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{M}]
. We show in the following lemma that
\CG^{0}(p,q)
is precisely the set of shadowed gradings in
\CG(p,q)
.

Lemma 5.23
.

One of
\CG^{0}_{\pm}(p,q)
is empty unless
p=q=0
. Precisely, when
(p,q)\neq(0,0)
,

(1)

\CG^{0}_{+}(p,q)=\varnothing
if
qd_{1}\leq pd_{2}
;

(2)

\CG^{0}_{-}(p,q)=\varnothing
if
qd_{1}\geq pd_{2}
.

Proof.

By symmetry, it is enough to prove the first case, where we assume
\frac{q}{p}\leq\frac{d_{2}}{d_{1}}
. Suppose such a compatible grading
\omega
exists. By assumption, we have
\frac{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}{\omega(\sh(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}}))}=\frac{q}{p}
. Hence there must be some horizontal edge
u
such that
\frac{\omega(u)}{\omega(\sh(u))}\leq\frac{q}{p}
. Choose
v
to be the unique vertical edge such that
|(\overrightarrow{uv})_{\mathbf{N}}|=\omega(u)
.

By the compatibility of
\omega
, we must have
|(\overrightarrow{uv})_{\mathbf{E}}|\geq\omega(\sh(u))+1
. However, by the modular definition of the maximal Dyck path (see, for example,
[
2
, Lemma 1.3]
), we then must have that
\omega(\sh(u))d_{2}<\omega(u)d_{1}
. However, this yields a contradiction since we have
\frac{\omega(u)}{\omega(\sh(u))}\leq\frac{q}{p}\leq\frac{d_{2}}{d_{1}}
.
∎

Fix an endpoint
Q=(Q_{1},Q_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{>0}
such that the quantity
(-m+ka)Q_{2}-(-n+kb)Q_{1}
is positive if
bd_{1}\leq d_{2}a
, and negative otherwise. Let
\BL(ka,kb)
denote the set of broken lines with initial exponent
(-d_{1},-d_{2})
and final exponent
(-d_{1}+ka,-d_{2}+kb)
that terminate at
Q
. Let
\BL^{t}(ka,kb)
denote the subset of broken lines in
\BL(ka,kb)
that bend at the positive
y
-axis with multiplicity
t
. Given a set
B
of broken lines, let
|B|\coloneqq\sum_{\beta\in B}c(\beta)
.

Lemma 5.24
.

For all
t\in\{0,1,\dots,kb\}
, we have

|\BL^{t}(ka,kb)|=|\CG^{t}(ka,kb)|\,.

Proof.

We handle the case when
bd_{1}\geq ad_{2}
, with the other case following analogously. We proceed by induction on the quantity
kb-t
. The base case
kb-t=0
is readily checked, as both quantities are simply a product of two binomial coefficients.

For the inductive step, we first note that the sets
\CG(ka,kb-t)
and
\BL(ka,kb-t)
can be refined as
\CG(ka,kb-t)=\bigcup_{s=0}^{kb-t}\CG^{s}(ka,kb-t)
and
\BL(ka,kb-t)=\bigcup_{s=0}^{kb-t}\BL^{s}(ka,kb-t)
. Since these unions are disjoint, we have

\displaystyle|\CG(ka,kb-t)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{kb-t}|\CG^{s}(ka,kb-t)|,

\displaystyle|\BL(ka,kb-t)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{kb-t}|\BL^{s}(ka,kb-t)|\,.

Applying
Theorem

4.1
yields

\sum_{s=0}^{kb-t}|\CG^{s}(ka,kb-t)|=\sum_{s=0}^{kb-t}|\BL^{s}(ka,kb-t)|\,.

Using the inductive hypothesis, we have
|\CG^{s}(ka,kb-t)|=|\BL^{s}(ka,kb-t)|
for
s>0
. Hence we can also conclude
|\CG^{0}(ka,kb-t)|=|\BL^{0}(ka,kb-t)|
. By separating out the contribution of the edges in
\mathcal{P}_{N}\cut\sh(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})
to the weight of each compatible grading, we see

|\CG^{t}(ka,kb)|=\pi_{d_{1}-ka,t}(p_{2,\bullet})|\CG^{0}(ka,kb-t)|\,.

We also have

|\BL^{t}(ka,kb)|=\pi_{d_{1}-ka,t}(p_{2,\bullet})|\BL^{0}(ka,kb-t)|\,,

which can be seen examining the preimage of the map taking each broken line in
\BL^{t}(ka,kb)
to a broken line in
\BL^{0}(ka,kb-t)
by forgetting the last bending. We can therefore conclude
|\BL^{t}(ka,kb)|=|\CG^{t}(ka,kb)|
, as desired.
∎

Proposition 5.25
.

We have
|\BL^{0}(ka,kb)|=\lambda^{(m)}(ka,kb)
.

Proof.

It follows directly from
Corollary

5.14
(2) and
Remark

5.12
(using our choice of
Q
) that
\BL^{0}(ka,kb)
consists of a single broken line. This broken line, which we denote by
\beta
, bends with multiplicity
k
at the wall of slope
\frac{b}{a}
with no other bendings. By hypothesis, we have
|(d_{1},d_{2})\cdot(-b,a)|=m
. Thus, the weight associated to the bending of
\beta
over
\mathfrak{d}
with multiplicity
k
is given by the coefficient of
x^{kb}y^{ka}
in
(f_{\mathfrak{d}})^{m}
, which is precisely
\lambda(ka,kb)
. Since
\beta
is the unique broken line in
\BL^{0}(ka,kb)
and
\beta
only bends once, we can conclude that
|\BL^{0}(ka,kb)|=|\beta|=\lambda^{(m)}(ka,kb)
, as desired.
∎

Remark 5.26
.

In the cluster algebra case, a tight grading has weight
1
if it takes values in
\{0,\ell_{1}\}
on the vertical edges and
\{0,\ell_{2}\}
on the horizontal edges, and weight
0
otherwise. If we restrict to considering only these gradings, then the total weight of gradings in
\CG^{t}(ka,kb)
is the same as its cardinality. Thus, in order for
|\CG^{t}(ka,kb)|
to be nonzero,
t
must be a multiple of
\ell_{1}
.

The first main theorem now readily follows.

Proof of
Theorem

5.17
.

By
Lemma

5.23
, the gradings in
\CG^{0}(ka,kb)
are precisely the shadowed gradings contributing to the formula in
Theorem

5.17
. Hence the main result follows directly by combining the equalities in
Lemma

5.24
and
Proposition

5.25
when
t=0
.
∎

5.5.
Another proof of
Theorem

5.17

We provide an alternative proof that does not rely on the results in
Section

4
.

We first prove some simple properties of shadowed gradings, allowing us to relate them to the the polynomials
d_{+}(p,q)
and
d_{-}(p,q)
.

Lemma 5.27
.

We have

\displaystyle|\CG(p,q)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{p}\pi_{d_{2}-q,s}(p_{1,\bullet})\left|\CG^{0}_{+}(p-s,q)\right|

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=0}^{q}\pi_{d_{1}-p,s}(p_{2,\bullet})\left|\CG^{0}_{-}(p,q-s)\right|\,.

Proof.

The first equality follows by viewing
s
as total value of
\omega
on the horizontal edges outside of
\sh(S_{2})
, so
|\CG(p,q)|=\sum_{s=0}^{p}|\CG^{s}_{+}(p-s,q)|
. Each summand
\pi_{d_{2}-q,s}(p_{1,\bullet})\left|\CG^{0}_{+}(p-s,q)\right|
on the right hand side is then equal to
|\CG^{s}_{+}(p,q)|
, where
\pi_{d_{2}-q,s}(p_{1,\bullet})
accounts for the weight contributed by the horizontal edges outside
\sh(S_{\mathbf{N}})
and
|\CG^{0}_{+}(p-s,q)|
accounts for the remaining weight of all vertical edges and the horizontal edges in
\sh(S_{\mathbf{N}})
. The second equality follows similarly, reversing the roles of vertical and horizontal edges.
∎

Proposition 5.28
.

Fix
(d_{1},d_{2})
. For any
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have

|\CG_{+}^{0}(p,q)|=d_{+}(p,q)\quad\text{and}\quad|\CG_{-}^{0}(p,q)|=d_{-}(p,q).

Proof.

Recall
d_{\pm}(p,q)
from
Definition

3.7
. We prove by induction on
(p,q)
. The base case is

|\CG_{+}^{0}(0,0)|=d_{+}(0,0)=1\quad\text{and}\quad|\CG_{-}^{0}(0,0)|=d_{-}(0,0)=1.

Then the result follows from
Lemma

5.27
,
Lemma

3.8
, and
|\CG(p,q)|=c(p,q)
from
Theorem

3.18
.
∎

Corollary 5.29
.

The polynomials
d_{\pm}(p,q)
satisfy

\sum_{s=0}^{p}\pi_{d_{2}-q,s}(p_{1,\bullet})d_{+}(p-s,q)=\sum_{s=0}^{q}\pi_{d_{1}-p,s}(p_{2,\bullet})d_{-}(p,q-s).

When
(p,q)\neq(0,0)
, we have

\displaystyle d_{+}(p,q)=0\quad\text{when}\quad d_{1}q\leq d_{2}p;

\displaystyle d_{-}(p,q)=0\quad\text{when}\quad d_{1}q\geq d_{2}p.

Proof.

This is a direct corollary of
Lemma

5.23
,
Lemma

5.27
, and
Proposition

5.28
.
∎

As a consequence of
Proposition

5.28
, we present the following sharpened recursive formula of
c(p,q)
, which generalizes
[
36
, Proposition 1.6]
and
[
51
, Proposition 2.13]
.

Proposition 5.30
.

Fix
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. The polynomials
c(p,q)
satisfy the following recursion

c(p,q)=\begin{cases}\sum_{k=1}^{p}-c(p-k,q)\varsigma_{d_{2}-q,k}(p_{1,\bullet})\quad&\text{if $d_{1}q\leq d_{2}p$;}\\
\sum_{k=1}^{q}-c(p,q-k)\varsigma_{d_{1}-p,k}(p_{2,\bullet})\quad&\text{if $d_{1}q\geq d_{2}p$.}\end{cases}

Proof.

With the emphasis on
d_{\pm}(p,q)
, the stated recursion of
c(p,q)
follows immediately from
Corollary

5.29
and
Definition

3.7
. We also see that
c(p,q)
is attained by one of two polynomials in
Definition

3.5
.
∎

Fix
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Let
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

\BL^{t}_{+}(p,q)=\BL^{t}_{+}(d_{1},d_{2},p,q)\quad\text{(resp. $\BL^{t}_{-}(p,q)=\BL^{t}_{-}(d_{1},d_{2},p,q)$)}

denote the set of broken lines with initial exponent
(-d_{1},-d_{2})
, final exponent
(-d_{1}+p,-d_{2}+q)
, and endpoint
Q_{+}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}
(resp.
Q_{-}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}
) chosen close enough to the positive
x
-axis (resp. positive
y
-axis) such that the bend at the positive
x
-axis (resp. positive
y
-axis) corresponds to the monomial
x^{t}
(resp.
y^{t}
).

Lemma 5.31
.

We have

\displaystyle|\BL_{+}^{t}(p,q)|

\displaystyle=\pi_{d_{2}-q,t}(p_{1,\bullet})|\BL_{+}^{0}(p-t,q)|,

\displaystyle|\BL_{-}^{t}(p,q)|

\displaystyle=\pi_{d_{1}-p,t}(p_{2,\bullet})|\BL_{-}^{0}(p,q-t)|.

Proof.

When it is non-empty, erasing the last bending of any broken line in
\BL_{+}^{t}(p,q)
(resp.
\BL_{-}^{t}(p,q)
) induces a surjection to
\BL_{+}^{0}(p-t,q)
(resp.
\BL_{-}^{0}(p,q-t)
). The computation of the weights follows from the initial wall-functions on the axes.

If
\BL_{+}^{t}(p,q)
is empty, then either
\pi_{d_{2}-q,t}(p_{1,\bullet})=0
or
\BL_{+}^{0}(p-t,q)
is empty. The same is true for the
-
case.
∎

Lemma 5.32
.

One of
\BL^{0}_{\pm}(p,q)
is empty unless
p=q=0
. Precisely, when
(p,q)\neq(0,0)
,

(1)

\BL^{0}_{+}(p,q)=\varnothing
if
qd_{1}\leq d_{2}p
,

(2)

\BL^{0}_{-}(p,q)=\varnothing
if
qd_{1}\geq d_{2}p
.

Proof.

Since the broken lines in consideration do not bend at positive axes, the slopes of their linearity domains can only continue to increase or decrease from the start. Therefore, the order of the terminal slope and the initial slope determines which one of the two sets
\BL_{+}^{0}(p,q)
and
\BL_{-}^{0}(p,q)
is empty, unless the broken lines do not bend at all, which is the case
p=q=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.33
.

\sum_{t=0}^{p}\pi_{d_{2}-q,t}(p_{1,\bullet})|\BL^{0}_{+}(p-t,q)|=\sum_{t=0}^{q}\pi_{d_{1}-p,t}(p_{2,\bullet})|\BL^{0}_{-}(p,q-t)|.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.31
, it is equivalent to show

\sum_{t=0}^{q}|\BL_{+}^{t}(p,q)|=\sum_{t=0}^{p}|\BL_{-}^{t}(p,q)|.

The left-hand side computes the coefficient of
x^{p-d_{1}}y^{q-d_{2}}
in the theta function
\vartheta_{Q_{+},(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
. The right-hand side does the same for
\vartheta_{Q_{-},(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
. By
Corollary

2.31
, they equal.
∎

Proposition 5.34
.

Fix
(d_{1},d_{2})
. For any
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have

|\BL_{+}^{0}(p,q)|=d_{+}(p,q)\quad\text{and}\quad|\BL_{-}^{0}(p,q)|=d_{-}(p,q).

Proof.

Compare the recursion of
|\BL_{\pm}^{0}(p,q)|
determined by
Lemma

5.32
and
Lemma

5.33
with the recursion of
d_{\pm}(p,q)
given in
Corollary

5.29
.
∎

Let
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy the one-bending property
Definition

5.9
with
d_{1}\geq ka
and
d_{2}\geq kb
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.17
.

We first deal with the case where
d_{1}>ka
and
d_{2}>kb
. Suppose that

m=d_{1}b-d_{2}a>0.

By the one-bending assumption, every broken line
\beta
in
\BL_{+}^{0}(ka,kb)
is of the same bend-type, that is, the only bend is at the ray
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)
with multiplicity
m
. Notice that
\beta
indeed reaches the first quadrant. The sum of weight monomials of every
\beta
in
\BL_{+}^{0}(ka,kb)
gives the coefficient
\lambda^{(m)}(ka,kb)
of
x^{ka}y^{kb}
in
(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)})^{m}
. Hence by
Proposition

5.34

\lambda^{(m)}(ka,kb)=|\BL_{+}^{0}(ka,kb)|=d_{+}(ka,kb).

The situation where
m=d_{2}a-d_{1}b>0
is similar.

Suppose that
d_{1}=ka
or
d_{2}=kb
. A one-bending broken line
\beta
at
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)
with initial exponent
(-d_{1},-d_{2})
and final exponent
(ka-d_{1},kb-d_{2})
does not reach the first quadrant. So it does not belong to
\BL_{\pm}^{0}(ka,kb)
. Suppose
d_{2}=kb
. Then again

m=d_{1}b-d_{2}a>0.

Consider the theta function
\vartheta_{Q,(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
for
Q
chosen generally in the fourth quadrant. By the one-bending property, the sum of the weight of the aforementioned one-bending
\beta
with endpoint
Q
is the coefficient
\lambda^{(m)}(ka,kb)
. Meanwhile, it also computes the coefficient of
x^{ka-d_{1}}y^{kb-d_{2}}=x^{ka-d_{1}}
in
\vartheta_{Q,(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
. By
Corollary

2.31
,
\lambda^{(m)}(ka,kb)
equals the coefficient of
x^{ka-d_{1}}
in
\vartheta_{Q_{+},(-d_{1},-d_{2})}
. The latter actually equals

|\BL_{+}^{0}(ka,kb)|=d_{+}(ka,kb)

because any broken line contributes to
x^{ka-d_{1}}
must not bend at positive
x
-axis, thus belonging to
\BL_{+}^{0}(ka,kb)
. The situation when
d_{1}=ka
is similar.

Summarizing we have shown

\lambda^{(m)}(ka,kb)=\begin{cases}d_{+}(ka,kb)\quad&\text{if}\ d_{1}b-d_{2}a>0,\\
d_{-}(ka,kb)\quad&\text{if}\ d_{1}b-d_{2}a<0.\end{cases}

Finally the polynomials
d_{\pm}(ka,kb)
are related to weighted counts of shadowed gradings by
Lemma

5.23
and
Proposition

5.28
.
∎

5.6.
A universal scattering diagram

Recall the monoid
\mathscr{P}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
in
Section

2.2
. For
\ell_{1}^{\prime}\geq\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}^{\prime}\geq\ell_{2}
, consider the algebra map

\varphi\colon\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{\ell_{1}^{\prime},\ell_{2}^{\prime}}]}\rightarrow\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}]}

that evaluates
p_{1,i}=p_{2,j}=0
for
i>\ell_{1}
and
j>\ell_{2}
.

For any
\ell\geq 1
, denote

P_{1}^{(\ell)}=1+p_{1,1}x+\dots+p_{1,\ell}x^{\ell}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{2}^{(\ell)}=1+p_{2,1}y+\dots+p_{2,\ell}y^{\ell}.

By the functoriality (
2.11
), we have

\varphi\left(\Scat\left(P_{1}^{(\ell_{1}^{\prime})},P_{2}^{(\ell_{2}^{\prime})}\right)\right)\equiv\Scat\left(P_{1}^{(\ell_{1})},P_{2}^{(\ell_{2})}\right).

The scattering diagrams associated to all
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
thus have a (projective) limit, viewed as a universal scattering diagram. By the tight grading formula
Theorem

5.17
, the limiting wall-function on
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)
is given by

(5.35)

\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka}\sum_{w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb}\lambda_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb},

where
\lambda_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})
is the number of tight gradings of weight polynomial
p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
. To recover the wall-function in
\Scat(P_{1}^{(\ell_{1})},P_{2}^{(\ell_{2})})
, simply let the variables
p_{1,i}
,
i>\ell_{1}
and
p_{2,j}
,
j>\ell_{2}
be zero. This amounts to considering the subset of tight gradings
\omega
whose value on horizontal edges is bounded above by
\ell_{2}
and on vertical edges is bounded above by
\ell_{1}
.

We note that the limiting structure is more general than the scattering diagrams in
Definition

2.3
as the monoid in the limit is not finitely generated. However, one can nonetheless assign degree
k
to the variable
p_{i,k}
and have a filtration of ideals
\mathfrak{m}^{(k)}\subseteq\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]
generated by elements of degree at least
k
. Then we can still define scattering diagrams with finitely many walls over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{(k)}
and a limiting object of those is regarded as a scattering diagram over the completion
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}=\lim\limits_{\longleftarrow}\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{(k)}
just as in
Definition

2.3
. Wall-crossing automorphisms and consistency can be discussed analogously.

6.
Applications of the tight and shadowed grading formulas

Since rank-2 scattering diagrams are known to encode interesting invariants
[
29
,
28
,
47
,
49
]
, we explore these links in terms of tight and shadowed gradings in this section. We give formulas for computing Euler characteristics of quiver moduli spaces in
Section

6.1
and relative Gromov–Witten invariants on toric surfaces in
Section

6.2
.

6.1.
Euler characteristics of quiver moduli

We review a result of Reineke and Weist
[
49
]
expressing wall-function coefficients in terms of Euler characteristics of certain moduli spaces of quiver representations. Then we establish the link to tight gradings in
Corollary

6.3
.

In
[
49
]
(as well as
[
29
,
28
]
), the coefficients in the initial wall-functions differ with those in
Section

2.2
. Precisely, we consider polynomials

(6.1)

P_{1}=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}}(1+s_{i}x)\quad\text{and}\quad P_{2}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}(1+t_{j}y)

and the scattering diagram
\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
.
This setup can be accommodated in the framework of
Section

2.1
and
Section

2.2
by choosing the monoid

\mathscr{P}=M\oplus\mathbb{M}_{1}\oplus\mathbb{M}_{2}
where
\mathbb{M}_{1}\cong\mathbb{N}^{\ell_{1}}
is the (multiplicative) monoid of monomials in
\{s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell_{1}}\}
and
\mathbb{M}_{2}\cong\mathbb{N}^{\ell_{2}}
is the monoid of monomials in
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell_{2}}\}
. The coefficients
p_{1,k}
and
p_{2,k}
used in
Section

2.2
are elementary symmetric polynomials respectively in
s_{i}
and in
t_{j}
by comparing (
6.1
) with (
2.13
).

Let
K=K(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
be the complete bipartite quiver with
\ell_{1}
sinks
\{s_{1},\dots,s_{\ell_{1}}\}
and
\ell_{2}
sources
\{t_{1},\dots,t_{\ell_{2}}\}
. For example,
K(3,2)
is depicted in
Figure

6
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{t_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{s_{3}}}

Figure 6.
The quiver
K(3,2)

Let
\mathbf{P}_{1}=(d_{1,i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell_{1}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}=(d_{2,j})_{1\leq j\leq\ell_{2}}
be tuples of nonnegative integers. They will be referred to as
ordered partitions
once we fix the sums
|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=\sum d_{1,i}
and
|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=\sum d_{2,j}
. As the notation suggests,
\mathbf{P}_{1}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}
represent two monomials

s^{\mathbf{P}_{1}}\coloneqq\prod_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}}s_{i}^{d_{1,i}}\in\mathbb{M}_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad t^{\mathbf{P}_{2}}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}t_{j}^{d_{2,j}}\in\mathbb{M}_{2},

Meanwhile they also represent a dimension vector
(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})
on the quiver
K
such that the dimension on the sink
s_{i}
is
d_{1,i}
and on the source
t_{j}
is
d_{2,j}
.

Let
V_{i}=\mathbb{C}^{d_{1,i}}
for
i=1,\dots,\ell_{1}
and
W_{j}=\mathbb{C}^{d_{2,j}}
for
j=1,\dots\ell_{2}
. Consider the following affine space of representations of
K
(in
\mathbb{C}
-vector spaces)

\operatorname{rep}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})\coloneqq\{(f_{i,j})_{1\leq i\leq\ell_{1},1\leq j\leq\ell_{2}}\mid f_{i,j}\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(W_{j},V_{i})\}.

Let
G=\prod_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}}\operatorname{GL}(V_{i})\times\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}\operatorname{GL}(W_{j})
. Then
G
acts on the two spaces

\operatorname{rep}^{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})\coloneqq\operatorname{rep}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})\times\prod_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}}V_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{rep}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})\coloneqq\operatorname{rep}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})\times\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}W_{j}

respectively by

(g_{i},h_{j})\cdot(f_{i,j},v_{i})=(g_{i}f_{i,j}h_{j}^{-1},g_{i}v_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad(g_{i},h_{j})\cdot(f_{i,j},w_{j})=(g_{i}f_{i,j}h_{j}^{-1},h_{j}w_{j}).

The superscripts
f
and
b
stand for respectively
front
and
back framing
, where the vectors
v_{i}
and
w_{j}
are called
framing vectors
. Elements in
\operatorname{rep}^{\bullet}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})
are called
framed representations
.

We further consider back or front framed representations that are
stable
in the following sense. We call
(f_{i,j},w_{j})\in\operatorname{rep}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})
(resp.
(f_{i,j},v_{i})\in\operatorname{rep}^{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})
)
stable
if for every tuple
U_{j}\subseteq W_{j}
of subspaces, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}\dim f_{i,j}(U_{j})\geq\frac{|\mathbf{P}_{1}|}{|\mathbf{P}_{2}|}\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}U_{j},

with strictly inequality if
w_{j}\in U_{j}
(resp.
v_{i}\in\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}f_{i,j}(U_{j})
) for every
j
(resp.
i
). It is clear that framed stable representations are closed under the action of
G
.

By
[
49
, Corollary 5.4]
(based on the construction of
[
15
]
), there are smooth irreducible varieties
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})
and
\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})
as moduli spaces parametrizing respectively back and front framed stable representations up to the action of
G
. We use
\chi(\cdot)
to denote the Euler characteristic of a complex variety in analytic topology.

Theorem 6.2

(
[
49
, Theorem 6.1]
)
.

For any coprime
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}\right)^{b}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=ka}\sum_{|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=kb}\chi(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2}))s^{\mathbf{P}_{1}}t^{\mathbf{P}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb},

\displaystyle\left(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}\right)^{a}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=ka}\sum_{|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=kb}\chi(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2}))s^{\mathbf{P}_{1}}t^{\mathbf{P}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb}.

For coprime
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
k\geq 1
, consider
(d_{1},d_{2})=(kab+1,kb^{2})
. By
Corollary

5.14
(2), the vectors
(d_{1},d_{2})
and
(ka,kb)
satisfy the one-bending property (
Definition

5.9
). Notice that
a\cdot kb^{2}-b\cdot(kab+1)=-b
.
Recall from
Section

5.3
that by
Theorem

5.17
,
\lambda^{(b)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})
is the number of shadowed gradings on
\mathcal{P}(kab+1,kb^{2})
of weight monomial
p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
where
w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka
and
w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb
. Similarly,
\lambda^{(a)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})
is the number of shadowed gradings on
\mathcal{P}(ka^{2},kab+1)
of weight monomial
p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
.

We now write down explicit formulas of Euler characteristics of framed stable quiver moduli in terms of counts of shadowed gradings.

Corollary 6.3
.

For any coprime
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, ordered partitions
|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=ka
,
|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=kb
, we have

\displaystyle\chi(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{b}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka}\sum_{w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb}\lambda^{(b)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}_{\mathbf{P}_{1}}\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}_{\mathbf{P}_{2}},

\displaystyle\chi(\mathcal{M}^{\mathrm{f}}(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka}\sum_{w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb}\lambda^{(a)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}_{\mathbf{P}_{1}}\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}_{\mathbf{P}_{2}},

where
\mu_{\mathbf{P}_{i}}^{\mathbf{Q}_{i}}
for
i=1,2
are nonnegative integers defined by the expansions

(6.4)

p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}=\sum_{|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})}\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}_{\mathbf{P}_{1}}s^{\mathbf{P}_{1}}\quad\text{and}\quad p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}=\sum_{|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})}\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}_{\mathbf{P}_{2}}t^{\mathbf{P}_{2}}.

Proof.

By
Theorem

5.17
, we can express
\left(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}\right)^{b}
as

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka}\sum_{w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb}\lambda^{(b)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb}.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=ka\\
|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=kb\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka\\
w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb\end{subarray}}\lambda^{(b)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}_{\mathbf{P}_{1}}\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}_{\mathbf{P}_{2}}\right)s^{\mathbf{P}_{1}}t^{\mathbf{P}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb}.

Comparing the above coefficients with the ones in
Theorem

6.2
we have the desired equation. The other case is similar.
∎

6.2.
Formula for relative Gromov–Witten invariants

In
[
29
]
, Gross, Pandharipande, and Siebert expressed the wall-function
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}
in terms of relative Gromov–Witten invariants on toric surfaces. We briefly recall their results and give a combinatorial formula for these invariants in
Theorem

6.8
.

For
(a,b)
positive and coprime, let
X=X_{a,b}
denote the toric surface over
\mathbb{C}
associated to the complete fan generated by rays
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(0,1)
,
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}(1,0)
, and
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)
. The three rays correspond to three toric divisors in
X
which we denote as
D_{1}
,
D_{2}
, and
D_{\mathrm{out}}
.

Fix two positive integers
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
. As in
Section

6.1
, let
\mathbf{P}_{1}=(d_{1,i})_{1\leq i\leq\ell_{1}}
and
\mathbf{P}_{2}=(d_{2,j})_{1\leq j\leq\ell_{2}}
be ordered partitions such that

|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}}d_{1,i}=ka\quad\text{and}\quad|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}d_{2,j}=kb

for some
k\geq 1
.
Consider
\nu\colon X[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]\rightarrow X
the blow-up of
X
along
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
general points respectively of
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
away from the
0
-dimensional toric stratum
S
(i.e., three toric fixed points). Denote

X^{\circ}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]=X[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]\cut\nu^{-1}(S)\quad\text{and}\quad D_{\mathrm{out}}^{\circ}=D_{\mathrm{out}}\cut\nu^{-1}(S)\,.

Let
\beta\in H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
be the homology class determined by the intersection numbers

\beta\cdot D_{1}=ak,\quad\beta\cdot D_{2}=bk,\quad\beta\cdot D_{\mathrm{out}}=k.

Define the homology class

\beta[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]=\nu^{*}(\beta)-\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{1}}d_{1,i}[E_{1,i}]-\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{2}}d_{2,j}[E_{2,j}]\in H_{2}(X[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})],\mathbb{Z}).

where
E_{1,i}
and
E_{2,j}
are the exceptional divisors over the blown-up points on
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
.

There is a moduli space
\mathfrak{M}
of relative stable maps of genus 0 curves into
X^{\circ}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]
representing the homology class
\beta[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]
with full tangency order
k
at an unspecified point in
D_{\mathrm{out}}^{\circ}
. The moduli space
\mathfrak{M}
is proper of virtual dimension zero and thus the integration over the virtual fundamental class defines a Gromov–Witten type invariant

N_{a,b}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]\coloneqq\int_{\mathfrak{M}^{\mathrm{vir}}}1\in\mathbb{Q}.

We refer the reader to
[
29
, Section 4]
for more details on the construction of the moduli space and the definition of
N_{a,b}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]
.

Theorem 6.5

(
[
29
, Theorem 5.4]
)
.

For any positive coprime
(a,b)
, we have

(6.6)

\log f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=ka\\
|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=kb\end{subarray}}kN_{a,b}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]s^{\mathbf{P}_{1}}t^{\mathbf{P}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb}.

Combined the above theorem of Gross–Pandharipande–Siebert with the tight grading formula
Theorem

5.17
, we have the following direct corollary.

Corollary 6.7
.

We have for any
m\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=ka\\
|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=kb\end{subarray}}kN_{a,b}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]s^{\mathbf{P}_{1}}t^{\mathbf{P}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb}

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{m}\log\left(1+\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka\\
w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb\end{subarray}}\lambda^{(m)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb}\right)\,.

Next we present a formula for each single
N_{a,b}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]
that is linear on counts of compatible gradings without involving taking logarithms.

Theorem 6.8
.

For any
(a,b)
coprime,
k\geq 1
and ordered partitions
|\mathbf{P}_{1}|=ka
and
|\mathbf{P}_{2}|=kb
, we have

N_{a,b}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{i}\binom{k}{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka\\
w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb\end{subarray}}\lambda^{(i)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})\mu_{\mathbf{P}_{1}}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}\mu_{\mathbf{P}_{2}}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}.

Proof.

Denote
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}=1+Y
. In the infinite series

\log f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}=Y-\frac{1}{2}Y^{2}+\dots+\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}Y^{k}+\cdots

every term with
x^{ka}y^{kb}
is contained in the part

(6.9)

Y-\frac{1}{2}Y^{2}+\dots+\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}Y^{k}=c_{0,k}+c_{1,k}(1+Y)+c_{2,k}(1+Y)^{2}+\dots+c_{k,k}(1+Y)^{k}.

By
Theorem

5.17
, combining the terms with
x^{ka}y^{kb}
we obtain

\sum_{i=1}^{k}c_{i,k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=ka\\
w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=kb\end{subarray}}\lambda^{(i)}_{a,b}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})p_{1}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}p_{2}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}x^{ka}y^{kb}

where each
\lambda_{a,b}^{(i)}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})
counts shadowed gradings. Expand this sum using the expression (
6.4
) of
p_{i}^{\mathbf{Q}_{i}}
in terms of
s^{\mathbf{P}_{1}}
and
t^{\mathbf{P}_{2}}
and then compare with the right-hand side of (
6.6
). We see that
N_{(a,b)}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]
is expressed as desired modulo that
c_{i,k}=\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{i}\binom{k}{i}
. To compute these numbers, we write each term on the left-hand side of (
6.9
) as

\frac{(-1)^{j-1}}{j}Y^{j}=\frac{(-1)^{j-1}}{j}(1+Y-1)^{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{j}\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{j}\binom{j}{i}(1+Y)^{i}.

Then we have

c_{i,k}=\sum_{j=i}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{j}\binom{j}{i}=\sum_{j=i}^{k}\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{i}\binom{j-1}{i-1}=\frac{(-1)^{i-1}}{i}\binom{k}{i}.

∎

In particular, when
k=1
, we have

N_{a,b}[(\mathbf{P}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{2})]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w(\mathbf{Q}_{1})=a\\
w(\mathbf{Q}_{2})=b\end{subarray}}\lambda_{a,b}^{(1)}(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}_{\mathbf{P}_{1}}\mu^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}_{\mathbf{P}_{2}},

where
\lambda_{a,b}^{(1)}
are honest coefficients of
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(a,b)}
.

Example 6.10
.

Let
\ell_{1}=3
and
\ell_{2}=2
. Consider the ordered partitions

\mathbf{P}_{1}=3+0+0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{P}_{2}=3+0.

They correspond respectively to the monomials
s_{1}^{3}
and
t_{1}^{3}
. Only when
\mathbf{Q}_{1}=p_{1,1}^{3}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{2}=p_{2,1}^{3}
, we have non-zero coefficients

\mu_{\mathbf{P}_{1}}^{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}=1\quad\text{and}\quad\mu_{\mathbf{P}_{2}}^{\mathbf{Q}_{2}}=1.

Then the formula in
Theorem

6.8
gives

N_{1,1}[(3+0,3+0+0)]=\frac{1}{3}\left(3\lambda^{(1)}_{1,1}(p_{1,1}^{3},p_{2,1}^{3})-\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{1,1}^{(2)}(p_{1,1}^{3},p_{2,1}^{3})+\frac{1}{3}\lambda^{(3)}_{1,1}(p_{1,1}^{3},p_{2,1}^{3})\right).

Using compatible gradings, we can see that only the third term is non-trivial

\lambda^{(3)}_{1,1}(p_{1,1}^{3},p_{2,1}^{3})=1.

For this particular grading (depicted in
Figure

7
), one can choose
(d_{1},d_{2})=(12,9)
, which with
(ka,kb)=(3,3)
satisfy the one-bending property by
Corollary

5.14
. Notice
d_{1}b-d_{2}a=3
.
Therefore, we have computed
N_{1,1}[(3+0,3+0+0)]=1/9
, which coincides with the multiple cover formula
[
5
]
and
[
29
, Proposition 5.2]
.

Figure 7.
A shadowed grading of weight monomial
p_{1,1}^{3}p_{2,1}^{3}

7.
Generalized cluster structures

7.1.
Generalized cluster structures

Fomin and Zelevinsky’s cluster algebra
[
18
]
has been generalized by Chekhov and Shapiro
[
9
]
to incorporate polynomial exchange relations (as opposed to binomials). We review the basic notions here.

Let
\mathbb{P}
be a torsion-free (multiplicative) abelian group, known as the
coefficient group

[
18
,
1
]
. Let
n
be a positive integer and
I
denote the index set
\{1,\dots,n\}
. We take a field
\mathcal{F}
isomorphic to the field of rational functions in
n
independent variables over
\operatorname{Frac}(\mathbb{ZP})
, the fraction field of
\mathbb{ZP}
the group ring of
\mathbb{P}
.

Definition 7.1
.

A
seed
in
\mathcal{F}
is a triple
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},B)
, where

•

the
cluster

\mathbf{x}=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
is an algebraically independent subset that generates
\mathcal{F}
over
\operatorname{Frac}(\mathbb{ZP})
;

•

the
exchange matrix

B=(b_{i,j})
is a skew-symmetrizable
n\times n
integer matrix;

•

the
coefficient tuples

\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{p}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{p}_{n})
consist of, for each
i\in I
, an
(\ell_{i}+1)
-tuple
\mathbf{p}_{i}=(p_{i,0},\dots,p_{i,\ell_{i}})
of elements in
\mathbb{P}
for
\ell_{i}=\gcd(b_{j,i}\mid j\in I)
.

For the above data, we refer to any
x_{i}\in\mathbf{x}
a
cluster variable
and a monomial in
\mathbf{x}
a
cluster monomial
. For
k\in I
, we define
x_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
by the
exchange relation

(7.2)

x_{k}x_{k}^{\prime}=P_{k}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{s=0}^{\ell_{k}}p_{k,s}\prod_{b_{i,k}>0}x_{i}^{sb_{i,k}/\ell_{k}}\prod_{b_{j,k}<0}x_{j}^{(s-\ell_{k})b_{j,k}/\ell_{k}}.

The set
\mathbf{x}-\{x_{k}\}\cup\{x_{k}^{\prime}\}
is clearly again an algebraically independent generating set for
\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 7.3
.

We say that a seed
\Sigma^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{p}^{\prime},B^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{F}
is obtained from
\Sigma
by a
seed mutation
in direction
k\in I
if

•

\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=\mathbf{x}-\{x_{k}\}\cup\{x_{k}^{\prime}\}
where
x_{k}^{\prime}
is defined as in (
7.2
);

•

the tuples
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{i}=(p^{\prime}_{i,0},\dots,p^{\prime}_{i,\ell_{i}})
satisfy when
i=k

p^{\prime}_{k,s}=p_{k,\ell_{k}-s},\quad\text{for $s=0,\dots,\ell_{k}$}

and when
i\neq k
, for
s=0,\dots,\ell_{i}

(7.4)

\frac{p^{\prime}_{i,s}}{p^{\prime}_{i,0}}=\begin{cases}p_{k,\ell_{k}}^{sb_{k,i}/\ell_{i}}\frac{p_{i,s}}{p_{i,0}}\quad&\text{if $b_{k,i}\geq 0$},\\
p_{k,0}^{sb_{k,i}/\ell_{i}}\frac{p_{i,s}}{p_{i,0}}\quad&\text{if $b_{k,i}\leq 0$};\end{cases}

•

B^{\prime}=(b_{i,j}^{\prime})
is the
Fomin–Zelevinsky matrix mutation
of
B
in direction
k
(denoted as
B^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(B)
) given by

(7.5)

b_{i,j}^{\prime}=\begin{cases}-b_{i,j}\quad&\text{if $i=k$ or $j=k$},\\
b_{i,j}+[-b_{i,k}]_{+}b_{k,j}+b_{i,k}[b_{k,j}]_{+}\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

It is straightforward to check that a seed mutation is involutive, that is, in the situation of
Definition

7.3
, the seed
\Sigma
is also obtained from
\Sigma^{\prime}
by a seed mutation in direction
k
. It is worth mentioning that a seed mutation is non-deterministic. For example one sees in (
7.4
) that uniformly multiplying an element in
\mathbb{P}
to every
p^{\prime}_{i;s}
,
s=0,\dots,\ell_{i}
does not affect the equality.

Let
\mathbb{T}_{n}
be the
n
-regular (infinite) tree such that the
n
edges incident to any vertex are distinctively labeled by
\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Definition 7.6
.

A
generalized cluster structure
in
\mathcal{F}
is an association of seeds

v\mapsto\Sigma_{v},\quad v\in\mathbb{T}_{n},

such that for any
k
-labeled edge
v\frac{k}{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}v^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
, the seeds
\Sigma_{v}
and
\Sigma_{v^{\prime}}
are obtained from each other by a seed mutation in direction
k
. When
\ell_{i}=1
for every
i\in I
, this is what we refer to as Fomin–Zelevinsky’s
ordinary cluster structure

[
18
,
1
]
.

For a seed
\Sigma_{v}=(\mathbf{x}_{v},\mathbf{p}_{v},B_{v})
associated with
v\in\mathbb{T}_{v}
, we often use the following notations to denote the gadgets in
Definition

7.1
:

(7.7)

\mathbf{x}_{v}=\{x_{1;v},\dots,x_{n;v}\},\quad\mathbf{p}_{v}=(\mathbf{p}_{i;v})_{i=1}^{n},\quad\mathbf{p}_{i;v}=(p_{i,s}^{v})_{s=0}^{\ell_{i}},\quad B_{v}=(b^{v}_{i,j}).

The first property one would hope a generalized cluster structure to inherit from that of ordinary cluster structures is the Laurent phenomenon
[
18
, Theorem 3.1]
. This is indeed the case.

Theorem 7.8

(
[
9
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

In a generalized cluster structure, any cluster variable is expressed in terms of any given cluster as a Laurent polynomial with coefficients in
\mathbb{ZP}
, that is, for any
v
and
v^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
and any
i\in I
we have

x_{i;v^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{ZP}[x_{1;v}^{\pm 1},\dots,x_{n;v}^{\pm 1}].

The following is a main result of this paper, the positivity of Laurent phenomenon. Its proof will be given in
Section

8.3
.

Theorem 7.9
.

Any cluster variable, when expressed as a Laurent polynomial in any given cluster, has coefficients in
\mathbb{NP}
, the subset in
\mathbb{ZP}
of linear combinations in
\mathbb{P}
with nonnegative integer coefficients.

Remark 7.10
.

In the case of ordinary cluster structures, the positivity was conjectured originally by Fomin and Zelevinsky in
[
18
]
, which was first proven by Lee and Schiffler
[
38
]
when
B
is skew-symmetric and then by Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich
[
27
]
when
B
is in general skew-symmetrizable. A less general version of
Theorem

7.9
was conjectured by Chekhov and Shapiro
[
9
, Section 5]
where the coefficient tuples are required to be reciprocal.

7.2.
Frozen directions and coefficients

In this subsection, every cluster structure is by default generalized. The cluster structure introduced so far can be slightly upgraded by introducing
frozen directions
of mutations. This amounts to choosing a subset
I_{\mathrm{uf}}\subseteq I
for
unfrozen directions
and forbidding mutations in
frozen directions

I_{\mathrm{fr}}=I\setminus I_{\mathrm{uf}}
. By permuting the indices, we can always assume

I_{\mathrm{uf}}=\{1,\dots,|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|\}\subseteq I.

So a cluster structure with frozen directions will be defined on the tree
\mathbb{T}_{|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|}
. Only the first
|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|
columns of the exchange matrices will be relevant in exchange relations (
7.2
) and matrix mutations (
7.5
).

Since no mutation is performed in frozen directions, all clusters share the same set of
frozen cluster variables

\{x_{i}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}\}.

Notice that in an exchange polynomial
P_{k}(\mathbf{x})
we can single out a monomial in frozen variables, combine it with
p_{k,s}
, and treat them together as a coefficient. The new coefficients

p_{k,s}\prod_{\{i\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}\mid b_{i,k}>0\}}x_{i}^{sb_{i,k}/\ell_{k}}\prod_{\{j\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}\mid b_{j,k}<0\}}x_{i}^{(s-\ell_{k})b_{j,k}/\ell_{k}}

follow the same mutation rule in
Definition

7.3
. Hence a cluster structure with frozen directions can be regarded as a special case of
Definition

7.6
with the square exchange matrices
(b_{i,j})_{i,j\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}}
. One nuance is to invert frozen variables so that the new coefficients naturally live in the product of
\mathbb{P}
and the (multiplicative) group of all Laurent monomials in frozen variables.

Remark 7.11
.

Starting with a cluster structure without frozen directions, we can add frozen directions by extending a square exchange matrix
B
by adding rows below it, obtaining a rectangular exchange matrix
\widetilde{B}
. This construction will be useful as later the scattering diagram technique behaves particularly well when
\widetilde{B}
is of full rank, which can always be achieved by extension.

We now discuss how much that a seed mutation is non-deterministic on coefficients influences cluster variables following Fraser
[
21
, Section 2.4]
. For a seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},B)
(with possible frozen directions), define for
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
,
s=1,\dots,\ell_{i}
, monomials

(7.12)

\hat{y}_{i,s}\coloneqq\frac{p_{i,s}}{p_{i,0}}\prod_{j\in I}x_{j}^{sb_{j,i}/\ell_{i}}.

Proposition 7.13

(
[
21
, Lemma 2.10]
)
.

If for two cluster structures

v\mapsto\Sigma_{v}\quad\text{and}\quad v\mapsto\Sigma_{v}^{\prime},\quad v\in\mathbb{T}_{|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|},

there is some vertex
v_{0}
such that
\Sigma_{v_{0}}\sim\Sigma^{\prime}_{v_{0}}
meaning

\frac{x_{i;v_{0}}}{x_{i;v_{0}}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{P}
for any
i\in I
and
\hat{y}_{i,s;v_{0}}=\hat{y}_{i,s;v_{0}}^{\prime}
for any
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
and
1\leq s\leq\ell_{i}
,

then we have
\Sigma_{v}\sim\Sigma_{v}^{\prime}
for any
v\in\mathbb{T}_{|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|}
.

Definition 7.14
.

Given a (generalized) cluster structure, the associated
(generalized) cluster algebra

\mathcal{A}
is the
\mathbb{ZP}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{F}
generated by all cluster variables

\bigcup_{v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}\mathbf{x}_{v}=\{x_{i;v}\mid i\in I,v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}\}.

The
(generalized) upper cluster algebra

\mathcal{U}
is defined to be the intersection

\bigcap_{v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}}\mathbb{ZP}[x_{i;v}^{\pm 1}\mid i\in I].

It follows from
Proposition

7.13
that in any cluster structure where
\Sigma
is associated to a given vertex
v_{0}
, any cluster variable in any seed is well-defined up to a factor in
\mathbb{P}
. Therefore we shall use the notations
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(\Sigma)
and
\mathcal{U}=\mathcal{U}(\Sigma)
for any seed
\Sigma
in the cluster structure since
\Sigma
is sufficient to determine both
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{U}
. By
Theorem

7.8
,
\mathcal{A}
is contained in
\mathcal{U}
.

Remark 7.15
.

Another manipulation on a seed
\Sigma
that does not change
\mathcal{A}(\Sigma)
is to scale the coefficient tuple
\mathbf{p}_{i}=(p_{i,0},\dots,p_{i,\ell_{i}})
by some uniform factor
p\in\mathbb{P}
. It follows from
Proposition

7.13
that any cluster variable is again well-defined up to a factor in
\mathbb{P}
. Thus to address the positivity in
Theorem

7.9
, we can always assume that there is some seed
\Sigma
where every
p_{i,0}=1
for
i\in I
. Moreover, we can take the free abelian group
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
generated by symbols

\{p_{i,s}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}},1\leq s\leq\ell_{i}\},

with the natural group homomorphism
\psi
to
\mathbb{P}
suggested by the notation. Cluster variables generated in the new cluster structure are subtraction-free rational expressions in
|I|
variables with coefficients in
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
. Then
\psi
induces a map that sends a cluster variable in the new cluster structure to the corresponding cluster variable in the old cluster structure. Therefore once the positivity of Laurent phenomenon is established for
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
, it also holds for
\mathbb{P}
.

8.
Generalized cluster scattering diagrams

8.1.
Definitions

We now introduce a construction generalizing Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich’s cluster scattering diagram
[
27
]
mostly carried out by Mou
[
40
]
and Cheung, Kelley, and Musiker
[
11
]
. Here we take a perspective of
[
3
]
that both coefficients and frozen directions are present. We impose no reciprocal conditions on coefficients to match the generality in
Section

7.1
.

The
generalized cluster scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
we are about to define is essentially attached to a seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},\widetilde{B})
where
\widetilde{B}
is a
|I|\times|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|
rectangular exchange matrix and
p_{i,0}=1
for any
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
. The coefficient group
\mathbb{P}
is taken to be the (multiplicative) abelian group freely generated by

\{p_{i,j}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}},1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}\}.

For easier comparison with
[
27
]
, we closely follow the language therein (see also
[
26
]
) where the information in
\Sigma
can be easily extracted from the so-called
fixed data
and
seed data
.

Definition 8.1
.

The fixed data
\Gamma
consists of

•

a lattice
N
of finite rank with a skew-symmetric bilinear form
\omega\colon N\times N\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
;

•

an
unfrozen sublattice

N_{\mathrm{uf}}\subset N
, a saturated sublattice of
N
;

•

an index set
I=\{1,\dots,\operatorname{rank}N\}
and a subset
I_{\mathrm{uf}}=\{1,\dots,\operatorname{rank}N_{\mathrm{uf}}\}
;

•

positive integers
d_{i}
for
i\in I
with greatest common divisor
1
;

•

a sublattice
N^{\circ}\subset N
of finite index such that
\omega(N_{\mathrm{uf}},N^{\circ})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
and
\omega(N,N_{\mathrm{uf}}\cap N^{\circ})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
;

•

M=\operatorname{Hom}(N,\mathbb{Z})
;

•

a free (multiplicative) abelian group
\mathbb{P}
of finite rank.

Definition 8.2
.

Given the fixed data
\Gamma
, a seed data
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{e},\mathbf{p})
consists of
\mathbf{e}=(e_{i})_{i\in I}\in N^{I}
and
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{p}_{i})_{i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}}
where
\mathbf{p}_{i}=(p_{i,j})_{1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}}
and
p_{i,j}\in\mathbb{P}
such that

(1)

\{e_{i}\mid i\in I\}
is a basis for
N
;

(2)

\{e_{i}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}
is a basis for
N_{\mathrm{uf}}
;

(3)

\{d_{i}e_{i}\mid i\in I\}
is a basis for
N^{\circ}
;

(4)

for
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
, the element
w_{i}\coloneqq\omega(-,d_{i}e_{i})/\ell_{i}
is primitive in
M
;

(5)

\{p_{i,j}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}},1\leq j\leq\ell_{i}\}
is a free generating set for
\mathbb{P}
.

We impose the following
injectivity assumption
.

Injectivity assumption:
The map

(8.3)

N_{\mathrm{uf}}\cap N^{\circ}\rightarrow M,\quad v\mapsto\omega(-,v)

is injective.

Before defining scattering diagrams, we briefly explain how to obtain a seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},\widetilde{B})
from
\Gamma
and
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{e},\mathbf{p})
. The ambient field
\mathcal{F}
is the fraction field of
\operatorname{Frac}(\mathbb{ZP})[M]
. Let
\{e_{i}^{*}\in M\mid i\in I\}
denote the dual basis of
\mathbf{e}
. The cluster
\mathbf{x}
in
\Sigma
is

\{x_{i}\mid i\in I\}=\{x^{e_{i}^{*}}\mid i\in I\}.

The coefficient tuples
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{p}_{i})_{i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}}
are copied from
\mathbf{s}
. For
i\in I
and
j\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
, let

b_{i,j}=\omega(e_{i},d_{j}e_{j})\in\mathbb{Z}.

Then we put
\widetilde{B}=(b_{i,j})\in\mathbb{Z}^{|I|\times|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|}
. The condition that each
\ell_{i}
divides the
i
-th column of
\widetilde{B}
is ensured by (4) of
Definition

8.2
. The injectivity assumption is then equivalent to that
\widetilde{B}
is of full rank.

Remark 8.4
.

It is straightforward to construct fixed and seed data from a seed
\Sigma
. Let
N=\mathbb{Z}^{|I|}
and
\{e_{i}\mid i\in I\}
be the standard basis. For
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
, let
d_{i}
be integers with greatest common divisor
1
such that the diagonal matrix
(d_{i}\delta_{i,j})_{i,j\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}}
is a left skew-symmetrizer for the first
|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|
rows of
\widetilde{B}
. We specify the form
\omega
, which is determined by
\omega(e_{i},e_{j})
for
i,j\in I
. Let

\omega(e_{i},e_{j})=\begin{cases}b_{i,j}/d_{j}\quad&\text{$i\in I$ and $j\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}$},\\
-b_{j,i}/d_{i}\quad&\text{$i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}$ and $j\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}$},\\
0\quad&\text{$i\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}$ and $j\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}$}.\end{cases}

For any
j\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}=I\setminus I_{\mathrm{uf}}
, set
d_{j}=\operatorname{lcm}(d_{i}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}})
. With these choices, the conditions in
Definition

8.1
and
Definition

8.2
are all satisfied.

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
will live in the vector space
M_{\mathbb{R}}=M\otimes\mathbb{R}
. As in the rank-
2
case, we need a monoid
\mathscr{P}
with a monoid map
r\colon\mathscr{P}\rightarrow M
. For our purposes in higher ranks, the choice will soon be specified.

Denote by
\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}=\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}_{\mathbf{s}}
the sub-monoid of
\mathbb{P}
generated by all
p_{i,s}
, which is isomorphic to
\bigoplus_{i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}}\mathbb{N}^{\ell_{i}}
(written additively). Let

\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}_{\mathbf{s}}\coloneqq M\oplus\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}

equipped with the monoid map

r\colon\mathscr{P}\rightarrow M,\quad(m,p)\mapsto m.

Recall that
\Bbbk
denotes a ground field of characteristic zero. Analogous to the rank-2 situation, define ideals (the same notation for both)

\mathfrak{m}\coloneqq\mathscr{P}\setminus\mathscr{P}^{\times}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{m}\coloneqq\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}\setminus\mathscr{P}^{\times}]\subseteq\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}],

where
\mathscr{P}^{\times}
is all units in
\mathscr{P}
, which is
\{(m,1)\mid m\in M\}
in the current case. Monomials in
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]
are associated with elements in
\mathscr{P}
, thus simply denoted as
px^{m}
for
(m,p)\in\mathscr{P}
.

The following definitions of a wall and a scattering diagram are with respect to the fixed data
\Gamma
and a seed data
\mathbf{s}
.

Definition 8.5
.

A
wall
is the data
\mathfrak{d}
of

•

the
support

S_{\mathfrak{d}}
, a convex rational polyhedral cone of codimension one in
M_{\mathbb{R}}

with primitive normal vector

n_{\mathfrak{d}}\in N^{+}=N^{+}_{\mathbf{s}}\coloneqq\left\{\sum_{i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}}a_{i}e_{i}\,\middle|\,a_{i}\in\mathbb{N}\right\}\setminus\{0\};

•

the
wall-function

f_{\mathfrak{d}}
, an element in
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
of an
\mathfrak{m}
-primary ideal
I
or in
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}\coloneqq\lim\limits_{\longleftarrow}\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/{\mathfrak{m}^{k}}
satisfying

f_{\mathfrak{d}}=1+\sum_{(m,p)\in\mathscr{P}}c_{(m,p)}px^{m},

where
r(m,p)=m\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}})\cap M
.

For a wall
\mathfrak{d}
, the primitive generator
m_{\mathfrak{d}}
of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}})\cap M
is called its
direction
. Any monomial
px^{m}
that appears in
f_{\mathfrak{d}}
then has
m=km_{\mathfrak{d}}
for some integer
k\geq 1
.

Definition 8.6
.

A
scattering diagram
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
for an
\mathfrak{m}
-primary ideal
I
is a finite collection of walls whose wall-functions are in
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/I
. A
scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}
over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
is a countable collection of walls whose wall-functions are in
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
such that for each
k\geq 0
, the set

\mathfrak{D}_{k}\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}\mid f_{\mathfrak{d}}\not\equiv 1\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\}

is a scattering diagram over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
.

As in the rank-2 situation, we write

(8.7)

\operatorname{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})\coloneqq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}}S_{\mathfrak{d}}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})\coloneqq\bigcup_{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}}\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}\cup\bigcup_{\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}(S_{\mathfrak{d}}\cap S_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}})

for the
support
and
singular locus
of
\mathfrak{D}
where the last union is taken over all pairs of walls such that the intersection of their supports is of dimension
\dim M_{\mathbb{R}}-2
.

Wall-crossing automorphisms are defined by the higher-rank analogue of (
2.5
), which act on the complete algebra
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
. The notion of a path-ordered product
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma,\mathfrak{D}}
is also extended verbatim to the higher-rank situation for a
regular path

\gamma
that avoids
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
and whose endpoints avoid
\operatorname{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
. The equivalence relation between scattering diagrams and the consistency of a scattering diagram are defined in the same way as respectively in
Definition

2.6
and
Definition

2.8
.

Definition 8.8
.

A wall
\mathfrak{d}
is said to be
incoming
if its direction
m_{\mathfrak{d}}
(or equivalently
\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}})
) is contained in the support
S_{\mathfrak{d}}
. Otherwise it is called
non-incoming
or
outgoing
.

Consider the initial scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathbf{s}}\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{d}_{i}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}\quad\text{where}\quad S_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}\coloneqq e_{i}^{\perp}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}\coloneqq 1+\sum_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}p_{i,1}x^{jw_{i}}.

Clearly every wall in
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathbf{s}}
is incoming.

The following result slightly generalizes
[
27
, Theorem 1.12]
and is again a consequence of
[
34
, 2.1.6]
; see
[
40
, Section 6.2]
and
[
11
, Section 3.2]
.

Theorem 8.9
.

There is a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
satisfying:

(1)

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
is consistent,

(2)

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}\subseteq\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathbf{s}}
,

(3)

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathbf{s}}
consists only of outgoing walls.

Moreover,
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
is unique up to equivalence.

When
p_{i,0}
and
p_{i,\ell_{i}}
specialize to
1
and any
p_{i,j}
,
j\notin\{0,\ell_{i}\}
specializes to
0
(then
f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}
becoming a binomial) for any
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
, then
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
is what now known as the
cluster scattering diagram
of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich
[
27
, Section 1.1]
. We call
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
defined by
Theorem

8.9
a
generalized cluster scattering diagram
.

Remark 8.10
.

When
I_{\mathrm{uf}}=I
and
|I|=2
, then
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
can be easily transformed to the generalized cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
considered in
Section

2.2
by a piecewise linear transformation as in
Theorem

2.16
.

Our main novel result concerning
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
is the positivity of its wall-functions,
Theorem

8.11
, from which all remaining positivity results follow. The proof will be given in
Section

9
.

Theorem 8.11
.

There is a representative (in the equivalence class) of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
such that for any wall
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
, the wall-function
f_{\mathfrak{d}}
belongs to
\widehat{\mathbb{N}[\mathscr{P}]}
, that is,

f_{\mathfrak{d}}=1+\sum_{(m,p)\in\mathscr{P}}c_{(m,p)}px^{m}

has nonnegative integer coefficients
c_{(m,p)}
.

8.2.
The cluster complex structure

The scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
possesses a
cluster complex structure
as in the rank-2 situation. This structure is important for uncovering the connection with the corresponding generalized cluster algebra and is thus essential to the proof of
Theorem

7.9
. In the ordinary case, the cluster complex structure is described in
[
27
]
. In the generalized case, the underlying cone complex is exactly the same
[
40
,
11
]
, which only depends on the exchange matrix
\widetilde{B}
. The more involved part in this generalization is the description of wall-functions (
Theorem

8.20
) due to the complication of coefficients.

We need some preparations. Let
B
be a skew-symmetrizable
n\times n
matrix associated to a chosen root
v_{0}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
. Then for each
v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, we define inductively
\mathbf{c}
-vectors

\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(i)}=\left(c_{j;v}^{(i)}\,\middle|\,j=1,\dots,n\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{n}\ \text{or}\ \mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{n},\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\dots,n

by the following rule.
Set initially

\mathbf{c}_{v_{0}}^{(i)}\coloneqq(\delta_{i,j}\mid j=1,\dots,n),\quad i=1,\dots,n,

the standard unit vectors in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.
For an edge
v\frac{k}{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}v^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
, their associated
\mathbf{c}
-vectors satisfy

(8.12)

\mathbf{c}_{v^{\prime}}^{(i)}=\begin{cases}-\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(k)}\quad&\text{if $i=k$;}\\
\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(i)}+b_{k,i}^{v}[\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(k)}]_{+}\quad&\text{if $i\neq k$ and $b_{i,k}^{v}\leq 0$;}\\
\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(i)}+b_{k,i}^{v}[-\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(k)}]_{+}\quad&\text{if $i\neq k$ and $b_{i,k}^{v}>0$}.\end{cases}

Notice that the relation (
8.12
) is reflexive for
v
and
v^{\prime}
. Therefore, we have well-defined
\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(i)}
for any
v
by propagating (
8.12
) along
\mathbb{T}_{n}
starting from
v_{0}
. The
\mathbf{c}
-vectors were developed by Fomin and Zelevinsky in
[
20
]
as a useful tool to study the dynamics of coefficients.

Theorem 8.13

(
[
27
, Corollary 5.5]
)
.

The
\mathbf{c}
-vectors are always sign coherent, that is, each
\mathbf{c}
-vector
\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(i)}
for
i=1,\dots,n
and
v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
is either in
\mathbb{N}^{n}
or
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{n}
.

Remark 8.14
.

The sign coherence of
\mathbf{c}
-vectors was conjectured in
[
20
, Conjecture 5.4 and Proposition 5.6(iii)]
. In the skew-symmetric case, it was proven by Derksen, Weyman and Zelevinsky
[
14
]
using quiver representations. More generally, for any skew-symmetrizable exchange matrix, the sign coherence was proven in
[
27
]
using scattering diagram techniques. It also follows that the vectors
\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(1)},\dots,\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(n)}
form a basis in
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
; see the proof of
[
20
, Proposition 5.6]
.

It is straightforward from the recurrence (
8.12
) that

c_{i;v}^{(i)}+1\quad\text{or}\quad c_{i;v}^{(i)}-1

is divisible by
\ell_{i}
, and
c_{j;v}^{(i)}
is divisible by
\ell_{i}
when
j\neq i
.

Definition 8.15
.

Define non-negative integers

(8.16)

h_{j;v}^{(i)}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{|c_{i;v}^{(i)}\pm 1|}{\ell_{i}}\in\mathbb{N}\quad&\text{if $i=j$};\\
\frac{|c_{j;v}^{(i)}|}{\ell_{i}}\in\mathbb{N}\quad&\text{if $i\neq j$}.\end{cases}

Remark 8.17
.

When
\ell_{i}=1
or
2
, there is ambiguity on
h_{i;v}^{(i)}
. However, it does not affect the description in
Theorem

8.20
.

For given seed data
\mathbf{s}
with
\mathbf{e}=\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
and
B=(b_{i,j})=\omega(e_{i},d_{j}e_{j})
for
i,j\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
. we define another set of vectors
g^{*}_{i;v}\in N_{\mathrm{uf}}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{n}
again indexed by
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}=\{1,\dots,n\}
and
v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
. These are called
dual
g
-vectors
. The relation they need to satisfy is analogous to (
8.12
) but with respect to
-B^{\intercal}
. Explicitly we set initially

g^{*}_{i;v_{0}}\coloneqq e_{i},\quad i=1,\dots,n,

and for
v\frac{k}{\hskip 8.19447pt\hskip 8.19447pt}v^{\prime}
in
\mathbb{T}_{n}
, put the relation

(8.18)

g_{i;v^{\prime}}^{*}=\begin{cases}-g_{k;v}^{*}\quad&\text{if $i=k$;}\\
g_{i;v}^{*}-b_{i,k}^{v}[g_{k;v}^{*}]_{+}\quad&\text{if $i\neq k$ and $b_{i,k}^{v}\leq 0$;}\\
g_{i;v}^{*}-b_{i,k}^{v}[-g_{k;v}^{*}]_{+}\quad&\text{if $i\neq k$ and $b_{i,k}^{v}>0$}.\end{cases}

For the same reason as
\mathbf{c}
-vectors, they are sign-coherent by
Theorem

8.13
and
(g_{1;v}^{*},\dots,g_{n;v}^{*})
form a basis of
N_{\mathrm{uf}}
. Moreover,
g_{i;v}^{*}
(in the basis
\mathbf{e}
) and
\mathbf{c}^{(i)}_{v}
always have the same sign (see
[
44
, (2,7)]
), which we denote by

\varepsilon_{i;v}\coloneqq\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{if $\mathbf{c}^{(i)}_{v}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}$},\\
-1\quad&\text{if $\mathbf{c}^{(i)}_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{n}$}.\end{cases}

Define for each
v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
, the convex cone

\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}\coloneqq\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\langle m,g_{i;v}^{*}\rangle\geq 0,\ i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}\subseteq M_{\mathbb{R}}.

It has
n
facets

S_{i;v}\coloneqq(g_{i;v}^{*})^{\perp}\cap\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+},\quad i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}.

One can verify inductively that

(8.19)

w_{i;v}\coloneqq\omega(-,\frac{d_{i}}{\ell_{i}}\varepsilon_{i;v}g_{i;v}^{*})\in M.

Now we are ready to state the
cluster complex structure
of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
.

Theorem 8.20
.

There is a pure
|I|
-dimensional cone complex
\Delta^{+}_{\mathbf{s}}
in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
whose top-dimensional cones are

\{\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}\mid v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}\}.

In its equivalent class,
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
has a representative containing the collection of walls

\mathfrak{D}(\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+})\coloneqq\{(S_{i;v},f_{i;v})\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}},v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}\}

where

(8.21)

f_{i;v}=\begin{cases}1+\sum_{s=1}^{\ell_{i}}p_{i,s}\prod_{j=1}^{n}p_{j,\ell_{j}}^{sh_{j;v}^{(i)}}x^{sw_{i;v}}\quad&\text{if $\ell_{i}h_{i;v}^{(i)}=|c_{i;v}^{(i)}|-1$}\\
