1+\sum_{s=1}^{\ell_{i}}\frac{p_{i,\ell_{i}-s}}{p_{i,\ell_{i}}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}p_{j,\ell_{j}}^{sh_{j;v}^{(i)}}x^{sw_{i;v}}\quad&\text{if $\ell_{i}h_{i;v}^{(i)}=|c_{i;v}^{(i)}|+1$}\end{cases}

such that
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}\setminus\mathfrak{D}(\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+})
has support disjoint from (the codimension
\leq 1
strata of)
\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}
.

Proof.

The cone complex
\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}
is understood and has equivalent description in
[
27
, Section 4 and 5]
. We thus focus on deriving the claimed expression of wall-functions from
[
40
, Theorem 7.10]
. There
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
has initial wall-functions for
i=1,\dots,n

\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}(1+t_{i,j}x^{w_{i}})

where
t_{i,j}
are formal variables. While these were denoted as
p_{i,j}
in
[
40
]
, we change everything there from the letter
p
to
t
in order to avoid conflict with our coefficients
p_{i,s}
. In fact, we can regard
p_{i,s}
as symmetric polynomials of
t_{i,j}
by equating

(8.22)

1+\sum_{s=1}^{\ell_{i}}p_{i,s}x^{sw_{i}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}(1+t_{i,j}x^{w_{i}}).

In particular we have
p_{i,\ell_{i}}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}t_{i,j}
.

The wall-function
f_{i;v}
is expressed in
[
40
, Theorem 7.10]
as

f_{i;v}=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{i}}\left(1+t_{i,j;v}^{\varepsilon_{i;v}}x^{w_{i;v}}\right)\quad\text{where}\quad\varepsilon_{i;v}=\begin{cases}1\quad&\text{if $\mathbf{c}^{(i)}_{v}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}$},\\
-1\quad&\text{if $\mathbf{c}^{(i)}_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{n}$},\end{cases}

and each
t_{i,j;v}
is a monomial in
\{t_{i,j}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}},j=1,\dots,\ell_{i}\}
. The exponent vector of
t_{i,j;v}
is termed as a
generalized
c
-vector
and has the same sign
\varepsilon_{i;v}
as
\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(i)}
(
[
40
, Proposition 3.17]
). By
[
40
, Lemma 3.15 and Lemma 3.16]
, the monomial
t_{i,j;v}
(simultaneously for all
j=1,\dots,\ell_{i}
) is of the form

t_{i,j}\cdot p_{i,\ell_{i}}^{\frac{c_{i;v}^{(i)}-1}{\ell_{i}}}\cdot\prod_{j\neq i}p_{j,\ell_{j}}^{\frac{c_{j;v}^{(i)}}{\ell_{j}}}\quad\text{or}\quad t_{i,j}^{-1}\cdot p_{i,\ell_{i}}^{\frac{c_{i;v}^{(i)}+1}{\ell_{i}}}\cdot\prod_{j\neq i}p_{j,\ell_{j}}^{\frac{c_{j;v}^{(i)}}{\ell_{j}}}\,.

We assume the former case and the latter one can be similarly treated. Now there are two further cases.

Case 1:

\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
. This means

\ell_{i}h_{i;v}^{(i)}=c_{i;v}^{(i)}-1\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{j}h_{j;v}^{(i)}=c_{i;v}^{(i)}.

Then we can express
f_{i;v}
as

\prod_{k=1}^{\ell_{i}}\left(1+t_{i,k}\prod_{j=1}^{n}p_{j,\ell_{j}}^{h_{j;v}^{(i)}}x^{w_{i;v}}\right)=1+\sum_{s=1}^{\ell_{i}}p_{i,s}\prod_{j=1}^{n}p_{j,\ell_{j}}^{sh_{j;v}^{(i)}}x^{sw_{i;v}},

proving the first scenario of (
8.21
).

Case 2:

\mathbf{c}_{v}^{(i)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}^{n}
. In this case, we have

\ell_{i}h_{i;v}^{(i)}=-c_{i;v}^{(i)}+1\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{j}h_{j;v}^{(i)}=-c_{i;v}^{(i)}.

We can then express
f_{i;v}
as

\prod_{k=1}^{\ell_{i}}\left(1+t_{i,k}^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{n}p_{j,\ell_{j}}^{h_{j;v}^{(i)}}x^{w_{i;v}}\right)=1+\sum_{s=1}^{\ell_{i}}\frac{p_{i,\ell_{i}-s}}{p_{i,\ell_{i}}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}p_{j,\ell_{j}}^{sh_{j;v}^{(i)}}x^{sw_{i;v}}.

This verifies the second scenario of (
8.21
).
∎

8.3.
Theta functions and positivity of the Laurent phenomenon

Broken lines can also be defined in higher ranks. It suffices for our purposes to consider the initial exponent in
M
. We treat
\Bbbk[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
as the coefficient ring and thus express a monomial (with coefficient) in
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]
as
cx^{m}
where
c\in\Bbbk[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
and
m\in M
.

Definition 8.23
.

Let
m_{0}\in M\setminus\{0\}
and
Q\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\cut\Supp(\mathfrak{D})
. A
broken line

\beta
for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
is a piecewise linear continuous proper path

\beta\colon(-\infty,0]\to M_{\mathbb{R}}\cut\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})

with values
-\infty=\tau_{0}<\tau_{1}<\cdots<\tau_{\ell+1}=0
and an associated monomial
c_{i}x^{m_{i}}\in\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
for each interval
(\tau_{i},\tau_{i+1})
,
i=0,\dots,\ell
such that

(1)

\beta(0)=Q
and
c_{0}=1
;

(2)

\dot{\beta}(\tau)=-m_{i}
for any
\tau\in(\tau_{i},\tau_{i+1})
for each
i=0,\dots,\ell
;

(3)

the subpath
\beta|_{(\tau_{i-1},\tau_{i+1})}
transversally crosses the support of some wall at
\tau_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,\ell-1
;

(4)

for
i=0,\dots,\ell-1
,
c_{i+1}x^{m_{i+1}}
is a monomial term (other than
c_{i}x^{m_{i}}
) of the power series

c_{i}x^{m_{i}}\prod_{\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}\mid\beta(\tau_{i})\in S_{\mathfrak{d}}\}}f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{n_{i}\cdot m_{i}}

where
n_{i}
is primitive in
N
and orthogonal to any
\mathfrak{d}
with
\beta(\tau_{i})\in S_{\mathfrak{d}}
such that
n_{i}\cdot r(m_{i})>0
.

As in rank
2
, a theta function is defined to be the formal sum of the final monomial terms of broken lines. Precisely, the theta function associated to
m_{0}
and
Q
is

(8.24)

\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}\coloneqq\sum_{\beta}c(\beta)x^{m(\beta)},

the sum of all final monomials over all broken line for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
.

We summarize in below the main properties of theta functions that we will need.

Proposition 8.25
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a scattering diagram. Let
m_{0}\in M\setminus\{0\}
and
Q\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\operatorname{Supp}(\mathfrak{D})
.

(1)

The theta function
\vartheta_{Q,m_{0}}
lives in
x^{m_{0}}\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
.

(2)

Let
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
, and let
Q\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+})
and
m\in\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}\cap M
. Then
\vartheta_{Q,m}=x^{m}
.

Proof.

(1) generalizes
[
27
, Proposition 3.4]
. The settings slightly differ as our monoid
\mathscr{P}
contains the coefficient part
\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}
. The proof nonetheless does not change.

(2) generalizes
[
27
, Corollary 3.9]
. It follows from generalizing
[
27
, Proposition 3.8]
and
[
27
, Proposition 3.6]
to our setting. The former is the special case when
v=v_{0}
and carries over by the same argument. The latter is a bijection between certain broken lines in
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
and
\mathfrak{D}_{\mu_{k}(\mathbf{s})}
. We have not defined the mutation
\mu_{k}(\mathbf{s})
but it can be understood as in
[
40
, Definition 6.3]
by unveiling the relation between the variables
t_{i,j}
and
p_{i,s}
(
8.22
). Then we can refer to
[
40
, Proposition 8.9]
.
∎

Let
\Sigma
be a seed arising from
\Gamma
and
\mathbf{s}=(\mathbf{e},\mathbf{p})
as in
Section

8.1
. Consider the generalized cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}(\Sigma)
.

Theorem 8.26
.

There is a generalized cluster structure such that
\Sigma_{v_{0}}=\Sigma
and the cluster monomials in
\mathcal{A}
(with frozen variables inverted)

\left\{\prod_{i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}}x_{i;v}^{a_{i}}\prod_{j\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}}x_{j;v}^{b_{j}}\,\middle|\,a_{i}\in\mathbb{N},b_{j}\in\mathbb{Z},v\in\mathbb{T}_{|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|}\right\}

are precisely the theta functions

\{\vartheta_{Q,m}\mid m\in\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}\cap M\}

for a general
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{v_{0}}^{+}
.

Proof.

In the ordinary case, this is
[
27
, Theorem 4.9]
. The generalized case is discussed in
[
40
, Section 8.4]
(and also in
[
11
]
with reciprocal coefficients) and essentially relies on
Theorem

8.20
the cluster complex structure of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
. We elaborate more as the setup here differs slightly.

In
[
40
]
, we assume
I_{\mathrm{uf}}=I
namely without any frozen directions. Hence in that case the maximal cones in
\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}
are all simplicial whose (primitive) generators are called
g
-vectors. With frozen directions, we can still define
g
-vectors (using the same recurrence)

\{g_{i;v}\in M\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}.

The difference caused by frozen directions is that the recurrence to define
g
-vectors only uses the rectangular exchange matrix
\widetilde{B}
(and its mutations). Another option is to extend
I_{\mathrm{uf}}
to
I
per the construction in
Remark

8.4
. Now we have a
|I|\times|I|
square exchange matrix which can be used to define the dual
g
-vectors
\{g_{i;v}^{*}\in N\mid i\in I\}
for any
v\in\mathbb{T}_{|I|}
. Then
\{g_{i;v}\in M\mid i\in I\}
can be defined to be the dual basis in
M
and we only take the ones with
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
.

The vectors
\{g_{i;v}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}
are dual to
\{g_{i;v}^{*}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}
in the sense that
\langle g_{i;v},g_{j;v}^{*}\rangle=\delta_{i,j}
. The cone
\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}
has the description

\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}\{g_{i;v}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}}\mathbb{R}e_{i}^{*}.

Any
m\in\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}\cap M
is a sum of a nonnegative integral sum of
\{g_{i;v}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}
and a vector
m_{\mathrm{fr}}\in\bigoplus_{i\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}}\mathbb{Z}e_{i}^{*}
. In
[
40
]
, the mutation of a seed is deterministic so we can generate a cluster structure from
\Sigma_{v_{0}}=\Sigma
, which can be extracted from
Theorem

8.20
. In particular, by
[
40
, Proposition 8.1]
, the unfrozen cluster variable
x_{i;v}
is given by

(8.27)

x_{i;v}=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}(x^{g_{i;v}})\in\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}

for a regular path
\gamma
going from
\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}
to
\mathcal{C}_{v_{0}}^{+}
.
Any wall-crossing automorphism acts trivially on
x^{m_{\mathrm{fr}}}
, which is a Laurent monomial of frozen variables
\{x^{e_{i}^{*}}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{fr}}\}
. Now we see that any cluster monomial (with frozen variables inverted) equals

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}(x^{m})\in\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}

for
m
in
\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}\cap M
.

To see that any
\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}(x^{m})
for
m\in\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}\cap M
is a theta function, we first have by
Proposition

8.25

\vartheta_{Q_{v},m}=x^{m}

for any
m\in\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}\cap M
and any
Q_{v}
in the interior of
\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}
. Then using a higher-rank analogue of
Theorem

2.29
(see
[
27
, Theorem 3.5]
and
[
8
]
), we have

(8.28)

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}(x^{m})=\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}(\vartheta_{Q_{v},m})=\vartheta_{Q,m}.

for a general
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{v_{0}}^{+}
.
∎

Now we show the positivity of Laurent phenomenon for cluster monomials in any generalized cluster structure (with possible frozen directions). See the statement in
Theorem

7.9
and terminologies in
Section

7.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

7.9
.

If suffices to show this when the (rectangular) exchange matrix
B
has full rank. If not, we can add more frozen directions by adding rows to
B
to get a full rank matrix. Evaluating added frozen variables at
1
recovers original cluster monomials.

Another simplification is to assume
p_{i,0}=1
for any
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
as discussed in
Remark

7.15
. Now with the injectivity assumption (
8.3
) ensured by the full rank of
B
, the generalized cluster structure is contained within the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
where
\mathbf{s}
can be constructed from a seed
\Sigma=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p},B)
as in
Remark

8.4
.

Now every cluster monomial is a theta function
\vartheta_{Q,m}
by
Theorem

8.26
. Now the positivity of the Laurent phenomenon follows from
Theorem

7.8
and that the weight
c(\gamma)
of every broken line
\gamma
is in
\mathbb{N}\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}
due to the positivity of wall-functions
Theorem

8.11
.
∎

There are variants of Chekhov–Shapiro’s generalized cluster structures, for example
[
43
,
22
,
40
]
, where certain restrictions are imposed on coefficients so that coefficients are “normalized” and thus mutations are deterministic. These settings can nonetheless be viewed as special cases of
Definition

7.6
as pointed out in
loc. cit
. Therefore, the positivity
Theorem

7.9
is still valid in those settings.

8.4.
Strong positivity of theta functions

We define structure constants for theta functions following
[
27
, Definition-Lemma 6.2]
, with the difference that now they are not just integers but in the coefficient ring
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
. The argument carries over and the positivity follows directly from
Theorem

8.11
. Remind that we are given a generalized cluster scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
.

Definition-Lemma 8.29
.

Let
p_{1},p_{2},q\in M
. Let
z\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
be chosen generally. There are at most finitely many pairs of broken lines
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
with
m_{0}(\gamma_{1})=p_{1}
,
m_{0}(\gamma_{2})=p_{2}
and both ending at
z
, and
m(\gamma_{1})+m(\gamma_{2})=q
. Define

\alpha_{z}(p_{1},p_{2};q)=\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})}c(\gamma_{1})c(\gamma_{2})

where the finite sum is over the aforementioned pairs of broken lines. Hence
\alpha_{z}(p_{1},p_{2};q)
is in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
by
Theorem

8.11
.

Now we can describe the product structure of theta functions using
\alpha_{z}(p_{1},p_{2};q)
. Let
Q
be general in
\mathcal{C}_{v_{0}}^{+}
. Write
\vartheta_{m}=\vartheta_{m,Q}
for any
m\in M
. The fact that the structure constants
\alpha(p_{1},p_{2};q)
are in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
is what we refer to as the
strong positivity
.

Theorem 8.30

(cf.
[
27
, Proposition 6.4(3)]
)
.

The theta functions
\{\vartheta_{q}\mid q\in M\}
form a topological basis of certain formal completion of a variant
\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}
of the upper cluster algebra
\mathcal{U}
. For any
p_{1},p_{2}\in M
, we have

(8.31)

\vartheta_{p_{1}}\cdot\vartheta_{p_{2}}=\sum_{q\in M}\alpha(p_{1},p_{2};q)\vartheta_{q}\in\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}

where
\alpha(p_{1},p_{2},q)\coloneqq\alpha_{z(q)}(p_{1},p_{2};q)
for
z(q)
chosen sufficiently near
q
and is independent of this choice.

Proof.

The proof essentially follows that of
[
27
, Proposition 6.4(3)]
. We first understand conceptually where the theta functions live. In fact, for each
v\in\mathbb{T}_{v}
, we have the algebra

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}][x_{i;v}^{\pm 1}\mid i\in I]\subseteq\mathcal{F}.

Denote the intersection of all these algebras by
\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}
(by
\mathcal{U}
if taking the coefficient ring
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
instead). It contains all cluster monomials. In fact, when expressing
x_{i;v}
in another cluster
\mathbf{x}_{v^{\prime}}
, it is the theta function
\vartheta_{Q_{v^{\prime}},g_{i;v}}
for some general
Q_{v^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{C}_{v^{\prime}}^{+}
by (
8.27
) and (
8.28
), so the coefficients are actually in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
. Take the completion

\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}\coloneqq\lim_{\longleftarrow}\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}/I^{k}

with respect to the ideal
I
generated by
\{p_{i,s}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}},1\leq s\leq\ell_{i}\}
. For the same reason
\mathcal{U}
contains cluster monomials,
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}
contains the theta functions
\{\vartheta_{m}\mid m\in M\}
.

We note that
\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}/I=\Bbbk[M]
since letting
p_{i,s}=0
makes any
\vartheta_{Q_{v^{\prime}},g_{i;v}}
a single monomial
x^{g_{i;v}}
. This phenomenon is phrased in
[
27
, Corollary 5.3]
as the central fiber of the family
\mathcal{U}_{\mathrm{prin}}^{\mathbf{s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}_{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}}^{n}
is the torus
T_{N}
. Here we are directly taking the regular functions instead of considering the relevant schemes. The family is in the ordinary case, thus recovered by restricting to all
p_{i,s}=0
except for the leading ones
\{p_{i,\ell_{i}}\mid i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}\}
, which correspond to the coordinates
\{X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\}
.

Back to the product structure, now it suffices to show (
8.31
) in
\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}/I^{k+1}
for each
k\geq 0
. Now the theta functions can be defined using the finite scattering diagram
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k}
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
. As argued in the first paragraph of the proof of
[
27
, Proposition 6.4]
, theta functions
\{\vartheta_{q}\mid q\in M\}
form a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]/I^{k+1}
-basis for
\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}/I^{k+1}
; it is clear when
k=0
as
\vartheta_{q}=x^{q}
and one can use Nakayama’s lemma to lift the basis
\{x^{q}\mid q\in M\}
to
\{\vartheta_{q}\mid q\in M\}
in
\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}/I^{k+1}
. We thus say that
\{\vartheta_{q}\mid q\in M\}
form a
topological basis
of
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}
.

For a given
k\geq 1
, express
\vartheta_{p_{1}}\cdot\vartheta_{p_{2}}
in the basis
\{\vartheta_{q}\mid q\in M\}
. Notice we can choose any general terminal point for this expression because for different terminal points, theta functions are transformed under a path-ordered product which does not change coefficients in
\Bbbk[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]/I^{k}
. For a given
q\in M
, choose a general terminal point
z
close enough to
q
so that they belong to the same chamber. By the definition of
\alpha_{z}
, we have

(8.32)

\vartheta_{z,p_{1}}\cdot\vartheta_{z,p_{2}}=\sum_{r}\alpha_{z}(p_{1},p_{2};r)x^{r}.

However, among all broken lines for all
\vartheta_{z,r}
,
r\in M
, there is only one broken line (whose image is
z+\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}q
) with associated final monomial
x^{q}
, which gives the theta function
\vartheta_{z,q}=x^{q}
. Thus the coefficient of
\vartheta_{z,q}
can be read off by the coefficient of
x^{q}
on the right-hand side of (
8.32
), namely
\alpha_{z}(p_{1},p_{2};q)
. This proves the product formula in order
k
. To see the independence of
z
for all
k
, we observe exactly as in the proof of
[
27
, Definition-Lemma 6.2]
that for given
p_{1},p_{2},q
only finitely many wall-function terms in
\mathfrak{D}
are relevant to the possible pairs of broken lines computing
\alpha_{z}(p_{1},p_{2};q)
(for any general
z
). Hence one can choose a big enough
k
and use
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
to define
\alpha(p_{1},p_{2};q)
.
∎

In general, the cluster complex
\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}
is only a proper subset of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Theta functions outside
\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}
may not be Laurent but a formal sum. The following observation generalizes
[
27
, Proposition 7.1]
immediately once we have the positivity of wall-crossing functions
Theorem

8.11
.

Proposition 8.33
.

Let
m_{0}\in M
. If for some general point
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{v}^{+}
for
v\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
there are only finitely many broken lines for
m_{0}
with endpoint
Q
, the same is true for any general point
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{v^{\prime}}^{+}
for any other
v^{\prime}\in\mathbb{T}_{n}
. In this case, the theta function
\vartheta_{m}
is a positive universal Laurent polynomial.

We can now extend
[
27
, Theorem 7.5]
to the generalized case.

Theorem 8.34
.

Let
\Theta\subseteq M
be the collection of
m
such that the theta function
\vartheta_{m}
is a finite sum, that is, by
Proposition

8.33
a universal Laurent polynomial.

(1)

\Delta_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}\cap M\subseteq\Theta
.

(2)

For
p_{1},p_{2}\in\Theta
,

\vartheta_{p_{1}}\cdot\vartheta_{p_{2}}=\sum_{r}\alpha(p_{1},p_{2};r)\vartheta_{r}

is a finite sum with coefficients in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
. If
\alpha(p_{1},p_{2};r)\neq 0
, then
r\in\Theta
.

(3)

\Theta
is a saturated monoid in
M
.

(4)

The
\Bbbk[\mathbb{P}]
-module

\mathcal{M}\coloneqq\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]}\{\vartheta_{m}\mid m\in\Theta\}

is an associative
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
-algebra with a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
-basis given by
\{\vartheta_{m}\mid m\in\Theta\}
. We have natural inclusions

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{U}.

Proof.

The proof uses the exact same arguments as in
[
27
, Theorem 7.5]
but relies on the generalized positivity
Theorem

8.11
. We note that the inclusion
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
sends a cluster monomial to the corresponding theta function given by
Theorem

8.26
and the second inclusion
\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
is guaranteed by
Proposition

8.33
.
∎

Remark 8.35
.

In the ordinary case, the algebra
\mathcal{M}
is denoted as
\operatorname{mid}(\mathcal{A})
(where
\mathcal{A}
denotes the cluster variety) and called the
middle cluster algebra
in
[
27
]
. The set
\Theta
a priori depends on the choices of
\ell_{i}
. In particular, when
\ell_{i}=1
for any
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
, it is the corresponding set for ordinary cluster algebras. It is interesting to ask whether the generalized and ordinary situations share the same
\Theta
.

8.5.
Theta functions are atomic

A non-zero element in the upper cluster algebra
\mathcal{U}
is called
universally positive

[
17
]
if it is a Laurent polynomial with coefficients in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}]
in every cluster, that is, it is in the subset

\bigcap_{v\in\mathbb{T}_{|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|}}\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}][x_{i;v}^{\pm 1}\mid i\in I]\subseteq\mathcal{U}.

Such an element is furthermore called
indecomposable
or
atomic
if it cannot be written as a sum of two universally positive elements. Any cluster monomial is universally positive by
Theorem

7.9
and is atomic since it is a monomial in some cluster.

Theta functions are not necessarily in
\mathcal{U}
but live in the completion
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}
. We follow Mandel’s definition
[
39
]
of atomicity using the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
.

Definition 8.36
.

A non-zero element
f\in\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}
is called
universally positive
with respect to the
scattering atlas
if regarded as an element in
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
, for any general
Q\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{s}}^{+}
, any general
Q^{\prime}\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\operatorname{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})
, and a regular path
\gamma
from
Q
to
Q^{\prime}
, the element

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}(f)\in\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}

is a formal Laurent series with coefficients in
\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
. It is in addition called
atomic
if it cannot be written as a sum of two elements that are universally positive with respect to the scattering atlas.

Theorem 8.37

(cf.
[
39
, Theorem 1]
)
.

The theta functions
\{\vartheta_{q}\mid q\in M\}
(up to a scalar in
\mathbb{P}
) are exactly the atomic elements of
\widehat{\mathcal{U}^{\oplus}}
with respect to the scattering atlas.

Proof.

It follows directly from
Theorem

8.11
and (
[
27
, Theorem 3.5]
and
[
8
]
)

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}(\vartheta_{Q,q})=\vartheta_{Q^{\prime},q}

that theta functions are universally positive with respect to the scattering atlas. Then it remains to show that for any
f
universally positive with respect to the scattering atlas, the expansion

f=\sum_{q\in M}a_{q}\vartheta_{q}

has
a_{q}\in\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
for any
q\in M
. However, for
r\in M
, we can take
Q^{\prime}
sufficiently close to
r
as in the proof of
Theorem

8.30
so that in the expansion

\mathfrak{p}_{\gamma}(f)=\sum_{q\in M}c_{q}x^{q},

we have
a_{r}=c_{r}\in\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}]
by the universal positive assumption on
f
.
∎

9.
Positivity of wall-functions

This section is devoted to positivity of scattering diagrams. We prove in
Section

9.2
that in rank 2, any consistent scattering diagram is positive with respect to the initial coefficients. In
Section

9.3
, we prove
Theorem

8.11
, the positivity of higher-rank generalized cluster scattering diagrams.

9.1.
The non-standard case

The scattering diagrams considered in
Section

2.2
are regarded as
standard
, as the directions of the two initial walls are standard unit vectors
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
. The non-standard case can be treated by the
change-of-lattice trick
already mentioned in the proof of
[
27
, Proposition C.13]
.

Let
M=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Suppose that
m_{1},m_{2}\in M
are primitive, linearly independent, but may only generate a rank-2 proper sublattice
M^{\circ}\subsetneq M
. The dual
N^{\circ}\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M^{\circ},\mathbb{Z})
naturally contains
N\coloneqq\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(M,\mathbb{Z})
as a sublattice.

For any
m\in M
, denote
m^{\perp}\coloneqq\{n\in N\otimes\mathbb{R}\mid n\cdot m=0\}
. We define the
index
of
m
to be

\operatorname{ind}(m)\coloneqq|(m^{\perp}\cap N^{\circ})/(m^{\perp}\cap N)|,

that is, the order of the finite cyclic group
(m^{\perp}\cap N^{\circ})/(m^{\perp}\cap N)
. For example, we have

(9.1)

\operatorname{ind}(m_{1})=\operatorname{ind}(m_{2})=|M/M^{\circ}|\eqqcolon\ell.

Notice that
\operatorname{ind}(m)
only depends on the line
\mathbb{R}m
. We then define
\operatorname{ind}(\mathfrak{d})\coloneqq\mathrm{ind}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})
,
where
m_{\mathfrak{d}}
is the direction of either a ray or a line
\mathfrak{d}
.

Take
\mathscr{P}=M\oplus\mathbb{M}
to be the monoid in
Section

2.2
with the projection
r\colon\mathscr{P}\rightarrow M
. Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}=\{\mathfrak{d}_{1},\mathfrak{d}_{2}\}
where

S_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}=\mathbb{R}m_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}=f_{i}\coloneqq 1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell_{i}}p_{i,k}x^{km_{i}},\quad i=1,2.

By
Theorem

2.10
, we can assume that there is at most one added ray in
\mathfrak{D}
with support
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(pm_{1}+qm_{2})
for any positive coprime pair
(p,q)
. Assuming that
m_{1},m_{2}
form a basis of
M
, we can express the attached wall-function as

(9.2)

f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(pm_{1}+qm_{2})}=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\lambda(dp,dq)x^{dpm_{1}+dqm_{2}},

where
\lambda(dp,dq)\in\mathbb{N}[\mathbb{M}]
is computed in
Theorem

5.17
.

Definition 9.3
.

Define
\lambda(dp,dq;\ell)
for coprime
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
d\in\mathbb{N}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
by the following procedure. First we evaluate
\lambda(\ell dp,\ell dq)
at
p_{i,k}=0
for any
k
not divisible by
\ell
. Then we substitute any
p_{i,\ell k}
by
p_{i,k}
.

Example 9.4
.

Let
(p,q)=(1,1)
,
d=1
,
\ell=2
. For
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=2
, we compute
\lambda(1,1;2)
. By
Theorem

5.17
, we have

\lambda(2,2)=p_{1,1}^{2}p_{2,2}+p_{2,1}^{2}p_{1,2}+2p_{1,2}p_{2,2}.

After the evaluation at
p_{1,1}=p_{2,1}=0
, we get
2p_{1,2}p_{2,2}
. Substituting
p_{i,2}
by
p_{i,1}
, we obtain
\lambda(1,1;2)=2p_{1,1}p_{2,1}
.

The following formula computes wall-functions in the non-standard case.

Theorem 9.5
.

The wall-function in
\mathfrak{D}
on any ray
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}
with
m^{\circ}=pm_{1}+qm_{2}
and
(p,q)
positive coprime is

f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}=\left(1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\lambda(dp,dq;\ell)x^{dpm_{1}+dqm_{2}}\right)^{\frac{\ell}{\mathrm{ind}(m^{\circ})}}.

where
\ell
is defined in (
9.1
) and
\ell/\operatorname{ind}(m^{\circ})
belongs to
\mathbb{N}
. Hence every coefficient in the expansion of
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}
is a polynomial in
p_{i,j}
with nonnegative integer coefficients.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{d}
be a wall in
\mathfrak{D}
. The associated wall-crossing automorphism is

x^{m^{\prime}}\mapsto x^{m^{\prime}}f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\pm n\cdot m^{\prime}},

where
n
is primitive in
N
and orthogonal to
m_{\mathfrak{d}}
. Notice that the index of
\mathfrak{d}
is equivalently

\mathrm{ind}(\mathfrak{d})\coloneqq\max\{d\in\mathbb{N}\mid n/d\in N^{\circ}\}.

Namely
n^{\circ}\coloneqq n/\mathrm{ind}(\mathfrak{d})
is primitive in
N^{\circ}
.

Consider the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\circ}\coloneqq\left\{\left(S_{\mathfrak{d}},f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\operatorname{ind}(\mathfrak{d})}\right)\,\middle|\,\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}\right\}.

We claim that it is the consistent scattering diagram
\Scat(f_{1}^{\ell},f_{2}^{\ell})
for the lattice
M^{\circ}
. In fact, for
m^{\prime}\in M^{\circ}
, the wall-crossing automorphism associated to
(S_{\mathfrak{d}},f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\operatorname{ind}(\mathfrak{d})})
can be expressed as

x^{m^{\prime}}\mapsto x^{m^{\prime}}\left(f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\mathrm{ind}(\mathfrak{d})}\right)^{\pm n^{\circ}\cdot m^{\prime}}=x^{m^{\prime}}f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\pm n\cdot m^{\prime}}

where
n
and
n^{\circ}
are respectively the primitive normal vectors of
S_{\mathfrak{d}}
in
N
and
N^{\circ}
. It follows immediately that
\mathfrak{D}^{\circ}
is consistent because so is
\mathfrak{D}
. Thus by the uniqueness, we have
\mathfrak{D}^{\circ}=\Scat(f_{1}^{\ell},f_{2}^{\ell})
.

In retrospect, we can use
\mathfrak{D}^{\circ}
to represent
\mathfrak{D}
. This in particular implies that any wall-function
f
of
\mathfrak{D}
is always a power series of
x^{m^{\circ}}
for some primitive
m^{\circ}
in
M^{\circ}
. Precisely, consider
(p,q)
positive coprime and
m^{\circ}=pm_{1}+qm_{2}\in M^{\circ}
. The wall-function
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}
in
\mathfrak{D}
is then recovered by

(9.6)

f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}=\left(f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}^{\circ}\right)^{\frac{1}{\mathrm{ind}(m^{\circ})}}

where
f^{\circ}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}
denotes the wall-function in
\mathfrak{D}^{\circ}
. This is not so ideal as taking the power of a fraction may break the integrality and the positivity.

However, we can
change the lattice
one more time. Consider the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}=\Scat(f^{\prime}_{1},f^{\prime}_{2})

again with respect to
M^{\circ}
where for
i=1,2

f_{i}^{\prime}\coloneqq f_{i}(x^{\ell m_{i}})=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}p_{i,d}x^{\ell dm_{i}}.

For the ray with support
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}
where
m^{\circ}=pm_{1}+qm_{2}
for
(p,q)
positive coprime, denote the wall-function by
f^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}
. By (
9.2
) and the substitution
Definition

9.3
, we have

f^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}=1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\lambda(dp,dq;\ell)x^{dpm_{1}+dqm_{2}}.

Now consider the wall-crossing automorphism associated to the above ray in
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
on
x^{a_{1}\ell m_{1}+a_{2}\ell m_{2}}
with
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
:

x^{a_{1}\ell m_{1}+a_{2}\ell m_{2}}\mapsto x^{a_{1}\ell m_{1}+a_{2}\ell m_{2}}(f^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}})^{\pm\ell(pa_{2}-qa_{1})}.

Observe that these wall-crossings can be viewed as in a scattering diagram for
M^{\prime}
generated by
\ell m_{1}
and
\ell m_{2}
. Therefore, by turning every
f^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}
into

(9.7)

f^{\circ}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}\coloneqq\left(f^{\prime}_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}\mid x^{m_{i}}\leftarrow x^{\ell m_{i}}\right)^{\ell},

we obtain a consistent scattering diagram for
M^{\circ}
with the initial wall-functions
f_{1}^{\ell}
and
f_{2}^{\ell}
. By the uniqueness, this is exactly
\mathfrak{D}^{\circ}
, hence justifying the previously defined notation
f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\circ}
.

Now combining (
9.6
) with (
9.7
), the wall-function for
\mathfrak{D}
can be recovered as

f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m^{\circ}}=\left(1+\sum_{d\geq 1}\lambda(dp,dq;\ell)x^{\ell d(pm_{1}+qm_{2})}\right)^{\frac{\ell}{\mathrm{ind}(m^{\circ})}},

as stated in the theorem. Since for any
m\in M
, we have

\ell(m^{\perp}\cap N^{\circ})\subseteq m^{\perp}\cap N\subseteq m^{\perp}\cap N^{\circ},

the index
\operatorname{ind}(m^{\circ})
divides
\ell
.
∎

Corollary 9.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}=\{\mathfrak{d}_{1},\mathfrak{d}_{2}\}
be a scattering diagram of two lines, where

f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}=1+\sum_{m\in\mathscr{P}(m_{i})}c_{i,m}x^{m}\in\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]},\quad i=1,2.

Then there is a consistent scattering diagram in the equivalent class of
\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})
such that any coefficient
c_{m}\in\Bbbk
of any wall-function

f_{\mathfrak{d}}=1+\sum_{m\in\mathscr{P}(m_{\mathfrak{d}})}c_{m}x^{m}

can be expressed as a polynomial in
\{c_{i,m}\mid i=1,2,m\in\mathscr{P}\}
with nonnegative integer coefficients.

Proof.

Notice that we do not assume
\mathscr{P}=M\oplus\mathbb{M}
and thus
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
is not exactly of the form in
Theorem

9.5
. It suffices to show the positivity at each order
k\geq 1
for
\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{k}
. For fixed
k
, we can choose large
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
and consider a close variant
\mathfrak{D}=\Scat(f_{1},f_{2})
of the scattering diagram in
Theorem

9.5
where

f_{i}=1+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell_{i}}p_{i,k},

with the monoid
\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
the monomials in
\{p_{i,k}\mid i=1,2,1\leq k\leq\ell_{i}\}
equipped with
r^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}\rightarrow M
such that
r^{\prime}(p_{i,k})=km_{i}
. Then consider the algebra homomorphism

\varphi\colon\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathbb{M}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}]}\rightarrow\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]},\quad\varphi(p_{i,k})=\sum_{r(m)=km_{i}}c_{i,m}x^{m}.

By the functoriality (
2.11
), we have
\varphi(\mathfrak{D})\equiv\Scat(\varphi(f_{1}),\varphi(f_{2}))
. The positive integers
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
are sufficiently large so that
f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}\equiv\varphi(f_{i})\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
. Then the positivity of
\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{k}\equiv\varphi(\mathfrak{D})_{k}
follows from that of
\mathfrak{D}
by
Theorem

9.5
.
∎

9.2.
Full positivity in rank 2

We now show that any rank-2 consistent scattering diagram is positive with respect to the initial coefficients.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}=\{\mathfrak{d}_{s}\mid s\in S\}
be a scattering diagram (as defined in
Section

2.1
) of lines, where
S
is a countable index set. We do not require the lines to pass through the origin. The initial wall-functions are expressed as

f_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}=1+\sum_{m\in\mathscr{P}(m_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}})}c_{s,m}x^{m}\in\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}.

Theorem 9.9
.

There exists a consistent scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
in the equivalent class of
\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})
such that any coefficient
c_{m}\in\Bbbk
of any wall-function

f_{\mathfrak{d}}=1+\sum_{m\in\mathscr{P}}c_{m}x^{m}

can be expressed as a polynomial in
\{c_{s,m}\mid s\in S,m\in\mathscr{P}\}
with nonnegative integer coefficients, which we call a positive polynomial.

Proof.

We prove by inductively constructing a finite scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
for each
k\geq 1
such that
\mathfrak{D}_{k}\equiv\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{k}
and that the positivity holds for
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
. This is clearly true for
k=1
as we simply take
\mathfrak{D}_{1}=(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{1}
. We add to the induction hypothesis that
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
is a union of
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{k}
and a set of rays such that no two rays have the same support.

Step 1: Reduction to the cone case.

We first reduce to the case where every initial line passes through the origin, which we refer to as the
cone case
.

For a singular point
p\in\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k})
, define

\mathfrak{D}_{k}(p)\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}\mid p\in S_{\mathfrak{d}}\}.

When viewed as over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
,
\mathfrak{D}_{k}(p)
may fail to be consistent at
p
. Translating the support of every wall in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}(p)
by
-p
, we obtain a scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{k}^{\mathrm{loc}}(p)
centered at the origin, which can be viewed as a local version of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}(p)
at
p
. Denote the set of lines in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}^{\mathrm{loc}}(p)
by
\mathfrak{D}_{k,\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(p)
.

Assuming the inductive construction is valid in the cone case, we can modify
\mathfrak{D}_{k}(p)
as follows. We can add a finite collection
\mathfrak{D}_{k}[p]
of new rays based at
p
whose wall-functions are in
1+\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
and modify existing rays
\mathfrak{d}\mapsto\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}
by only updating their wall-functions
f_{\mathfrak{d}}
by adding monomials in
\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
where the coefficients of add terms are positive polynomials such that

\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}(p)\coloneqq\mathfrak{D}_{k}[p]\cup\{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(p),p=\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}\}\cup\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}(p)\mid p\in S_{\mathfrak{d}}\cut\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}\}

is consistent at
p
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
. Notice that this means
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}^{\mathrm{loc}}(p)\equiv\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{k,\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{loc}}(p))\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
.

Globally, we construct

\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{k+1}\cup\bigcup_{p\,\in\,\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k})}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{k}[p]\cup\{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k},\,p=\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}\}\right).

We show that the induction hypothesis holds for
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
.

Clearly
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
is positive. To show
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}\equiv\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{k+1}
, it suffices to show the consistency for
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
because they share the same set of lines. Then we can check locally at each singular point
p\in\Sing(\mathfrak{D}_{k+1})
. Suppose that
p\in\Sing(\mathfrak{D}_{k})
. For each
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}(p)
such that
p\in S_{\mathfrak{d}}\cut\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}
, we write

f_{\mathfrak{d}}=f^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{d}}\cdot(1+\xi)\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+2},

where
\xi
is the part of
f_{\mathfrak{d}}
in
\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\cut\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
. The wall-crossing automorphism (and its inverse) associated to
1+\xi
commutes with others modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
. In the path-ordered product of a small loop around
p
, such wall-crossing automorphism and its inverse both appear once and thus cancel out. Therefore to check the consistency at
p
, we can replace
f_{\mathfrak{d}}
with
f_{\mathfrak{d}}^{\prime}
. After the replacement for every such
\mathfrak{d}
,
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}(p)
is equivalent to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}_{k}(p)
, hence consistent. A new singular point
p\in\Sing(\mathfrak{D}_{k+1})\cut\Sing(\mathfrak{D}_{k})
must be in the interior of some newly added ray. There is no ray based at
p
. For consistency at
p
, we can again ignore any newly added ray that contains
p
in its interior. The rest walls containing
p
are all in the same or the opposite direction, whose wall-crossing automorphisms commute and cancel out in the path-ordered product of a small loop around
p
. Thus
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
is consistent at
p
.

Then the induction hypothesis is proven for
k+1
by assuming that this can be done for the cone case.

Step 2: Perturbation trick.

We use the
perturbation trick
to handle the cone case. Choose for each
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
a sufficiently general vector
v_{\mathfrak{d}}\in M_{\mathbb{R}}
and consider the
perturbed scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}}
with supports
S_{\mathfrak{d}}+v_{\mathfrak{d}}
but the same wall-functions. After the perturbation, at most two initial lines intersect at any given point.

By the induction hypothesis, we have constructed positive
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k}
equivalent to
\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}^{\prime})_{k}
, where
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{1}=(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}})_{1}
. Because of the perturbation, for each
p\in\Sing(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k})
, there are at most two lines in
\left(\mathfrak{D}^{\prime,\mathrm{loc}}_{k,\mathrm{in}}(p)\right)_{1}
, that is, at most two lines are non-trivial modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{2}
. We want to show
\widetilde{\mathfrak{D}}^{\prime}_{k}(p)
is positive at each
p
. Notice that this is equivalent to show the induction hypothesis at
k+1
for the initial scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime,\mathrm{loc}}_{k,\mathrm{in}}(p)
. Once this is established, we can construct
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1}
as in
Step 1
. To get
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
, we then translate every wall in
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}_{k+1}
back to pass through or based at the origin. Therefore it suffices to prove the induction step in the cone case with at most two non-trivial lines in
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{1}

Step 3: Reduction to two initial lines.

By
Step 2
, we assume in the cone case that only
\mathfrak{d}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{2}
are non-trivial in
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{1}
. We may assume that
S=\{1,\dots,N\}
since only finitely many initial lines are non-trivial up to a sufficiently large order. We want to show the induction hypothesis at
k+1
, that is, on any ray in
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}=\Scat(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}})_{k+1}
, every term
c_{p}x^{p}
in
\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\cut\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
has positive polynomial coefficient
c_{p}
. Notice that

p=\sum_{i,s}a_{i,s}p_{i,s}

for
a_{i,s}\in\mathbb{N}
, where
p_{i,s}\in\mathscr{P}
appears in
f_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}
. If any
a_{i,s}\neq 0
for some
s\neq 1,2
, then at most
k
of
a_{i,s}
are non-zero. The crucial point is that
c_{p}x^{p}
can be viewed as a term of order at most
k
after the
change of monoid trick
as follows.
Express each
f_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}
as

f_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}=1+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{s}}c_{i,s}x^{p_{i,s}}\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}.

Consider the monoid

\mathbb{M}=\bigoplus_{s=1}^{N}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{\ell_{s}}\mathbb{N}\cdot e_{i,s}

with
r^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{M}\rightarrow M
such that
r^{\prime}(e_{i,s})=r(p_{i,s})
. Denote
\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}=\mathbb{M}\cut\{0\}
. Consider the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}=\Scat\left(1+\sum_{i=1}^{\ell_{s}}c_{i,s}x^{e_{i,s}}\,\middle|\,s=1,\dots,N\right).

By the functoriality (
2.11
), we can obtain
c_{p}x^{p}
by a wall-function term
c_{p}x^{\sum_{i,s}a_{i,s}e_{i,s}}
in
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
. Notice that now
\sum_{i,s}a_{i,s}e_{i,s}
is in
(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})^{j}\cut(\mathfrak{m}^{\prime})^{j+1}
for some
j\leq k
. So we apply the induction hypothesis at
k
to this situation to conclude that
c_{p}
is positive with respect to the initial coefficients.

Now we are left with
p
as a linear combination of
p_{i,s}
for
s=1,2
only. Notice that these same terms appear in
\Scat(\mathfrak{d}_{1},\mathfrak{d}_{2})
as one can simply let
x^{p_{i,s}}=0
for
s\neq 1,2
. Therefore we have reduced the problem to prove the induction hypothesis at
k+1
for the case of two initial lines.

Step 4.
When
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in}}
only consists of two initial lines, the positivity follows immediately from
Corollary

9.8
.

Now the induction is complete.
∎

9.3.
Positivity in higher ranks

We proceed to prove
Theorem

8.11
the positivity of wall-functions in
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
as defined in
Section

8.1
. The strategy is to construct a sequence of scattering diagrams
(\mathfrak{D}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
such that each
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
is over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
, has positive wall-functions, and is equivalent to
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k}
.

We first realize the singular locus (
8.7
) of a finite scattering diagram as a cone complex. The support of each wall, as a polyhedral cone, is a finite intersection of closed linear half-spaces. Consider the collection of these half-spaces and their other halves from all the walls in
\mathfrak{D}
. Some of their intersections are contained in
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
and
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
is a union of these intersections. They give rise to a cone complex structure on
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
.

Definition 9.10
.

For any scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
of finitely many walls, the singular locus
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
can be realized as the union of the maximal cones of a cone complex of dimension
\dim M_{\mathbb{R}}-2
such that for each such cone
\mathfrak{j}
and
x\in\operatorname{Int}(\mathfrak{j})
(the interior points of
\mathfrak{j}
), the set of walls

\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}\mid x\in S_{\mathfrak{d}}\}

is independent of
x
. With such a realization, the maximal cones are called
joints
. In particular, any facet of
S_{\mathfrak{d}}
is a union of joints with disjoint interiors.

The aforementioned cone complex structure on
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D})
clearly satisfies the requirement in the above definition, thus giving a choice of joints.

Let
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
be
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathbf{s}})_{1}
. Concretely, the walls in
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathbf{s}})_{1}
are still
\mathfrak{d}_{i}
for
i\in I_{\mathrm{uf}}
with the support
S_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}=e_{i}^{\perp}
and the wall-function

f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}=1+p_{i,1}x^{w_{i}}+\dots+p_{i,\ell_{i}}x^{\ell_{i}w_{i}}

because each monomial is in
\mathfrak{m}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{2}
. It is consistent as the wall-crossing automorphisms commute over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
.

Example 9.11
.

For
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
, we have

\operatorname{Supp}(\mathfrak{D}_{1})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}^{\perp}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{1})=\bigcup_{i\neq j}e_{i}^{\perp}\cap e_{j}^{\perp}.

The joints of
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
are as follows. Let
H_{k}^{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}
be the half space

H_{k}^{\varepsilon}\coloneqq\{m\in M_{\mathbb{R}}\mid\mathrm{sgn}(\langle e_{k},m\rangle)=\varepsilon\}.

A joint is the closure of

e_{i}^{\perp}\cap e_{j}^{\perp}\cap\bigcap_{k\neq i,j}H_{k}^{\varepsilon_{k}}

for
i\neq j
and
\varepsilon_{k}\in\{+,-\}
.

Definition 9.12
.

A scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}
is called
consistent
at a joint
\mathfrak{j}
if the path-ordered product associated to a small loop around
\mathfrak{j}
is identity. It is called
consistent
if it is consistent at each of its joint. This is equivalent to the previous definition in
Section

8.1
where the consistency means the path-ordered product of any regular path only depends on the endpoints.

Remark 9.13
.

By the definition of joints, the set of walls (whose support) containing a given joint is well-defined. There are two situations that a wall can contain a joint
\mathfrak{j}
: (1)
\mathfrak{j}
is contained in
\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}
; (2)
\mathrm{int}(\mathfrak{j})
is contained in
\mathrm{int}(S_{\mathfrak{d}})
. To check the consistency at
\mathfrak{j}
, the small loop around
\mathfrak{j}
is chosen to cross a wall of the first kind once and a wall of the second kind twice.

Lemma 9.14

(
[
27
, Definition-Lemma C.2]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{D}
be a finite scattering diagram as in
Section

8.1
. Let
\mathfrak{j}
be a joint of
\mathfrak{D}
. Then either every wall containing
\mathfrak{j}
has direction tangent to
\mathfrak{j}
or every wall containing
\mathfrak{j}
has direction not tangent to
\mathfrak{j}
. In the first case we call the joint parallel; in the second case, perpendicular.

Now we inductively construct for any
k\geq 1
, a finite scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
to satisfy the following four assumptions.

(A1)

\mathfrak{D}_{k}
is consistent over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
, that is, consistent at each joint.

(A2)

Any wall-function has positive coefficients, i.e., in
\mathbb{N}[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
.

(A3)

Any non-initial wall
\mathfrak{d}
is outgoing and always has support of the form

S_{\mathfrak{d}}=F_{\mathfrak{d}}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}})

where
F_{\mathfrak{d}}\subseteq\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}
is a facet. In particular,
F_{\mathfrak{d}}
is the only facet not tangent to the direction of
\mathfrak{d}
. We say that
\mathfrak{d}
or
S_{\mathfrak{d}}
is
based
at
F_{\mathfrak{d}}
.

(A4)

Any perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}
is contained in the facet of at most one outgoing wall in each possible direction.

The base case
\mathfrak{D}_{1}
clearly satisfies all four assumptions.

Now we construct
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
by essentially modifying existing walls in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
and adding new walls based at perpendicular joints of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
. Precisely, the construction comprises three steps.

Step 1: subdivision of outgoing walls.

By the assumption (A3), every outgoing wall
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}
has support

S_{\mathfrak{d}}=F_{\mathfrak{d}}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}})

where
F_{\mathfrak{d}}
is a facet not tangent to
\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}})
. Being in the boundary of
S_{\mathfrak{d}}
, the facet
F_{\mathfrak{d}}
inherits part of the cone complex realization of
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k})
. Thus we can subdivide
F_{\mathfrak{d}}
into joints

F_{\mathfrak{d}}=\mathfrak{j}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathfrak{j}_{s}.

Note that these joints are all perpendicular. Accordingly
\mathfrak{d}
is subdivided into walls
\mathfrak{d}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{d}_{s}
with smaller supports

S_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}=\mathfrak{j}_{1}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}}),\dots,S_{\mathfrak{d}s}=\mathfrak{j}_{s}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}}),

and with wall-functions

f_{\mathfrak{d}_{1}}=\dots=f_{\mathfrak{d}_{s}}=f_{\mathfrak{d}}.

We replace
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
with an equivalent scattering diagram by implementing the above subdivision on every outgoing wall. Clearly it still satisfies the four assumptions. The set of joints remains unchanged. We also note that an initial wall never gets subdivided as it is incoming.

Step 2: rank-2 scattering at perpendicular joints.

Consider at each perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
the scattering diagram

\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}}\coloneqq\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}\mid\mathfrak{j}\subseteq S_{\mathfrak{d}}\},

that is, the collection of walls whose support contains
\mathfrak{j}
. Let
\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j}}
denote the tangent space of (any interior point of)
\mathfrak{j}
. It can thus be viewed as a
(\dim M_{\mathbb{R}}-2)
-dimensional subspace of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
.
We next construct a rank-2 scattering diagram
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}}
with respect to the lattice

M_{\mathfrak{j}}\coloneqq\omega(-,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j}}^{\perp})\cap M,

living in the subspace
M_{\mathfrak{j},\mathbb{R}}\coloneqq\omega(-,\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j}}^{\perp})\subseteq M_{\mathbb{R}}
. These are of rank
2
because of the injectivity assumption on the form
\omega(-,-)
.

The monoid for
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}}
is

\mathscr{P}_{\mathfrak{j}}\coloneqq\{p\in\mathscr{P}\mid r(p)\in M_{\mathfrak{j}}\}=M_{\mathfrak{j}}\oplus\mathbb{P}^{\oplus}=M_{\mathfrak{j}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{|I_{\mathrm{uf}}|}\mathbb{N}^{\ell_{i}}.

Let
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{j}}
denote
\mathscr{P}_{\mathfrak{j}}\setminus\mathscr{P}_{\mathfrak{j}}^{\times}
as well as the corresponding monomial ideal in
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{\mathfrak{j}}]
. For each
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}}
, define a wall
\overline{\mathfrak{d}}
with the support

S_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}\coloneqq S_{\mathfrak{d}}\cap M_{\mathfrak{j},\mathbb{R}}

and the wall-function
f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}\coloneqq f_{\mathfrak{d}}
.
Clearly by the particular form of the wall-function, the direction of
\overline{\mathfrak{d}}
is
m_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}=m_{\mathfrak{d}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}})\cap M
where
n_{\mathfrak{d}}\in N^{+}
is the normal vector of
\mathfrak{d}
. One should carefully check that when
S_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}
is a ray, it must be of the form
\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}m_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}
instead of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}m_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}
. By the assumption (A3), this is indeed the case.

Now we define the scattering diagram

\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}}\coloneqq\{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}\mid\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}}\}.

The walls in this rank-2 scattering diagram are either lines or rays. By the assumption (A4), rays are in distinct directions (but may overlap with lines).

We then take the consistent completion
\Scat(\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}})
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}_{\mathfrak{j}}]/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{j}}^{k+2}
. This is equivalent to the consistent completion of only lines in
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}}
, as studied in
Section

9.2
. Notice that
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}}
is already consistent over
\Bbbk[P_{\mathfrak{j}}]/{\mathfrak{m}}_{\mathfrak{j}}^{k+1}
because by (A1)
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}}
is consistent at
\mathfrak{j}
. Therefore, by the positivity
Theorem

9.9
we can obtain a representative of
\Scat(\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}})
by

(1)

adding new rays
\mathfrak{d}(n_{0})
with support
S_{\mathfrak{d}(n_{0})}=\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\omega(-,n_{0})
and wall-function
f_{\mathfrak{d}(n_{0})}
with only non-trivial monomial terms in
\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
with positive integer coefficients for distinct
n_{0}\in\Lambda_{\mathfrak{j}}^{\perp}\cap N^{+}
;

(2)

for any existing ray
\overline{\mathfrak{d}}
in
\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}}
, adding positive terms in
\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
to
f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}
(and calling it
f^{\prime}_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}
).

The crucial point here is that the positivity is guaranteed by
Theorem

9.9
.

Step 3: constructing
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
.

For each new ray added in (1), define a wall in
M_{\mathbb{R}}
with the support

\mathfrak{j}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\omega(-,n_{0})

and the same wall-function
f_{\mathfrak{d}(n_{0})}
. Since
\mathfrak{j}
is perpendicular, such a wall is outgoing as the support must not contain
\omega(-,n_{0})
. Denote this (finite) collection of walls by
\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{j})
.

Now we define

\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}\coloneqq(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}\cup\bigcup_{\mathfrak{j}}\left(\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{j})\cup\{(S_{\mathfrak{d}},f^{\prime}_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}})\mid\mathfrak{j}\subseteq\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}},\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}}\}\right),

where
\bigcup_{\mathfrak{j}}
is over all perpendicular joints in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
. We note that each existing outgoing
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}
appears exactly once in the union because it only contains a unique perpendicular joint after the subdivision in
Step 1
. We have finished the construction of
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
.

We next elaborate on why
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
satisfies assumptions (A2) to (A4). We then discuss the consistency assumption (A1), which will be proven in
Lemma

9.16
.

For (A2), as pointed out earlier, the positivity is preserved by
Theorem

9.9
. Any wall in
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
(except the initial ones) is either in some
\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{j})
or of the form
(S_{\mathfrak{d}},f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}^{\prime})
arising from some
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}}
such that
\mathfrak{j}\subseteq\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}
. Clearly both kinds are outgoing. Any such
\mathfrak{d}
has support of the form

\mathfrak{j}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}})

where
\mathfrak{j}
is regarded as
F_{\mathfrak{d}}
. So (A3) is satisfied by
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
.

The statement of (A4) involves joints in
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
upon a cone complex realization of
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k+1})
. Since
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k+1})
contains
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k})
, we can require that the cone complex of
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k+1})
, when restricted on
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k})
, is a refinement of the cone complex of
\operatorname{Sing}(\mathfrak{D}_{k})
. We then have the following two cases for any perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
.

Case 1.
Suppose that
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is contained in a perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
. Notice that the outgoing walls in
\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{j})
and the outgoing walls of the form
(S_{\mathfrak{d}},f^{\prime}_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}})
are all in distinct directions. Thus we only need to show if there is a wall
\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
based at another perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}_{1}
(of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
) such that
S_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}
contains
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
, the only case can happen is

\mathrm{int}(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathrm{int}(S_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}).

Otherwise assume that
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is contained in
\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}
. Notice that we have

S_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}=\mathfrak{j}_{1}+\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}}),

where
\mathfrak{j}_{1}
is the only facet not tangent to the direction
\omega(-,n_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}})
. Thus
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is contained in
\mathfrak{j}_{1}
. Then
\mathfrak{j}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{j}
(both as joints of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
) have maximal dimensional intersection, a contradiction.

Case 2.
Suppose that
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is not contained in any perpendicular joint of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
. Assume that
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is contained in a facet of a wall
\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
. Then it must be contained in the only possible facet that is not tangent to the direction. However, such a facet is always a perpendicular joint of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
, a contradiction. This argument also implies that any such perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
is not in the boundary of any wall.

Lemma 9.15
.

\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
is consistent at each perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
.

Proof.

The analysis proceeds by considering the same two cases on whether
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is contained in a perpendicular joint
\mathfrak{j}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
, as when we show (A4).

When
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is contained in some perpendicular
\mathfrak{j}
of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
, we check all walls
\mathfrak{d}
containing
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
, namely the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}}
. The idea is then to derive the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}}
from
\Scat(\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}})
. There are two kinds of
\mathfrak{d}
:

\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}\subseteq\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}}\quad\text{or}\quad\mathrm{int}(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathrm{int}(S_{\mathfrak{d}}).

The walls of the first kind are exactly those in

\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{j})\cup\{(S_{\mathfrak{d}},f^{\prime}_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}})\mid\mathfrak{j}\subseteq\partial S_{\mathfrak{d}},\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}}\}.

For the second kind, we can get rid of those whose wall-functions are purely in order
k+1
since their wall-crossing automorphisms commute with others over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
. The remaining walls of the second kind are from walls in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
. The possible difference on the wall-function is only in order
k+1
, as mentioned in the construction, namely
f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}^{\prime}
versus
f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}
. Notice that

f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}^{\prime}=f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}\cdot(1+f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}^{\prime}-f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}})\mod\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}.

The wall-crossing automorphism associated to the function
1+f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}^{\prime}-f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}
commute with others over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
. Therefore, we can replace
f^{\prime}_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}
with
f_{\overline{\mathfrak{d}}}=f_{\mathfrak{d}}
and such a wall rolls back to the version in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
. Now we see that the consistency of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}}
is equivalent to that of
\Scat(\overline{\mathfrak{D}}_{\mathfrak{j}})
.

The case where
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is not contained in any perpendicular joint of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
is simpler. We have understood that
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is not in the boundary of any wall. Such a joint is created only because of the intersection with new walls, i.e., the ones in
\mathfrak{D}(\mathfrak{j})
for some
\mathfrak{j}
. Those walls always contain
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
of the form

\mathrm{int}(\mathfrak{j}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathrm{int}(S_{\mathfrak{d}}).

Thus as argued earlier, we can get rid of those walls and are left with only existing walls in
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
containing
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
. The only possibility is that the walls

\{\mathfrak{d}\in\mathfrak{D}_{k}\mid\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}\subseteq S_{\mathfrak{d}}\}

are all in the same direction; otherwise
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
would have been in a perpendicular joint of
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
. Since
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is not in the boundary of any wall, these walls all pass through
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
. The consistency around
\mathfrak{j}^{\prime}
is then clear.
∎

We apply
Lemma

9.15
to show

Lemma 9.16
.

\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
is consistent over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
.

Proof.

The strategy is to compare
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
with
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}
and to show they are equivalent. The scattering diagram
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}
is over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
, consistent and contains finitely many outgoing walls besides the initial walls. We note that the existence of
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}
does not depend on the constructive process described here. See
Theorem

8.9
and also
[
27
, Theorem 1.21]
, which are both incarnation of
[
34
, Theorem 2.1.6]
.

Firstly, these two scattering diagrams are equivalent over
\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
, for they are consistent and share the same collection of incoming walls. It is convenient to consider their difference
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
, as a scattering diagram. In fact, one can subdivide walls in
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
and
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}
and add walls with trivial wall-functions so that they have walls labeled by the same index set
S

\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}=\{\mathfrak{d}_{i}\mid i\in S\}\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}=\{\mathfrak{c}_{i}\mid i\in S\}

such that
S_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}=S_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}
for every
i\in S
. Their difference is
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}=\{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{i}\mid i\in S\}
where

S_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{i}}\coloneqq S_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{i}}\coloneqq f_{\mathfrak{d}_{i}}\cdot f_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}^{-1}.

The wall-function
f_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{i}}
must be of the form
1+\xi
where
\xi\in\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}\setminus\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
since it is trivial modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
.

Take another representative of
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
such that there are only non-trivial walls and they intersect only at codimension two. Notice that in this representative, walls are all outgoing since both
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
and
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}
have the exact same collection of incoming walls.

Assume that there are non-trivial walls in a hyperplane
n_{0}^{\perp}
. Let
\mathfrak{j}
be a joint (for
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
) in
n_{0}^{\perp}
. If it is not tangent to
\omega(-,n_{0})
, we claim that there are walls in
n_{0}^{\perp}
on both sides of
\mathfrak{j}
with the same wall-function. In fact, by
Lemma

9.15
,
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
is consistent at every perpendicular joint modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{k+2}
. Hence
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
is also consistent at
\mathfrak{j}
(as
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}
is consistent overall). Since the wall-crossing automorphisms associated to those
f_{\mathfrak{d}^{\prime}_{i}}
all commute, the claim follows. Now take the union
U
(which is a polyhedral cone but not necessarily convex) of all walls in
n_{0}^{\perp}
. Each facet of
U
is tangent to
\omega(-,n_{0})
. This forces the whole line
\mathbb{R}\omega(-,n_{0})
to lie in the boundary of
U
, giving the presence of a non-trivial incoming wall in
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
, a contradiction. We can then conclude that
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}
is trivial and therefore
\mathfrak{D}_{k+1}
and
(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}})_{k+1}
are equivalent.
∎

Now we have obtained a sequence
(\mathfrak{D}_{k})_{k\geq 1}
of consistent scattering diagram over
\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]/\mathfrak{m}^{k+1}
with finitely many walls without any incoming walls besides the initials. For the final step, we ensure that the limit when
k\rightarrow\infty
exists as a scattering diagram over
\widehat{\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}]}
. Notice that in this order by order construction, we either add a new outgoing wall or add higher order terms to the wall-function of an existing outgoing wall. The limit
\mathfrak{D}_{\infty}
is taken to consist of
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathrm{in},\mathbf{s}}
and any outgoing wall that first appears in some
\mathfrak{D}_{k}
with the wall-function being the limit of the wall-functions on the (support of the) same wall in
\mathfrak{D}_{i}
,
i\geq k
. Evidently for each
k\geq 1
we have

\left(\mathfrak{D}_{\infty}\right)_{k}=\mathfrak{D}_{k}.

It follows that
\mathfrak{D}_{\infty}
is consistent and has every wall-function in
\widehat{\mathbb{N}[\mathscr{P}]}
. By the uniqueness in
Theorem

8.9
,
\mathfrak{D}_{\infty}
is equivalent to
\mathfrak{D}_{\mathbf{s}}
. This is the end of the proof of
Theorem

8.11
.

Appendix A
Proof of the generalized greedy element formula

In this appendix, we prove the expansion formula for generalized greedy elements in terms of compatible gradings given by
Theorem

3.18
. The majority of the proof closely follows that of
[
51
, Theorem 2.15]
, and we discuss only those portions that require modification here. Namely, we generalize some of the necessary lemmas to the case where
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are not necessarily palindromic.

A.1.
Cluster variables up to a scalar

Recall the notations in
Section

2.2
and the definition of cluster variables
x_{i}
from
Definition

2.35
. We define elements
\{\widetilde{x}_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subseteq\mathcal{F}=\operatorname{Frac}(\Bbbk[\mathscr{P}])
and will show
x_{k}/\widetilde{x}_{k}\in\mathbb{P}
.
These
scaled cluster variables
will be convenient for our proofs in
Sections

A.2
and
A.3
.

Let
\widetilde{x}_{1}=x_{1}
and
\widetilde{x}_{2}=x_{2}
.
We inductively define
\widetilde{x}_{k}\in\mathcal{F}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
by the rule:

\widetilde{x}_{k+1}\widetilde{x}_{k-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}P_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{k})&\text{if }k\equiv 1\mod 4,\\
P_{2}(\widetilde{x}_{k})&\text{if }k\equiv 2\mod 4,\\
\overline{P}_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{k})&\text{if }k\equiv 3\mod 4,\\
\overline{P}_{2}(\widetilde{x}_{k})&\text{if }k\equiv 0\mod 4.\\
\end{array}\right.

Lemma A.1
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the quotient
x_{k}/\widetilde{x}_{k}
is a Laurent monomial in
p_{1,\ell_{1}}
and
p_{2,\ell_{2}}
. More precisely, we have

x_{k}/\widetilde{x}_{k}=\begin{cases}W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}-e_{2})\quad\text{if $k\equiv 0\mod 4$},\\
W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}+e_{1})\quad\text{if $k\equiv 1\mod 4$},\\
W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}+e_{2})\quad\text{if $k\equiv 2\mod 4$},\\
W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}-e_{1})\quad\text{if $k\equiv 3\mod 4$}.\end{cases}

Proof.

It suffices to show that
x_{k}/W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}\pm e_{i})
satisfy the exchange relations of
\widetilde{x}_{k}
. We check for
k\equiv 1\mod 4
the following relation and the remaining cases are similar. Notice that by
Lemma

2.36
, we have

\displaystyle x_{k-1}x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\bar{\zeta}_{n}(x_{k})=\frac{W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}+e_{1})^{\ell_{1}}}{p_{1,\ell_{1}}}P_{1}(x_{k}/W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}+e_{1})),

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{k-1}\widetilde{x}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\frac{W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}+e_{1})^{\ell_{1}}}{p_{1,\ell_{1}}W({\mathbf{d}}_{k-1}-e_{2})W({\mathbf{d}}_{k+1}+e_{2})}P_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{k}).

Now the desired relation follows from
W({\mathbf{d}}_{k}+e_{1})^{\ell_{1}}=p_{1,\ell_{1}}W({\mathbf{d}}_{k-1}-e_{2})W({\mathbf{d}}_{k+1}+e_{2})
, which is result of

{\mathbf{d}}_{k-1}+{\mathbf{d}}_{k+1}=\ell_{1}{\mathbf{d}}_{k}\,.\qed

A.2.
The strong Laurent phenomenon

We begin by establishing the strong Laurent phenomenon for the rank-
2
generalized cluster algebras
\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
, which is the
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{F}
generated by all scaled cluster variables
\widetilde{x}_{k}
. By
Lemma

A.1
, this notion coincides with the
\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
defined in
Section

2.4
.

Theorem A.2

(following
[
51
, Theorem 2.2]
)
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k-1},x_{k},x_{k+1},x_{k+2}]\,.

Proof.

By
Lemma

A.1
, it is enough to work with cluster pre-variables instead of cluster variables. We show via induction on
m
that
\widetilde{x}_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k-1},x_{k},x_{k+1},x_{k+2}]
, hence
\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k-1},x_{k},x_{k+1},x_{k+2}]
.
Consider the element
\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{\ell_{1}}\widetilde{x}_{m+4}
, where without loss of generality we assume
m\equiv 2\mod 4
.
We have

\displaystyle\widetilde{x}_{m}-\frac{\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{\ell_{1}}\widetilde{x}_{m+4}}{p_{1,\ell_{1}}}

\displaystyle=p_{1,\ell_{1}}\widetilde{x}_{m}-\frac{\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{\ell_{1}}P_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{m+3})}{p_{1,\ell_{1}}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{x}_{m}-\frac{\overline{P_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1})}{\widetilde{x}_{m+2}}+\frac{p_{1,\ell_{1}}\overline{P_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1})-\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{\ell_{1}}P_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{m+3})}{p_{1,\ell_{1}}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{p_{1,\ell_{1}}\overline{P_{1}}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1})-\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{\ell_{1}}P_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{m+3})}{p_{1,\ell_{1}}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{t=0}^{\ell_{1}}p_{1,t}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{t}\widetilde{x}_{m+3}^{t}-1)\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{\ell_{1}-t}}{p_{1,\ell_{1}}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}}\,.

Using that
\overline{P_{2}}
has constant term
1
, we have
\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{t}\widetilde{x}_{m+3}^{t}-1=(\overline{P_{2}}(\widetilde{x}_{m+2}))^{t}-1\in\widetilde{x}_{m+2}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m+2}]
for every
t\geq 0
. Thus,

\frac{\sum_{t=0}^{\ell_{1}}p_{1,t}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{t}\widetilde{x}_{m+3}^{t}-1)\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{\ell_{1}-t}}{\widetilde{x}_{m+2}}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m+1},x_{m+2}]\,.

We then can see that
\widetilde{x}_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m+1},x_{m+2},x_{m+3},x_{m+4}]
, and using a similar argument we obtain
\widetilde{x}_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-4},x_{m-3},x_{m-2},x_{m-1}]
. We can then conclude that

\widetilde{x}_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k-1},x_{k},x_{k+1},x_{k+2}]\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{k}

for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since we have by definition that
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k-1},x_{k},x_{k+1},x_{k+2}]\subseteq\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
, the desired equality follows.
∎

We now show that the cluster algebra
\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
equals the corresponding upper cluster algebra
\mathcal{U}
, as defined in
Section

2.4
, and moreover that it is the intersection of the Laurent polynomial ring of three consecutive clusters.

Theorem A.3

(Strong Laurent Phenomenon, following
[
51
, Theorem 2.5]
)
.

For any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})=\bigcap_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{T}_{k}=\bigcap_{k=m-1}^{m+1}\mathcal{T}_{k}\,.

Proof.

By
Theorem

A.2
, it is enough to show that

\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k-1},x_{k},x_{k+1},x_{k+2}]=\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}\,.

The containment of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k-1},x_{k},x_{k+1},x_{k+2}]
into
\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}
follows from
Proposition

2.38
. We show the other containment by taking the decomposition

\mathcal{T}_{m}=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]+\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m},x_{m+1}^{\pm 1}]+\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][\widetilde{x}_{m}^{\pm 1},\widetilde{x}_{m+1}]

and proving containment of the intersection of each summand with
\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}
.

We begin by establishing the following:

(A.4)

\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]&\text{ if }\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\geq 2\\
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1}x_{m+2}]&\text{ if }\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=1\\
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1},x_{m-1}^{\ell_{2}}x_{m+2}]&\text{ if }\ell_{1}=0\\
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1}x_{m+2}^{\ell_{1}},x_{m+2}]&\text{ if $\ell_{2}=0$}\end{cases}

Without loss of generality, we assume
m\equiv 1\mod 4
. In order to simplify the exchange relations, we often work with the scaled cluster pre-variables rather than cluster variables due to
Lemma

A.1
.

We first show that the ring on the right of (
A.4
) is contained in the intersection on the left of
(
A.4
)
. By
Proposition

2.38
, each ring on the right is contained in
\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}
, so it remains to show containment in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]
. This containment is clear for
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\geq 2
. When
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=1
, we have

\widetilde{x}_{m-1}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}=P_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{m})\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-1}P_{2}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1})\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]\,.

When
\ell_{1}=0
or
\ell_{2}=0
, where without loss of generality we assume the former, we have
\widetilde{x}_{m-1}=\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-1}
and

\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{\ell_{2}}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}=\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-\ell_{2}}P_{2}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1})\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1}=p_{2,\ell_{2}}\overline{P_{2}}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-1})\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]\,.

In each of these cases, we can now readily see that the generators of the ring on the right of (
A.4
) are contained in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]
.

We now handle the other containment of (
A.4
) case-by-case. Consider
y\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]
, and express
y
as

y=\sum_{s,t\geq 0}c_{s,t}\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-s}\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-t}\,,

with finitely many nonzero coefficients
c_{s,t}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
. We then have

y=\sum_{t\geq 0}\frac{\sum_{s\geq 0}c_{s,t}\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-s}}{P_{1}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m})}\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{t}=\sum_{t\geq 0}\frac{\sum_{s\geq 0}c_{s,t}\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-s}}{\overline{P_{1}}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1})}p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{t}\widetilde{x}_{m}^{\ell_{1}t}\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{t}\,.

If
y\in\mathcal{T}_{m-1}
, then for all
t\geq 0
we have
\frac{\sum_{s\geq 0}c_{s,t}\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-s}}{\overline{P_{1}}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1})}p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1}]
, where we denote this polynomial by
C_{t}(x_{m}^{-1})
. So we can now express
y
as

(A.5)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0}C_{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1})\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-\ell_{1}t}\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0}C_{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1})P_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{m})^{t}\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-\ell_{1}t}\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 0}C_{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1})p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{t}\overline{P_{1}}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1})\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-t}\,.

Similarly, if
y\in\mathcal{T}_{m+1}
, we have

(A.6)

y=\sum_{t\geq 0}C^{\prime}_{s}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-1})\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-\ell_{2}s}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}^{s}=\sum_{t\geq 0}C^{\prime}_{s}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-1})p_{2,\ell_{2}}^{s}\overline{P_{2}}^{s}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-1})\widetilde{x}_{m}^{s}

for some polynomials
C_{s}^{\prime}(\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{-1})\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m+1}^{-1}]
.

From this point, the proof follows essentially verbatim from Rupel’s work
[
51
, Theorem 2.5]
, but we include the remainder of the argument here for completeness.

If
d_{2}=0
, then
\widetilde{x}_{m}^{-1}=\widetilde{x}_{m+2}
and (
A.5
) becomes

y=\sum_{t\geq 0}C_{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m+2})\widetilde{x}_{m+2}^{\ell_{1}t}\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1}x_{m+2}^{\ell_{1}},x_{m+2}]\,.

Similarly, if
d_{1}=0
then

y=\sum_{s\geq 0}C_{s}^{\prime}(\widetilde{x}_{m-1})\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{\ell_{2}s}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}^{s}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1},x_{m-1}^{\ell_{2}}x_{m+2}]\,.

We now consider when
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\neq 0
. Let
\sigma(t)
be maximal such that
c_{\sigma(t),t}\neq 0
, and using the last equality in (
A.5
) we must have
\sigma(t)\geq\ell_{1}t
. Similarly, let
\tau(s)
maximal such that
c_{s,\tau(s)}\neq 0
, and we have
\tau(s)\geq\ell_{2}s
.

Suppose
t
is maximal such that
c_{s,t}\neq 0
for some choice of
s
. Then
c_{\sigma(t),\tau(\sigma(t)))}\neq 0
, and
\tau(\sigma(t))\geq\ell_{2}\sigma(t)\geq\ell_{1}\ell_{2}t
. For
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\geq 2
and
t>0
this implies
\tau(\sigma(t))>t
, which contradicts the maximality of
t
. Thus we must have
t=0
and, similarly,
s=0
when
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\geq 2
. Hence
y\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}]
.

Now suppose
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}=1
. Then we have
\tau(\sigma(t))\geq\sigma(t)\geq t
, and by the maximality of
t
these are equalities. Using induction on the maximal value
t
such that
c_{t,t}\neq 0
, we can see that
y\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1}x_{m+2}]
. The base case
t=0
is clear. When
t>0
, the element
y-c_{t,t}\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{t}\widetilde{x}_{m+2}^{t}
is contained in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1}x_{m+2}]
, hence
y
is also contained in
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1}x_{m+2}]
. Thus (
A.4
) is proved.

It then remains to show the equality

\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m},x_{m+1}^{\pm 1}]=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1},x_{m},x_{m+1}]\subset\mathcal{T}_{m+1}\,.

The inclusion of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1},x_{m},x_{m+1}]
into
\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m},x_{m+1}^{\pm 1}]
is clear. For the other inclusion, note that any
y\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m},x_{m+1}^{\pm 1}]
can be written in the form

y=\sum_{t=-N}^{N}c_{t}x_{m+2}^{t}

for some positive integer
N
and coefficients
c_{t}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}]
. We then have

y=\sum_{t=0}^{N}c_{t}P_{1}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m})\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{-t}+\sum_{t=1}^{N}\frac{c_{-t}}{P_{1}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m})}\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{t}\,.

If
y\in\mathcal{T}_{m-1}
, then we must have
\frac{c_{-t}}{P_{1}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m})}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{\pm 1}]
for all
1\leq t\leq N
. Since
P_{1}(\widetilde{x}_{m})
has constant term
1
, this implies
\frac{c_{-t}}{P_{1}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m})}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}]
. Thus we have

y=\sum_{t=0}^{N}c_{t}\widetilde{x}_{m+1}^{t}+\sum_{t=1}^{N}\frac{c_{-t}}{P_{1}^{t}(\widetilde{x}_{m})}\widetilde{x}_{m-1}^{t}\in\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1},x_{m},x_{m+1}]\,.

We can similarly obtain
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{\pm 1},x_{m+1}]\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}=\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m},x_{m+1},x_{m+2}]\subset\mathcal{T}_{m-1}
.
Combining these inclusions yields

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m}\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\left(\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]+\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m},x_{m+1}^{\pm 1}]+\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{\pm 1},x_{m+1}]\right)\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{m-1}\cap\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m}^{-1},x_{m+1}^{-1}]\cap\mathcal{T}_{m+1}+\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m-1},x_{m},x_{m+1}]

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{m},x_{m+1},x_{m+2}]

\displaystyle\subset\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{P}][x_{k-1},x_{k},x_{k+1},x_{k+2}]\,,

as desired.
∎

A.3.
Proof of the compatible grading formula

This section closely follows the approach of Rupel in
[
51
]
, with necessary adjustments made to extend the argument to the case when
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are not palindromic. In the compatible grading formula given by
Theorem

3.18
, the horizontal edges of the Dyck path are weighted by the coefficients of
P_{2}
and the vertical edges are weighted by the coefficients of
P_{1}
. We introduce auxiliary variants of this weighting, replacing
P_{i}
with
\overline{P_{i}}
.

Definition A.7
.

For a grading
\omega:E(d_{1},d_{2})\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we define

\displaystyle\text{wt}^{\prime}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d_{1}}p_{2,\ell_{2}-\omega(u_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{d_{2}}p_{1,\omega(v_{j})}\in\Bbbk[\mathbb{Z}^{\ell_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\ell_{2}}]\,,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\text{wt}^{\prime\prime}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d_{1}}p_{2,\omega(u_{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{d_{2}}p_{1,\ell_{1}-\omega(v_{j})}\in\Bbbk[\mathbb{Z}^{\ell_{1}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{\ell_{2}}]\,.

We need to introduce the following auxiliary variants of the generalized greedy elements.

Definition A.8
.

We define
x^{\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}]\coloneqq x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{(S_{1},S_{2})}\wt^{\prime}(\omega)x_{1}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}
, and similarly let
x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}]\coloneqq x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{(S_{1},S_{2})}\wt^{\prime\prime}(\omega)x_{1}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}
.

We now consider two automorphisms of the field of rational functions
\mathcal{F}
. These maps are defined by their behavior on a generating set and given by
\sigma_{1}:(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto(x_{1},x_{0})
and
\sigma_{2}:(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto(x_{3},x_{2})
. In the case that
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are palindromic, these are the usual reflection automorphisms
\sigma_{j}
, where
\sigma_{j}(x_{m})=x_{2j-m}
. We show that applying these automorphisms to a varient of some generalized greedy element results in a “mutated” generalized greedy element.

Proposition A.9

(following
[
51
, Proposition 2.17]
)
.

For any
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
the elements
x[d_{1},d_{2}]
defined by (
3.19
) satisfy

\sigma_{1}(x^{\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}])=x[d_{1},\ell_{1}[d_{1}]_{+}-d_{2}]\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sigma_{2}(x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}])=x[\ell_{2}[d_{2}]_{+}-d_{1},d_{2}]\,.

Proof.

By symmetry, it is enough to establish the second identity. We do so by handling several cases.

First, suppose
d_{1},d_{2}\leq 0
. Then
