\mathcal{P}([d_{1}]_{+},[d_{2}]_{+})=\mathcal{P}(0,0)
is a single point, so we have

\sigma_{2}(x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}])=\sigma_{2}(x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}})=x_{3}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}=x_{1}^{d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}P_{2}(x_{2})^{-d_{1}}\,.

We can also directly compute
x[\ell_{2}[d_{2}]_{+}-d_{1},d_{2}]=x[-d_{1},d_{2}]
using
Theorem

3.18
. Since
\mathcal{P}_{[-d_{1}]_{+},[d_{2}]_{+}}=\mathcal{P}_{-d_{1},0}
, we have
x[-d_{1},d_{2}]=x_{1}^{d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}P_{2}(x_{2})^{-d_{1}}
, as desired.

Next, suppose
d_{1}\leq 0<d_{2}
. Then
\mathcal{P}([d_{1}]_{+},[d_{2}]_{+})
consists of
d_{2}
vertical edges. Similar to the previous case, we have
x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}]=x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{d_{2}}\overline{P_{1}}(x_{1})^{d_{2}}
. Applying
\sigma_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}])

\displaystyle=x_{3}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{d_{2}}\overline{P_{1}}(x_{3})^{d_{2}}

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{d_{2}}P_{2}(x_{2})^{-d_{1}}\overline{P_{1}}(P_{2}(x_{2})x_{1}^{-1})^{d_{2}}

Note that every vertical edge in
\mathcal{D}_{\ell_{2}a_{2}-d_{1},d_{2}}
has at least
\ell_{2}
vertical edges in front of it. Thus any compatible grading with vertical weights bounded by
\ell_{2}
can be constructed by taking a set of vertical edges and any horizontal edges not contained in the shadow of the vertical edges. Focusing just a vertical edge and the edges in its shadow, which is identical to
\mathcal{D}_{\ell_{2},1}
, we can see that

\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{C}_{\ell_{2},1}}\wt(\omega)x_{1}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}=p_{1,\ell_{1}}\overline{P_{1}}(P_{2}(x_{2})x_{1}^{-1})x_{1}^{\ell_{2}}\,.

We can view compatible gradings on
\mathcal{D}_{\ell_{2}d_{2}-d_{1},d_{2}}
as a collection of
d_{2}
compatible gradings on
\mathcal{D}_{\ell_{2},1}
along with compatible gradings on
-d_{1}
additional horizontal edges. So, using the compatible grading definition, we have

\displaystyle x[\ell_{2}d_{2}-d_{1},d_{2}]

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{-\ell_{2}d_{2}+d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\left(p_{1,\ell_{1}}\overline{P_{1}}(P_{2}(x_{2})x_{1}^{-1})\right)^{d_{2}}P_{2}(x_{2})^{-d_{1}}

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{d_{2}}P_{2}(x_{2})^{-d_{1}}\overline{P_{1}}(P_{2}(x_{2})x_{1}^{-1})^{d_{2}}\,.

Lastly, suppose
0<d_{1},d_{2}
. Then we have
\displaystyle x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}]=x_{1}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{C}_{d_{1},d_{2}}}\wt^{\prime\prime}(\omega)x_{1}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}
. Hence

\displaystyle\sigma_{2}(x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}])

\displaystyle=x_{3}^{-d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{C}_{d_{1},d_{2}}}\wt^{\prime\prime}(\omega)x_{3}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{-\ell_{2}d_{2}+d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{C}_{d_{1},d_{2}}}\wt^{\prime\prime}(\omega)x_{1}^{\ell_{2}d_{2}-\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}P_{2}(x_{2})^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})-d_{1}}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}\,.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\sh}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
denote the set of compatible gradings on
\mathcal{P}(d_{1},d_{2})
such that
S_{\mathbf{E}}\subseteq\sh(S_{\mathbf{N}})
, where
S
is the set of edges with nonzero value in the grading. Given a grading
\omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\sh}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
, define the grading
\theta(\omega)\coloneqq(\Omega(S_{\mathbf{E}}),\varphi_{\ell_{2}}^{*}S_{\mathbf{N}})
on
\mathcal{D}_{\ell_{2}d_{2}-d_{1},d_{2}}
as in
[
51
, Section 4.2]
. By definition, we have
\wt(\theta(\omega))=\wt^{\prime\prime}(\omega)
. Then by
[
51
, Lemma 4.4 and Proposition 4.20]
, we have

\displaystyle x[\ell_{2}d_{2}-d_{1},d_{2}]

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{-\ell_{2}d_{2}+d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{\omega^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{[\ell_{2}d_{2}-d_{1}]_{+},d_{2}}}\wt(\omega^{\prime})x_{1}^{\omega^{\prime}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega^{\prime}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{-\ell_{2}d_{2}+d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{C}^{\sh}_{d_{1},d_{2}}}\wt(\theta(\omega))x_{1}^{\ell_{2}d_{2}-\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}

\displaystyle=x_{1}^{-\ell_{2}d_{2}+d_{1}}x_{2}^{-d_{2}}\sum_{\omega\in\mathcal{C}_{d_{1},d_{2}}}P_{2}(x_{2})^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})-d_{1}}\wt^{\prime\prime}(\omega)x_{1}^{\ell_{2}d_{2}-\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{N}})}x_{2}^{\omega(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{E}})}.

Thus in all cases, we can conclude
\sigma_{2}(x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}])=x[\ell_{2}d_{2}-d_{1},d_{2}]
.
∎

Corollary A.10

(following
[
51
, Corollary 2.18]
)
.

For any
(d_{1},d_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
the generalized greedy element
x[d_{1},d_{2}]\in\mathcal{T}_{1}
defined by (
3.19
) is contained in
\mathcal{A}(P_{1},P_{2})
.

Proof.

First note that
x^{\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}]
and
x^{\prime\prime}[d_{1},d_{2}]
are contained in
\mathcal{T}_{1}
for any
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. By definition, for any
z\in\mathcal{T}_{1}
, we have
\sigma_{1}(z)\in\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
\sigma_{2}(z)\in\mathcal{T}_{2}
. The result then follows directly from
Theorem

A.3
and
Proposition

A.9
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.18
.

This can be proved in the same way as
[
51
, Theorem 2.15]
, except with the above results replacing their more specialized analogues in
[
51
]
. In particular, we replace references to
[
51
, Proposition 2.17]
with
Proposition

A.9
and
[
51
, Corollary 2.18]
with
Corollary

A.10
, and the remainder of the argument proceeds unchanged.
∎

Appendix B
Every wall coefficient in the Badlands is non-trivial

As a direct application of our results, we can prove that for any rank-
2
generalized cluster scattering diagram, every wall-function coefficient of every wall in the Badlands is strictly positive. The Badlands of the scattering diagram
\mathfrak{D}\coloneqq\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
is the same region as the Badlands of
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
.

The density of the Badlands in
\mathfrak{D}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}
was suggested by various sources, including
[
27
, Example 1.15]
,
[
29
, Example 1.6]
, and
[
30
, Example 1.4]
, before recently being proved by Gräfnitz and Luo
[
23
]
. In fact, Gräfnitz and Luo prove the stronger result that every wall-function coefficient of walls in the Badlands is strictly positive (except those that trivially vanish by the choice of
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
). In the skew-symmetric case, density was proven in 2010 by Gross–Pandharipande
[
28
]
and strict positivity of every wall-function coefficient was proven in 2021 by Davison–Mandel
[
13
]
. All the previous approaches use either representation theory or deformations of scattering diagrams. Using our tight grading formula, we give a lower bound on every wall-function coefficient in the Badlands of generalized scattering diagrams, which in particular establishes strict positivity. As a consequence, we recover the results of
[
28
,
13
,
23
]
using elementary combinatorics.

Theorem B.1
.

Suppose the coefficients of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are contained in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Fix
a,b
coprime such that the wall of slope
\frac{b}{a}
is in the Badlands of
\mathfrak{D}\coloneqq\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
, and fix
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
such that
\ell_{1}
divides
ka
and
\ell_{2}
divides
kb
. Then the wall-function coefficient
\lambda(ka,kb)
in
\mathfrak{D}
is at least
k\cdot p_{1,\ell_{1}}^{ka/\ell_{1}}p_{2,\ell_{2}}^{kb/\ell_{2}}
.

Proof.

We proceed by using the tight grading formula
Theorem

5.17
(with
m=1
) and constructing
k
distinct tight gradings contributing to the coefficient
\lambda(ka,kb)
. Fix
c_{1},c_{2}
such that
ka=c_{1}\ell_{1}
and
kb=c_{2}\ell_{2}
, and choose
(d_{1},d_{2})
according to
Theorem

5.17
. Fix
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, and define the origin to be the minimal
(x,y)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that
d_{2}x-d_{1}y=i
. We then work on the
(x+y)
-th cyclic shift of
\mathcal{P}(d_{1},d_{2})
, which cyclically shifts the leftmost
x+y
edges of
\mathcal{P}(d_{1},d_{2})
to the right end. We then label the horizontal (resp. vertical) edges by
u_{j}
(resp.
v_{j}
) from left-to-right (resp. bottom-to-top). Let
\omega
be the grading that assigns value
c
to horizontal edges
u_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq c_{2}
, value
b
to vertical edges
v_{j}
for
c_{2}\ell_{2}-c_{1}+1\leq j\leq c_{2}\ell_{2}
, and value
0
to all other edges.

Consider the top left point of the vertical blocks in each column (call the set of these points
R
), and the top left point of the horizontal blocks in each row (call the set
B
). If the Dyck path starts at the origin
(0,0)
, then the points in
R
lie to the left of the line

y=\left(-\ell_{2}+\frac{d_{2}}{d_{1}}\right)x+c_{2}\ell_{2}-\frac{k-1}{d_{1}}\,.

Similarly, the points in
B
lie to the right of the line

y=\left(\frac{1}{\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}-\ell_{1}}\right)x+\frac{\ell_{1}(c_{1}-c_{2}\ell_{2})-\frac{k-1}{n}}{\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}-\ell_{1}}\,.

In order to show that points in
R
are to the left of the point in
B
of the same height, it is sufficient to show that the intersection point of these lines is to the left of the
y
-axis.
Let
\alpha=\frac{d_{1}}{d_{2}}
. These lines intersect when

x=\frac{\alpha(\alpha c_{2}\ell_{2}-c_{1}\ell_{1})+\frac{\ell_{1}k}{d_{2}}}{\ell_{2}\alpha^{2}-\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\alpha+\ell_{1}}\,.

The numerator is always positive by our choice of
\alpha
. Thus, it is sufficient to have
\ell_{2}\alpha^{2}-\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\alpha+\ell_{1}<0
. But the roots of this quadratic are precisely

\frac{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\pm\sqrt{\ell_{1}^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-4\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}}{2\ell_{2}}\,,

The greater of these two roots is the slope of the right boundary of the Badlands.

Repeating the same process swapping
b
and
c
handles all walls on the interior of the Badlands. For
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
such that
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}\neq 4
, the boundary of the Badlands will have irrational slope. Thus the result is proved for the entirety of the Badlands in all cases except the affine cases. In the affine cases, where
\ell_{1}\ell_{2}=4
, constructions of tight gradings corresponding to each wall-function coefficient are given in
Example

5.7
and
Example

5.8
.
∎

By specializing
Theorem

B.1
, we recover the results of
[
23
, Theorem 1]
,
[
28
, Section 4.7]
, and
[
13
, Example 4.10]
.

In the case when
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
have positive coefficients (in an ordered field), we can then guarantee that every wall appears within the Badlands.

Corollary B.2
.

Let
\Bbbk
be an ordered field, and suppose the coefficients of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are nonnegative elements of
\Bbbk
. Then for
\mathfrak{D}\coloneqq\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
, there are walls of every rational slope within the Badlands.

Proof.

As is shown in
Theorem

B.1
, there are tight gradings of non-zero weight corresponding to each wall-function coefficient in the Badlands. By our assumption on the coefficients of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
, the weights of these tight gradings are in fact positive. Thus, by
Theorem

3.18
, the wall-function coefficient are sums of positive elements and hence are positive.
∎

An alternative proof of
Corollary

B.2
can be obtained using the results of Gräfnitz and Luo for cluster scattering diagrams and applying our positivity result
Theorem

8.11
.

Remark B.3
.

If we allow the coefficients of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
to be negative, the results of
Theorem

B.1
. While the tight gradings constructed in
Theorem

B.1
still contribute to the wall-function coefficients, the weights of the tight gradings may cancel out. An explicit example of this phenomenon is given below. We do not know if
Corollary

B.2
can also fail in this setting.

Example B.4
.

Using our combinatorial formula for wall-function coefficients, we can show that certain coefficients vanish. For example, suppose
P_{1}(x)=1+p_{1,3}x^{3}
and
P_{2}(y)=1+p_{2,1}y+p_{2,2}y^{2}
, where we assume
p_{1,3}
,
p_{2,1}
, and
p_{2,2}
are nonzero. In order to calculate the degree-
3
coefficient of
f_{\mathbb{R}_{\leq 0}(1,1)}
in
\Scat(P_{1},P_{2})
, which we denote by
\lambda(3,3)
, we can take a weighted sum over tight gradings using
Theorem

5.17
. In particular, it is the weighted sum of the seven tight gradings on
\mathcal{P}(4,3)
below.

Figure 8.
For
P_{1}(x)
and
P_{2}(y)
chosen as in
Example

B.4
, these are the seven tight gradings on
\mathcal{P}(4,3)
with both total vertical and horizontal grade equal to
3
that have nonzero weight.

The leftmost six gradings have weight
p_{1,3}p_{2,1}p_{2,2}
and the rightmost grading has weight
p_{1,3}p_{2,1}^{3}
. Thus we have
\lambda(3,3)=6p_{1,3}p_{2,1}p_{2,2}+p_{1,3}p_{2,1}^{3}
, so we can conclude that this wall-function coefficient vanishes whenever
p_{2,2}=-\frac{p_{2,1}^{2}}{6}
.
